# Fidelity Report

**Date: 2026-08-05, audited 2026-08-30 (see the first log entry below)**

Honest assessment of the renderer on five pages. Phase 0 shipped a static
HTML→CSS→block/inline→paint→PNG pipeline that **linearised every page to one
column**. It has since grown a full box model (floats, flexbox, CSS grid, tables,
`position`), broad CSS (`var()`, `@media`, dark-mode, gradients, box-shadow,
border-radius, opacity, `!important`), SVG rasterisation, and **JavaScript
execution** (goja + a real DOM + a settle-then-render loop). The renderer is
**no longer static**: script-driven DOM mutations are reflected in the output;
render with `DisableJS` to get the static, no-JavaScript document. Where a page
still diverges from a browser it is stated below, not hidden. The phase-by-phase
log runs newest-first; see "Known gaps" for the current, audited scope boundary
rather than trusting any single phase entry in isolation.

The committed PNGs under `testdata/renders/` back every claim here. Reproduce
them with the commands at the bottom. The measured-vs-Chrome numbers live in
[`bench/REPORT.md`](bench/REPORT.md).

## 2026-11-08 (round 31) — hex literals survive their own trailing hex digits; `forceFallbackAdapter` resolves null (two fixes, go-wasmcontainers report, round 30 follow-up)

Reported after v0.45.8: go-wasmcontainers' own local run of 3 tests was clean, but their real CI lane (GitHub's macOS runner, the wider `TestBrowserCapabilities|TestBatch|TestWebGPUBackend` set) found two more failures, each diagnosed with the exact cause before being reported.

- **`litMSL` stripped a numeric literal's own type suffix with `strings.TrimRight(text, "iuhfIUHF")`** — a whole CUTSET, repeatedly removed from the end, not one character. A hex literal's own digits run through a-f/A-F, overlapping that cutset: `0xFFFFFFFFu` (go-gfx/webgpu's own loop-budget rewrite injects exactly this into every rewritten module) lost its real "u" suffix correctly, then kept eating every trailing "F" hex digit too, down to bare `0x` — which `strconv` then refused outright, rather than silently misreading the value. Fixed with `stripOneSuffix`, removing exactly one trailing occurrence of a GIVEN suffix string, called only with the ONE suffix letter each numeric kind could ever have (i/u for an integer, f/h for a float) — an integer branch never strips `f`/`F`, since a hex integer literal has no float suffix at all (WGSL §15.6.1: only a hex FLOAT literal's own mandatory `p` exponent marker could make a trailing `f` ambiguous, and that marker is never present on an integer literal), so a trailing `f`/`F` there is always a hex digit, never a suffix.
- **`requestAdapter({forceFallbackAdapter: true})` ignored its own argument** entirely, handing back the real hardware adapter regardless. WebGPU §4.4.1 requires it to resolve with a software adapter "if one exists, else null" — never hardware as a substitute. This binding has no software adapter at all, so the fix is unconditional: `forceFallbackAdapter: true` now always resolves `null`.
- Tests: `TestHexLiteralsSurviveTheirOwnTrailingHexDigits` (a suffixed, an unsuffixed, and a signed hex literal in one kernel — real GPU hardware) and `TestRequestAdapterForceFallbackResolvesNull`. The hex-literal fix verified decisive against TWO distinct wrong fixes, not just the original bug: restoring the exact original `TrimRight` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' own reported error message verbatim; a NAIVE single-character fix that still doesn't know a hex digit can end in a real suffix-alphabet letter also fails the test (strips `0xFFFFFFFFu`'s own trailing real `F` digit down to `0xFFFFFFF`) — the per-kind, exact-suffix-string design is what survives both.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`gofmt -l`/`govulncheck ./...` clean; `go test ./...` green across the whole repo; `go test -race -count=5` clean on both affected packages; real GPU hardware, not a stub.

## 2026-11-08 (round 30) — a shader-creation error (a reserved word, a syntax error) now surfaces at createShaderModule, not later (fix, go-wasmcontainers report, round 29 follow-up)

Reported after v0.45.7 cleared batch/batch-grant: batch-shaders, their last red container case, feeds a module using the reserved word `meta` as an identifier — genuinely invalid WGSL by spec, which every real engine refuses at `createShaderModule` itself (captured by an open error scope, reported by `getCompilationInfo()`), not only once a pipeline first tries to use it.

- **Two genuinely different questions, now kept separate**: "is this real WGSL at all" (go-gfx/wgsl's own `Validate` — the same CTS-conformant Parse+Check frontend `wgslmsl.Translate` itself consumes, called independently here) versus "can THIS translator's own deliberately bounded backend run it" (`wgslmsl.Translate`, unchanged). A genuine WGSL error (reserved word, syntax error, type error) is now delivered to the device's error scope stack AT `createShaderModule`, via the same scope-walking mechanism `gpuGuard` already uses elsewhere for every other GPU-content validation failure (factored out as `gpuDeliverError`, since `createShaderModule` itself must still return a real, usable module object either way — spec: shader module creation never throws or rejects — so it can't just panic through `gpuGuard` the way most other calls do). A construct this translator doesn't support yet but IS genuinely valid WGSL (a switch statement, a pointer parameter) stays exactly as before: not reported as a shader-creation error (a real browser would compile it fine), surfaced only via `getCompilationInfo()`'s own best-effort, unpositioned message or a later `createComputePipeline` throw.
- `getCompilationInfo()` now reports the REAL line/column/offset go-gfx/wgsl's own `Validate` found for a genuine WGSL error, not the placeholder zeros every message got before.
- Tests: `TestCreateShaderModuleGenuinelyInvalidWGSLCapturedAtCreationTime` (the reserved-word case, checking both the error-scope capture and `getCompilationInfo`'s own real position) and its decisive contrast, `TestCreateShaderModuleUnsupportedButValidWGSLNotCapturedAtCreationTime` (a switch statement, which must NOT be captured). Both independently verified: the early-capture fix sabotaged alone broke only the first; a SEPARATE sabotage (wrongly using `wgslmsl.Translate`'s own wrapped error instead of `wgsl.Validate`'s raw one) broke BOTH, for complementary reasons — the first lost its positioned message entirely (a `%w`-wrapped error doesn't type-switch as `*wgsl.Error`), the second wrongly fired a validation error for genuinely valid WGSL.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`gofmt -l`/`govulncheck ./...` clean; `go test ./...` green across the whole repo; `go test -race -count=5` clean; real GPU hardware, not a stub.

## 2026-11-08 (round 29) — `arrayLength(&resource)` (fix, go-wasmcontainers report, round 28 follow-up)

Reported after v0.45.6 cleared the entire ONNX lane (123/123, mnist-12 matching Chromium/Safari on Apple Metal to 3.1e-05): their batch/batch-grant kernel (`go-wasmcontainers/gpu` `examples/batchcompute/main.go:50-54`) still failed with `wgslmsl: unsupported: unary operator "&" (pointers are out of scope)`. Asked for the exact source before designing anything (own earlier guess — a local `atomicStore(&flag,...)` or a `ptr<function, T>` helper parameter — was wrong on both counts): the kernel's ONLY `&` is `if (id.x < arrayLength(&out)) { ... }` — address-of a module-scope runtime-sized storage array, passed to the `arrayLength` built-in. No atomics, no pointer parameters, confirmed directly by the peer reading their own source.

- **The fix, scoped to exactly this one shape** (general `&`/`ptr<function, T>` stays out of scope — `unaryExpr`'s own default case still rejects a bare `&`, matching `atomicCallExpr`'s identical precedent for `&resource` inside an atomic built-in): `callExpr` recognizes `arrayLength(&X)` where `X` resolves to a tracked `BindingStorageArray` resource, and emits the resource's own already-computed bounds-check sizes-buffer entry (`_sizes.sN`) directly — the SAME per-binding element count `indexExpr`'s own clamp already reads, correctly the bound SUB-RANGE's count (engine#391's own fix), not the whole buffer's, with no new mechanism needed.
- Test: `TestArrayLengthReturnsRealElementCount`, go-wasmcontainers' own exact kernel, dispatched at a count NOT a multiple of its own `@workgroup_size(64)` so go-macos/metal's real "overhang" threads (id.x past the real count, inherent to how it picks a threadgroup shape) exercise the guard too — every element starts at a sentinel bit pattern, so any wrong (too-small) arrayLength leaves it there instead of the computed formula. Verified decisive in that direction (sabotaged to return 0, confirmed every output stayed at the sentinel). A too-LARGE sabotage (999999) did NOT reliably fail: the translator's own separate, pre-existing index clamp absorbs it by funnelling every out-of-range write onto the buffer's own last element, racing with its legitimate writer — a real, acceptable defense-in-depth property (a wrong arrayLength that's too permissive still cannot corrupt memory outside the buffer), not a gap in the fix itself, but not something this one test can show cleanly either.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`gofmt -l`/`govulncheck ./...` clean; `go test ./...` green across the whole repo; `go test -race -count=5` clean; real GPU hardware, not a stub.

## 2026-11-08 (round 28) — a `continue` inside `for`/`loop` skipped the update/continuing block, hanging the kernel (fix, go-wasmcontainers report, round 27 follow-up)

Reported after v0.45.5 fixed bitcast and resource-touching helpers (57 → 100 of 123 ONNX cases): every remaining failure — every padded conv/pool case, the 3-D "large" ones, mnist-12 — failed identically with `batch: a shader exceeded its loop budget and was truncated`, while Chromium/Firefox/Safari ran the same kernels fine. go-wasmcontainers' own diagnosis, from their own kernels.go (padded cases `continue` past out-of-range positions inside nested `for` loops; unpadded variants, with no `continue`, pass): wgslmsl's `for`-loop lowering runs `update` AFTER the body, so a bare MSL `continue` — which jumps straight back to the `while(true)` wrapper — skips it outright, leaving the loop variable never advancing. Confirmed directly with a minimal repro before touching anything: the exact generated shape, `while(true){ if(!cond)break; body; update; }` with a bare `continue` inside body, loops forever the instant that branch fires.

- **The fix is NOT goto+labels** — tried first, confirmed wrong directly: Metal's own compiler refuses it outright ("error: labeled statements are not supported in Metal"), on every test that has ANY `for`/`loop` at all, not just ones with a `continue`. Metal has no labeled statements, full stop.
- **The real fix**: `forStmt`/`loopStmt` wrap their own body in `do { ... } while (false)`, with a fresh per-loop `bool _brkN` flag. A `continue` inside body is rewritten to a bare `break` — exiting only the `do/while(false)` wrapper, falling through to update/continuing exactly as a real continue should. A real `break` is rewritten to `_brkN = true; break;` — same exit from the wrapper, but the line right after it (`if (_brkN) break;`) then breaks the REAL, outer loop too; without the flag, every `break` inside body would silently become a continue instead. Scoped exactly like WGSL's own break/continue (`loopFlag`'s own doc comment): saved and restored around each loop construct, cleared inside a plain `while` (no continuing/update clause to protect, MSL's native break/continue already correct there), so a break/continue inside a NESTED loop always targets that nested loop, never an outer one.
- Tests: `TestForLoopContinueStillRunsUpdate` (continue AND break together in one `for`, real GPU, proving each keeps its own distinct meaning) and `TestLoopContinueStillRunsContinuingBlock` (the identical shape for `loop { … continuing { … } }`, go-wasmcontainers' own flagged "same issue would hit this too" — confirmed it did, same fix). Deliberately NOT sabotage-verified by re-running an actually-broken version on real hardware: the pre-fix repro, read directly rather than executed to a hang, already showed the exact infinite-loop-causing shape; re-triggering a genuinely unbounded GPU dispatch to "prove" it again is not a risk worth taking on real hardware.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`gofmt -l`/`govulncheck ./...` clean; `go test ./...` green across the whole repo; `go test -race -count=5` clean; real GPU hardware, not a stub.

## 2026-11-08 (round 27) — bitcast reinterprets bits, and a helper function may touch a resource directly (two fixes, go-wasmcontainers report, round 26 follow-up)

Reported on their v0.45.4 retest of round 26's helper-function support (26 → 57 of 123 ONNX cases): one silent-correctness bug, worse than a refusal, and one real remaining gap.

- **`bitcast<T>(e)` was translated as a numeric CONVERSION, not a bit reinterpretation** (WGSL §17.2: bitcast is defined as reinterpreting the same bits as a different type — `bitcast<f32>(1u)` is the float whose bits equal the integer 1, a tiny subnormal, never `1.0`). `callExpr`'s own generic "callee names a type" branch caught `bitcast<f32>(...)`'s own call syntax and emitted a plain C-style conversion, `float(x)`. Their own Gemm/MatMul kernels decode an f32 `alpha` stored as raw bits in a u32 uniform this way — every result came back ~1e9 too large (`0x3F800000`, `1.0f`'s own bits, read as an integer — their own oracle caught it, not visual inspection). Fixed by intercepting `bitcast` before the generic type-call branch and emitting Metal's own `as_type<T>(e)` — the same, one emission, for a scalar AND a same-arity vector bitcast alike (go-wasmcontainers' own explicit ask: `vecN<u32>`/`vecN<f32>`/`vecN<i32>` too), confirmed by a decisive test covering both shapes in one kernel, through a storage array declared `array<u32>` so a numeric conversion and a bit reinterpretation give provably different, not merely imprecise, numbers.
- **A helper function touching a resource directly** (`wm`/`ninf` in their own kernels.go, reading the module-scope storage buffer `prm` without it ever appearing in the helper's own WGSL parameter list) was round 26's own stated, deliberate scope boundary — "a real feature this package does not implement yet." Implemented: `emitHelperFuncs` now computes, for every helper (callee-before-caller, so a callee's own needs are already known), the resources it needs — its own direct touches UNION every function it calls already needs — and threads each through as an extra MSL parameter (plus `_sizes` whenever any needed resource is a bounds-checked storage array), at both the helper's own signature and every call site that reaches it, however many levels away. Verified transitive specifically: a helper that never references a resource itself, only calling one that does, must still receive and forward it — confirmed by a dedicated structural test, and by sabotaging the UNION step alone and watching it fail.
- Tests: `TestBitcastReinterpretsBitsNotNumericValue` (wgslmsl, real GPU), `TestHelperFunctionThreadsResourceTransitively` (wgslmsl, structural — asserts the exact generated MSL signatures and call sites, matching this file's own `TestUniformBudgetAndAtomicFlag` convention), `TestComputePipelineWithHelperFunctionReadingAResource` (js/webgpu_test.go, real GPU, go-wasmcontainers' own exact `wm`/`prm` shape through the full `navigator.gpu` surface with two real storage buffers). Every new guard verified decisive by sabotaging it alone and watching the matching test fail, then restoring.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`gofmt -l`/`govulncheck ./...` clean; `go test ./...` green across the whole repo; `go test -race -count=5` clean on both affected packages; real GPU hardware, not a stub.

## 2026-11-08 (round 26) — wgslmsl translates calls to WGSL helper functions, not only the entry point's own body (fix, go-wasmcontainers report, engine#391 follow-up)

Reported after retesting v0.45.3 (round 25's sub-range fix): `wgslmsl: unsupported: calls to helper functions (entry point "main" reaches 2 functions; only the entry point itself is supported)` on EVERY remaining ONNX failure they could read the cause of (97 of 123 cases) — their nn kernels (`go-wasmcontainers/gpu`'s own `nn/webgpu/kernels.go`) call small WGSL helper functions for index math and activation.

- **The fix**: `wgslmsl.Translate`'s own `len(ep.Functions) != 1` restriction is gone. Every function go-gfx/wgsl's own call-graph analysis (`EntryPoint.Functions`) says the entry point transitively calls is now emitted as a real MSL function, callee-before-caller (MSL/C++, unlike WGSL, requires a function be declared above any call to it), before the entry kernel itself.
- **Found along the way, not assumed**: go-gfx/wgsl's own `FuncObj.Calls`/`.Globals` fields — which look like exactly the call-graph/resource-use data this fix needs — are **not populated** for a function outside an entry point (confirmed directly: both come back empty even for a function actually called by this module's own entry point). go-gfx/wgsl's own newer `msl` package (released the same day, v0.5.0, a separate non-entry-point MSL emitter for go-gfx/gpu2d) hits the identical gap and documents it at its own equivalent call site, writing its own AST walker instead of trusting the field. wgslmsl now does the same: `walkFuncBody`/`walkFuncStmt`/`walkFuncExpr`, mirroring stmt.go's and expr.go's own recursion exactly, drive both the callee-before-caller ordering (`funcCallees`) and the one new scope boundary below.
- **Scoped to pure value functions, on purpose**: a helper that directly references a resource variable (`<storage>`/`<uniform>`) is rejected, clear and named — a resource is wired to the entry kernel's OWN buffer parameter list and nowhere else; threading it through a helper's own parameter list is a real feature, not implemented yet. A helper whose parameter or return type isn't a scalar or vector (an array, a pointer, a struct) is rejected the same way `mslType` already rejects one in the entry body — nothing new to write there.
- **Considered and rejected**: delegating helper-function emission to go-gfx/wgsl's own new `msl` package instead of wgslmsl's own existing `stmtBlock`/`expr`. It has real advantages (WGSL-exact signed-overflow/div-by-zero/shift semantics wgslmsl's own simpler arithmetic does not reproduce) but integrating a second code generator now would split helper-function math from entry-kernel-body math onto two different correctness bars for no reported need — go-wasmcontainers' own kernels are ordinary index/activation math, not integer-overflow edge cases. Left as a documented, not a silent, limitation; worth revisiting if a real kernel ever needs it.
- Tests: `TestHelperFunctionsEndToEnd` (a two-level call chain, main → scaleAndRelu → relu, relu itself calling the built-in `max` — proves emission order, parameter/return plumbing, and a built-in call from inside a helper, not just the entry body, all at once; bit-for-bit on real Metal hardware) and `TestHelperFunctionCalledFromInsideAForLoop` (a helper called from inside ANOTHER helper's own for-loop body, declared AFTER its caller in the source — the one shape straight-line bodies don't exercise, and the reason callee-before-caller ordering cannot just follow source order). The pre-existing "helper function call" rejection case in `TestUnsupportedConstructsAreRejected` is now a genuinely different rejection (a helper touching a resource directly), since calling one is supported now. Both the ordering and the resource-touch guard independently verified decisive: each sabotaged alone, confirmed the matching test failed, then restored.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`gofmt -l`/`govulncheck ./...` clean; `go test ./...` green across the whole repo; `go test -race -count=5` clean on the affected package; real GPU hardware, not a stub.

## 2026-11-08 (round 25) — `createBindGroup` accepts a GPUBufferBinding sub-range, not only the whole buffer (fix, go-wasmcontainers report)

Reported by go-wasmcontainers after v0.45.2 fixed their GPU hang (engine#388): `createBindGroup: a GPUBufferBinding size other than the whole buffer is not supported`, blocking `batch`/`batch-grant`/`batch-shaders`, 3 of 8 `batch-refusals` cases, and all 123 ONNX cases — all of them pack several logical buffers into one real allocation, binding each as its own sub-range, the one shape `createBindGroup` had never supported (its own doc comment stated it outright: "the one shape wgslmsl's own storage-array-only resource scope ever needs" was "the WHOLE buffer").

Two independent gaps, both needed for the fix (confirmed independently: a test deliberately sabotaging just one, with the other intact, still failed):

- **go-macos/metal's own `Encoder.Buffer` always bound `setBuffer:offset:atIndex:` at offset 0** — no caller anywhere in this fleet could bind a sub-range, only the whole buffer. Fixed upstream (`go-macos/metal` v0.7.0): new `Encoder.BufferAt(index, b, offset)`, `Buffer` now `BufferAt(index, b, 0)`.
- **The bounds-check sizes buffer (`wgslmsl`'s own per-binding array-length mechanism, `_sizes.sN`, used to clamp every storage-array index — Metal performs none of its own) was computed from the bound `*gpuBufferState`'s whole-buffer size**, since `createBindGroup` only ever stored the whole buffer, never a sub-range. Fixed by introducing `gpuBoundBuffer{buf, offset, size}` as what a bind group entry actually resolves to, carrying this binding's OWN offset/size (defaulted and bounds-checked against WebGPU §8.2.1's "resides inside the buffer" rule, with §4.2.1's `minStorageBufferOffsetAlignment`/size-multiple-of-4 validated using the limits this binding already advertises) through to both the Metal-level bind (now at the real offset) and the sizes-buffer computation (now `size / ElemBytes`, this binding's own element count).
- Tests: `TestCreateBindGroupSubRangeHonoursOffsetAndItsOwnLength` (a single, deterministic write to a literal out-of-range local index — no concurrent writer ever touches wgslmsl's own clamp target, so the result is bit-for-bit regardless of the clamp — proving the write lands exactly where the offset AND this binding's own length both say it should, not where either alone, wrongly computed, would put it) and `TestCreateBindGroupSubRangeBindsDisjointRegionsOfOneBuffer` (two bind groups, two disjoint 256-byte-aligned regions of one buffer, two different uniform constants — identical constants would have hidden one binding's offset silently falling back to 0). Both independently verified decisive by temporarily reintroducing each sub-bug in isolation and confirming the matching test failed, then restoring the fix.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`gofmt -l`/`go test ./...` clean in both repos; `go test -race -count=5` clean on the new and neighbouring WebGPU tests; real GPU hardware, not a stub, on both sides.

## 2026-11-08 (round 24) — `Worker.postMessage` is genuinely asynchronous now, not a blocking round trip (fix, engine#388, M2/M3 follow-up)

Found and diagnosed jointly with go-wasmcontainers' own session, root cause of the v0.45.0 GPU hang round 22/23 didn't explain: `postToWorker` (worker.go) blocked the sender until the receiving Worker finished handling the posted message — a deliberate M2 design choice ("a deterministic, testable round trip"), and a real, intentional deviation from the spec (`postMessage` must be asynchronous — HTML Standard §9.4.3, "queue a task... the sender never waits for the receiver"). For a container whose entire life runs inside handling its first message (go-wasmcontainers' own `worker.js`: `onmessage` calls `_start()` synchronously, returning only once the guest exits), "wait until the receiver finishes handling it" became "wait until the container exits" — and a GPU call needing the SENDER's own event loop to run again (a promise it must resolve, to `Atomics.notify` the waiting container) could never get it, since the sender was still waiting on `postToWorker`. Confirmed directly via a `SIGQUIT` goroutine dump on go-wasmcontainers' own failing `TestBatchComputeContainers`: the container's own "run" goroutine parked in `Atomics.wait` (no timeout); the outer worker's goroutine parked inside `postToWorker`'s own wait loop.

- **Rejected first**, confirmed by a direct test before considering it further: keep `postToWorker` blocking, but also drain the sender's own pending timers/promise jobs while it waits (extending round 17's inbox-only drain). This does unblock the deadlock, but breaks JS's own run-to-completion guarantee — a `setTimeout`/`Promise.then` callback can run in the MIDDLE of the currently-executing synchronous script that called `postMessage`, before that script's own next statement runs. Caught with a worker that takes 50ms to reply, racing a zero-delay timeout and an already-resolved promise against a `console.log` immediately after `postMessage`: the timeout's own log line appeared first.
- **The fix**: `postToWorker` clones (per the structured-clone algorithm's own timing — fixed at the moment `postMessage` is called, not when the receiver later processes it) and queues the message, then returns immediately. A reply, if any, is observed later, through the same mechanisms that already deliver a worker's unsolicited messages (`drainWorkerInboxes`, called from `drainTimers` or a worker's own ticker) — never synchronously from `postMessage` itself.
- **A genuinely new data race, exposed (not caused) by workers now running truly concurrently with the main goroutine**: `Options.Log` is one func value every binder in a session shares (main plus every worker, however nested) — previously serialized in practice by `postToWorker`'s own block, now not. `console.*` and this package's own internal `logf` now funnel through one new `emitLog` helper, serialized by a session-wide mutex (mirroring the existing `wasmInstantiateMu` pattern) — confirmed by `go test -race` catching the exact race on a test's own `logs = append(...)` closure before this fix, clean after.
- **The four invariants go-wasmcontainers' own test suite checks on every release**, preserved: per-port FIFO order (new `TestWorkerPostMessagePerPortFIFOOrder`); structured-clone semantics fixed at post time (unchanged — cloning already happened before any queueing); a child's `Atomics.wait` inside its own `onmessage` no longer blocks the parent (`TestWorkerPostMessageDoesNotBlockItsOwnWakeUp`, restructured to poll rather than observe synchronously); unsolicited messages delivered while a child keeps running (unchanged mechanism).
- Every existing test asserting a worker's reply was visible immediately after `postMessage` returned — the "deterministic round trip" M2 was built for — is restructured to poll (`RunPending` on a real, short interval) until the expected condition is observed, via a new shared `settleUntil` test helper, rather than assuming postMessage's own return means anything was delivered. Caught by running the full suite repeatedly (`-count=25`, `-race -count=10`), not a single pass: several of these only failed intermittently, since Go's own scheduler sometimes ran the worker goroutine fast enough for the old assumption to still hold by luck.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo, repeated), cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets, `govulncheck` clean, the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` clean.
- Reporting back to go-wasmcontainers with this fix's version for `TestBatchComputeContainers`/`TestWebGPUBackend`.

## 2026-11-08 (round 23) — a self-requeuing short-delay timer could starve an older, larger-delay one indefinitely under `drainTimers`' own delay-ascending ordering (fix, engine#342 fallout)

Found while investigating a go-wasmcontainers report of a new hang on v0.45.0 (GPU containers now get past `InstallReplay`, then the render never completes until `JSTimeout`). A minimal, isolated `GOOS=js` program — a background goroutine that keeps re-arming its own `time.Timer` at a doubling, capped gap, exactly `go-gfx/webgpu`'s own `kickLoop` shape, which exists specifically so Go's "all goroutines asleep" deadlock detector doesn't fire while a sibling goroutine awaits a promise — reproduced a real, independently-confirmed `drainTimers` defect: `js/js.go`'s own "ascending delay, ties FIFO" selection compared each due job's raw, static `delay` field. A job that keeps re-queueing a *fresh* (non-repeat) copy of itself never grows that field, so it always wins the comparison over an older job with a larger delay, for as many sweeps as it keeps re-arming — unbounded, since nothing ages the older job's own priority.

This narrower defect does not, by itself, explain go-wasmcontainers' own hang: their driver sets `EnableWorkerStreaming`, which gates every job's first firing by a real `readyAt` (`RealTimeTimers`, settle_scripts.go) — under that mode a further reproduction shows the page's `setTimeout` job sitting in `b.jobs` unexamined for the full budget, which points at the render's own live-loop/callback-reentrancy path, not at `drainTimers`' job selection. That piece is still open; this fix addresses the real, separately-confirmed one found along the way, for the default (non-`EnableWorkerStreaming`) timer queue.

- **The fix**: each `timerJob` (js/js.go) gained a `skipped` counter. Every `drainTimers` sweep that passes a due job over for a smaller-priority one increments its `skipped`; selection now compares `delay - skipped` instead of raw `delay`. A persistently-starved job's effective priority strictly falls, sweep by sweep, until it wins — bounded and deterministic, independent of how much real wall-clock time a tight, no-delay drain loop actually advances between sweeps (confirmed by first trying a wall-clock-based virtual deadline instead, which turned out to be exactly that: flaky, since 2000 synchronous goja calls in a unit test don't reliably advance even one millisecond of real time).
- Test: new `TestTimerSelfRequeuingChainDoesNotStarveAnOlderJob` — an unbounded, `kickLoop`-shaped chain (1ms doubling to 8ms, always below an older 10ms job's own delay) against the engine's own `maxTimerJobs` cap. Verified failing (the cap hit with the older job still starved) against the pre-fix code, passing reliably (10/10 runs) with the fix; the existing `TestTimerDelayOrdering`/`TestTimerNegativeDelayClampsToZero`/`TestTimerDelayTiesAreFIFO` are unaffected, since a job that runs within a few sweeps of being enqueued never accrues enough skips to change its relative order.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo).
- Reporting back to go-wasmcontainers: this fix is real but does not resolve their own hang; the minimal reproduction and the live-loop/reentrancy lead are shared for the continuing investigation.

## 2026-11-08 (round 22) — `Blob`, `URL.createObjectURL`/`revokeObjectURL`, and `importScripts` resolving a `blob:` URL (feature, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' retest of v0.44.5: "the whole container chain now runs end to end: every guest starts, makes its WASI calls and exits with its real code" — `batch-no-gpu` PASSES outright, their first fully green case on this engine. The other five cases now stop earlier, in their GPU host opening its device inside the engine worker: `go-gfx/webgpu v0.15.0 record_js.go` loads its replay script in a Worker as `new Blob([source], {type: "text/javascript"})` -> `URL.createObjectURL(blob)` -> `importScripts(thatBlobURL)` -> `URL.revokeObjectURL(url)` — and none of `Blob`/`createObjectURL`/a `blob:`-aware `importScripts` existed in this engine at all.

- **Scope, confirmed by go-wasmcontainers' own report**: the ONE shape actually needed is a `Blob` built from an array of STRING parts with a `type` option, `URL.createObjectURL`/`revokeObjectURL` (File API §8's "blob URL store"), and `importScripts` resolving a `blob:` URL against that store — not BufferSource/Blob parts, `.arrayBuffer()`/`.stream()`, or any other Blob method, none of which any confirmed caller touches.
- **Implementation** (new `js/blob.go`): `Blob`'s own content and `URL`'s own static methods are both scoped PER-BINDER, never a package-level registry like `wasmModuleRegistry`/`wasmMemoryRegistry` — a `blob:` URL is only ever valid in the environment that minted it, matching a real browser's own per-environment blob URL store, and exactly matching go-wasmcontainers' own shape (one Worker creates and consumes its own Blob entirely by itself). `installURL` (replacing the bare `g.Set("URL", ...)` both the main document's own `install()` and every Worker's `installWorkerGlobals` previously had) wires the SAME `URL` constructor plus `createObjectURL`/`revokeObjectURL` as static methods on it, and also installs `Blob` — so both get identical treatment from one shared function. `importScripts` (worker.go) now checks a `blob:` prefix BEFORE `resolveURL`'s own path-resolution logic (an opaque identifier, not a path to resolve against `PageURL`) and, for one, looks the exact string up in this binder's own blob URL store directly, skipping `fetchScript`'s HTTP path entirely.
- Test: new `TestWorkerBlobCreateObjectURLImportScripts`, the full round trip inside a Worker — `Blob`'s own `.size`/`.type`/`Object.prototype.toString` identity, a real `blob:`-prefixed URL, the imported script actually running (observed through a global it sets), and `revokeObjectURL` leaving that same URL genuinely unusable afterward (`importScripts` on it again throws). Caught a real arithmetic mistake in the test's own first draft (an assumed byte count that was off by two) on the very first run — not vacuously passing.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets, the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` clean.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with this fix's version — the one piece they flagged as likely last before the GPU path itself.

## 2026-11-08 (round 21) — a module's own shared memory now stays live for every agent even when grown by the wasm `memory.grow` INSTRUCTION, not just the JS-level grow() method (fix, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' retest of v0.44.4: exception identity FIXED (containers now report exit 2 with the guest's real stderr, and the page completes) — round 20's biggest confirmation yet. `clock_time_get` still failed, now pinned to the exact errno with their own debug build: `EFAULT writing 8 bytes at 3335848` against a memory the engine worker still saw at its ORIGINAL 50-page size. Their own diagnosis: round 20's fix only synced on the JS `grow()` method or a local `.buffer` read — but their REAL guest grows with the wasm `memory.grow` INSTRUCTION itself, during Go runtime start-up, inside wazero, and the container worker that ran it never touches `.buffer` again afterward (it's blocked in `Atomics.wait` inside a host import while the engine worker reads the memory). Their own refined probe reproduced it precisely: `byteLength 65536 here (never re-read), 131072 in the guest`.

- **Confirmed directly before changing anything**: wazero exposes no public hook for "a memory just grew" (`MemoryGrown` is an internal, unexported engine interface, not part of `api`/`experimental`) — ruling out the peer's own second suggestion (a memory listener). Their FIRST suggestion — read the registered buffer's length LIVE from `api.Memory.Size()`/`.Read()` on every access, in every agent, rather than caching a snapshot — is directly supported by wazero's own source: `MemoryInstance.Grow`, for a shared memory specifically, stores the new length with an ATOMIC operation "to make the new length visible across threads" (its own comment, `internal/wasm/memory.go`) — an explicit, intentional cross-goroutine contract. Verified empirically, not just read: an isolated program growing a real shared wazero memory from one goroutine while another concurrently calls `Size()`/`Read()` 200,000 times ran clean under `go test -race` — confirming this is actually safe to rely on, not merely "probably fine."
- **The fix**: `sharedBuffer` (atomics.go) gained an optional `liveMem` field (a minimal local interface — `Size`/`Read` — so this file stays wasm-agnostic) and a `resyncLocked()` method that re-fetches `.data` from it before any access. `wrapWasmMemory` (wasm.go) now sets `liveMem: mem` on the registered buffer for a module's own exported shared memory; every place that touches `sharedBuffer.data` — the `.buffer` accessor in both `wrapWasmMemory` and `wrapSharedWasmMemory` (used by every OTHER agent reconstructing this exact Memory after a postMessage), and every `Atomics.*` op (load/store/add/compareExchange/wait) — now calls `resyncLocked()` first. A buffer with no `liveMem` (every other kind: a plain `SharedArrayBuffer`, or the standalone `new WebAssembly.Memory({shared:true})` constructor) pays one nil check and nothing else changes for it.
- Test: new `TestWorkerExportedSharedMemoryGrownByInstructionIsVisibleAcrossAgents` — a hand-built module whose OWN exported function grows memory with the `memory.grow` instruction and writes a byte past the original size, entirely inside wasm; the worker posts the Memory WITHOUT ever touching `.buffer` itself. Verified failing (`65536`, the peer's own exact number) against round 20's own code, passing (full `131072` + the written byte) with this fix.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets, the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` clean.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with the confirmed mechanism, the empirical race-safety check, and this fix's version.

## 2026-11-08 (round 20) — a host import's own thrown exception propagates unchanged again, and a module's exported shared memory now grows visibly across every agent holding it (fix, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' retest of v0.44.3 was the biggest jump yet: the container guest fully RUNS — instantiated, memory accepted, `_start` entered, `fd_write` working end to end through the shared memory — then stops, with two findings in one report.

- **Exception identity across wasm frames (confirmed, fixed)**: their own `worker.js` host import throws `new Exit(code)` when the guest calls `proc_exit`; per the WebAssembly JS API, a host function's own exception unwinds the wasm frames UNCHANGED and is rethrown to the exported function's caller as the SAME value (only a genuine trap becomes a new `RuntimeError`) — their own `catch (err) { if (err instanceof Exit) ... }` depends on exactly that. Here it arrived as `RuntimeError: GoError: [object Object] ... (recovered by wazero)` instead — `instanceof Exit` always failed, every container reporting exit 134 with no code.
  - **Root cause, confirmed by reading wazero's own panic recovery directly** (`internal/wasmdebug/debug.go`'s `FromRecovered`): it intercepts and re-wraps ANY panic from a host-import Go callback, REGARDLESS of what was panicked with — but it preserves a panicked value's own identity via Go's `%w` ONLY when that value already satisfies the `error` interface (its own `recovered.(error)` branch); anything else gets stringified with `%v`, unrecoverable afterward. `hostImportFunc` (wasm.go) panicked with `b.vm.NewGoError(err)` — a brand-new Go error wrapping only err's STRING — discarding the original `*goja.Exception` (and the real thrown value inside it) entirely before wazero even got a chance to preserve it.
  - **The fix**: `hostImportFunc` now panics with `err` itself, unchanged — already a `*goja.Exception` when the JS import threw, so wazero's own `%w` wrapping keeps it reachable. `wrapWasmFunction`, on the way back out, now does `errors.As(err, &exc)` and panics with `exc.Value()` — the ORIGINAL thrown JS value — mirroring `importScripts`' own identical rethrow pattern (worker.go) already established in this codebase for a script-level exception. Falls back to the existing generic `RuntimeError` only when the error chain holds no such exception (a genuine trap).
  - Test: new `TestHostImportExceptionPropagatesUnchanged` (a hand-built module whose import throws a custom `Boom` instance; the calling script's own `catch` must see `instanceof Boom` hold and its own property intact). Verified failing (`caught=false code=undefined`) before, passing (`caught=true code=42`) after.
- **A module's own exported shared memory didn't grow visibly across agents (found and fixed the same round, go-wasmcontainers' own follow-up probe, confirmed with Chromium passing it)**: a worker instantiates a module whose exported memory is shared, calls `mem.grow(1)`, writes past the original size, posts the Memory to its creator — which saw the ORIGINAL byteLength, not the grown one, so every write above the initial size landed `EFAULT`, and Go's `runtime.nanotime`'s own `clock_time_get` call failed as collateral damage (its heap/stacks live above the initial pages once the Go runtime grows memory at startup).
  - **Root cause**: `wrapWasmMemory`'s own `*sharedBuffer` (the Go-level identity `clone.go` hands to every OTHER agent via postMessage) was built fresh, from scratch, on every `.buffer` read when wazero's own memory size changed — a NEW, disconnected `*sharedBuffer` object each time, never the SAME one already registered and already held by another agent. `grow()` itself never touched the registered buffer at all. Confirmed exactly: reverting the fix reproduced the peer's own numbers precisely (`65536` instead of `131072`).
  - **The fix**: the registered `*sharedBuffer` is now created exactly once and its own `.data` field updated IN PLACE (a fresh, zero-copy `mem.Read` — wazero itself never relocates a shared memory's own backing array on grow, confirmed directly, so this is a fresh VIEW over the identical bytes, not a copy) on every size change — never replaced. `grow()` now eagerly re-syncs it immediately after `mem.Grow` succeeds, rather than waiting for the next local `.buffer` read, since a receiving agent may check `.buffer.byteLength` right after being notified, with no guarantee the growing agent ever reads its own `.buffer` again first.
  - Test: new `TestWorkerExportedSharedMemoryGrowIsVisibleAcrossAgents` — a worker grows, writes, and posts the Memory to its creator (which must see both without ever calling `grow()` itself), then the creator writes back and posts the SAME Memory back, and the worker — which never re-read `.buffer` locally after its own grow — must see the creator's write too. Verified failing (`65536`, matching the peer's own exact number) before, passing (full round trip, both directions) after.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets, the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` clean.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with both confirmed mechanisms and this fix's version.

## 2026-11-08 (round 19) — `WebAssembly.Memory` never satisfied `instanceof WebAssembly.Memory`: a prototype that was never stamped, in every path this file builds one (fix, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' retest of v0.44.2: the `_start` double-run was fixed (containers now failed 5.6s in instead of 9s, at a later point), but every container still failed — their own `worker.js:138-141` check, `if (!(memory instanceof WebAssembly.Memory)) { postMessage({t:"trap", message:"the module exports no memory"}); ... }`, fired even though their guest genuinely exports a (wasip1-rewritten, shared) memory. Their own report named the exact shape to test: compile a module with a shared exported memory in one worker, post the Module to a second, instantiate there, and check `instance.exports.memory instanceof WebAssembly.Memory`.

- **Reproduced directly, fast, before guessing**: a new test posting a `memoryOnlyModuleBytes`-built shared-memory module's compiled `Module` — NESTED inside a plain data object's own property (`{module: mod}`, matching `runtime/browser/executor_js.go`'s own `msg.Set("module", mod)` shape exactly, not round 16's own bare top-level `postMessage(mod)`) — to a second worker, which instantiates it. Result: `isMemory=false isSAB=true` — `.buffer` correctly reports `SharedArrayBuffer`, but the Memory object itself fails `instanceof`.
- **Root cause, found by reading every place this file builds a Memory wrapper**: `wrapWasmMemory` (a module's own exported memory), `wrapSharedWasmMemory`, and `wrapNonSharedWasmMemory` (the standalone `new WebAssembly.Memory(...)` constructor, both kinds) each stamp `Symbol.toStringTag` so `Object.prototype.toString.call()` reports `"[object WebAssembly.Memory]"` — but NONE of the three ever called `SetPrototype`. Round 7 gave `WebAssembly.Module`/`.Instance` a real prototype via `installWasmClassIdentity` specifically so `instanceof` would hold for THEM (go-wasmcontainers' own `wasm_exec.js` need); `WebAssembly.Memory` never got the matching treatment, masked ever since because every existing test in this codebase checked the toStringTag, never `instanceof`, for Memory specifically.
- **The fix**: a new `wasmMemoryProto` (mirroring `wasmModuleProto`/`wasmInstanceProto`), a real `WebAssembly.Memory` constructor function wired with its own `.name`/`.prototype` (`wasmMemoryCtor`, the same pattern `wasmClassCtor` already gives Module/Instance, except Memory already has a REAL, spec-required synchronous constructor — `newWasmMemory` — unlike Module/Instance's deliberate panic-only stand-ins), and `SetPrototype(wasmMemoryProto)` added to all three Memory-wrapper construction sites.
- Tests: new `TestWasmMemoryInstanceofHolds` (standalone + module-exported, non-shared, no cross-origin isolation needed) and `TestWasmModulePostMessageWithSharedMemoryInstantiatesWithMemoryIntact` (the exact cross-worker, nested-property, shared-memory shape go-wasmcontainers described). Verified in both directions: reverting the fix reproduced `isMemory=false`/a leaked Go-side closure name in `WebAssembly.Memory.name` exactly; restored, both pass.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets, the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` clean.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with the confirmed mechanism and this fix's version.

## 2026-11-08 (round 18) — `WebAssembly.instantiate()` no longer auto-runs an export merely named `_start`: only the binary's own internal start section should ever run implicitly (fix, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' retest of v0.44.1: the 10-minute hang was gone (round 17's fix), the engine worker logged in real time, all 6 containers started — then, 9 seconds into every one, `LinkError: module[] function[_start] failed: wasm error: invalid table access`, with a repeating `runtime.write ← runtime.writeErrData ← runtime.badsystemstack ← ... ` wasm stack trace. Their own diagnosis, from the WebAssembly JS API spec's own instantiate algorithm and wazero's own documented default, named the exact mechanism before I'd read a line of code myself.

- **Confirmed directly, not taken on faith**: wazero's own `config.go` (`github.com/tetratelabs/wazero@v1.12.0`) sets `startFunctions: []string{"_start"}` as `NewModuleConfig`'s default, and documents `WithStartFunctions()` called with NO arguments as how to clear it — a WASI-CLI convenience (run the guest's own entry point automatically, matching `wasmtime run`'s own behaviour), not anything the WebAssembly JS API itself specifies. The JS API's own `instantiate()` runs only a module's internal, unexported start SECTION (rare, never present in a WASI module) — never an EXPORTED function merely named `_start`, which is just an ordinary export like any other.
- **The real trigger**: go-wasmcontainers' own real `worker.js` (`runtime/browser`, the exact version their e2e module pins) instantiates the guest, THEN posts its shared memory to the engine worker, THEN calls `instance.exports._start()` itself — precisely the JS spec's own division of responsibility. With this engine's `instantiateWasm` inheriting wazero's default, `_start` ran a FIRST time as part of `instantiate()` itself, before the "memory" message had even been sent — the guest's own first `fd_write` reached the host with no memory registered yet, failed, and Go's own panic/error-write path recursed into the identical failure, producing exactly the reported trap. worker.js's own explicit call then ran it a SECOND time regardless.
- **The fix**: the one `InstantiateModule` call `instantiateWasm` funnels every JS-facing instantiate path through (`instantiate(bytes)`, `instantiate(module)`, `instantiateStreaming`) now also passes `.WithStartFunctions()` with no arguments, clearing wazero's default. The binary format's own internal start section (set via `wazero.NewRuntimeConfig`'s own module-start machinery, unrelated to this list) still runs exactly where the spec says it should; nothing in this codebase's own tests ever relied on the auto-run (confirmed by search — `_start` appears nowhere else in this package).
- Tests: new `js/wasm_test.go`'s `TestInstantiateDoesNotAutoRunAnExportNamedStart`, a hand-built module importing `env.touch` and exporting it as `_start`. Verified in both directions: reverting the fix reproduced the double-run exactly (`touched=1` after `instantiate()` alone, `touched=2` after the explicit call that should have been the ONLY run); restored, `touched=0` then `1`.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets, the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` clean.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with the confirmed mechanism and this fix's version.

## 2026-11-08 (round 17) — `Worker.postMessage` no longer blocks its own wake-up: a nested-worker deadlock when the receiving handler calls `Atomics.wait` synchronously (fix, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' very next report on v0.44.0: `TestBatchComputeContainers` no longer failed with "not a WebAssembly.Module" (round 16's fix), but now hung the full 10-minute `JSTimeout`, ending in `RuntimeError: module closed with context deadline exceeded`. Their own reasoning, from the trace alone, named the right shape before any code was read: "the engine worker must keep receiving its child's messages while its own Go program is between events" — and flagged the exact possibility that turned out to be true, "If nested-worker → parent-worker messages are only delivered when the parent's event loop is idle in some specific way... this deadlocks."

- **Confirmed by reading both sides, not assumed**: go-wasmcontainers' real `runtime/browser` package (`github.com/go-wasmcontainers/engine`, their own dependency, read at the exact pinned version their e2e module uses) spawns one container `Worker` per guest and posts it a `{module, ctl, funcs}` setup message via `worker.Call("postMessage", msg)` — a call made from THEIR Go program, itself running as the "engine worker"'s own compiled-to-`js`-wasm guest, via `syscall/js`. The container's own `worker.js` (also read at that exact version) answers with `onmessage = async (e) => { ...; start(); }`, where `start()` runs the guest to completion; every WASI import it makes calls `postMessage({t:"call"})` to wake its creator, THEN calls `Atomics.wait` — SYNCHRONOUSLY, still inside that same `onmessage` call, never deferred via `setTimeout(fn, 0)`.
- **Why that is valid, spec-compliant code, not a bug in their worker.js**: `Worker.postMessage` is asynchronous in the HTML Standard and in every real browser — the sender's call returns immediately, regardless of what the receiver's `onmessage` handler does once dispatched. This engine's own `postToWorker` (`js/worker.go`) was, since round M2, **deliberately synchronous/blocking instead** — documented at the time as a trade-off for a deterministic, testable round trip, with `atomicsWait`'s own doc comment already naming the exact hazard this creates ("calling it synchronously from inside a message handler would hold the SENDER's blocked postToWorker hostage... see README's own documented interaction for the recommended pattern: `setTimeout(fn, 0)` to yield before waiting"). go-wasmcontainers' `worker.js` is real, unmodified, cross-browser-tested code with no reason to carry a go-webengine-specific workaround for a limitation no real browser has — so the gap was this engine's own synchronous `postMessage`, not their code.
- **Confirmed directly, fast, before touching the real 10-minute repro**: wrote `js/atomics_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerPostMessageDoesNotBlockItsOwnWakeUp` — a creator posts a setup message to a worker whose `onmessage` handler posts a "wake" doorbell THEN calls `Atomics.wait` synchronously, exactly mirroring `worker.js`'s own shape; the creator's own `onmessage` (for its child's wrapper) answers the doorbell with `Atomics.notify`. With the original flat blocking `select` in `postToWorker`, this hung out its full 2-second deadman every time — the same mechanism as the 10-minute hang, just compressed from 10 minutes to 2 seconds for a fast, repeatable test.
- **The fix**: `postToWorker`'s wait is now a poll loop (the same `workerTickInterval`/15ms cadence `jsWorker.run`'s own ticker already uses), calling `drainWorkerInboxes()` on every tick instead of once at the end. This lets the blocked creator keep dispatching its OWN children's queued messages — including a container's "wake me" doorbell — to its own `onmessage` handlers WHILE it waits, which is exactly where go-wasmcontainers' real `Atomics.notify` call lives (inside the handler `drainWorkerInboxes` dispatches). A worker with no such nested dependency sees no behavior change: draining an empty or not-yet-populated inbox on every tick is a no-op, and the loop still returns the instant `done` closes, exactly as before.
- Verified in both directions: the new test hangs its full deadman with the original single blocking `select` restored, and completes in ~20ms with the fix. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (whole repo), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets, the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` clean.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with the confirmed mechanism and this fix's version.

## 2026-11-08 (round 16) — `WebAssembly.Module` survives `postMessage` across workers: a session-shared wazero Runtime, plus two collision pitfalls go-wasmcontainers flagged before hitting them (feature, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' own confirmed shape: their engine worker compiles each guest module ONCE and posts the resulting `WebAssembly.Module` to the container's own worker, which instantiates it there (`WebAssembly.instantiate(module, imports)`) — never the bytes, since recompiling per-container would be wasted work at their scale. Round 15's crash fix was unrelated to this; this round is the feature they asked for next, not another round of the same investigation.

- **Why this needed real architecture, not just a clone case**: every binder (main document, every Worker, recursively) previously created its OWN `wazero.Runtime` lazily. wazero's own `CompiledModule` is tied to the specific `Runtime` that compiled it — confirmed directly in an isolated scratchpad program before writing any production code: instantiating a `CompiledModule` under a DIFFERENT `Runtime` fails immediately with `"source module must be compiled before instantiation"`. A Module posted from one worker to another, each with its own Runtime, would therefore be permanently unusable on the receiving side — not a missing clone case, a load-bearing assumption the whole feature breaks on.
- **The fix**: `wasmRT()` now delegates to a `rootBinder` (nil for the main document, set once at construction for every worker, however deeply nested, to the top-level binder itself — one hop regardless of nesting) so every worker, however many, shares exactly ONE `Runtime`. `clone.go` gained `clonedWasmModule` (mirroring the existing `clonedWasmMemory`/`clonedSharedBuffer` shape): a package-level, mutex-protected `wasmModuleRegistry map[*goja.Object]wasmModuleState`, keyed by object identity, lets `cloneAt` recognize a posted `WebAssembly.Module` wrapper without needing a specific binder's own per-binder map, and `reconstructValue` rebuilds it via the existing `wrapWasmModule` in the receiving VM.
- **Two collision pitfalls go-wasmcontainers flagged before their own workload would hit them, both confirmed directly in an isolated scratchpad program before touching production code, not assumed from wazero's docs**:
  - Sharing one Runtime means `InstantiateModule`'s own namespace is now also shared. wazero refuses a second instance under a name already live (`"module[...] has already been instantiated"`) — harmless when every worker had its own Runtime, but go-wasmcontainers' own real workload instantiates the SAME compiled guest many times concurrently, one per container, every one sharing the same default/custom-section name. Fixed: `instantiateWasm` now always instantiates anonymously (`wazero.NewModuleConfig().WithName("")`). Confirmed directly: repeated anonymous instantiation of one `CompiledModule` on a shared Runtime succeeds with zero errors under `-race`, and closing one such instance does not affect a sibling instance of the same module.
  - The SAME sharing exposed a second, independent collision this feature's own first implementation hit in testing, not flagged in advance: `buildHostImports` registers a temporary host module under the GUEST's own required import namespace (e.g. `"env"`) before instantiating it — on a shared Runtime, two guest instances that both import from `"env"` (go-wasmcontainers' own concurrent-container shape again) collide on THAT name too, even though neither guest cares what the host module was called. Confirmed directly (a second isolated scratchpad program, hand-assembled minimal WASM bytes) that wazero binds an import's host function into the guest at `InstantiateModule` time, not by a live name re-lookup on every call — so closing the host module immediately after the guest instantiates, then reusing the freed name for the next guest, does not disturb either guest's own already-bound calls. Fixed: `instantiateWasm` now holds a new `wasmInstantiateMu` (delegating to the root binder exactly like `wasmRT`) across the whole register-host-module→instantiate-guest→close-host-module sequence, serializing that narrow window across every worker sharing the Runtime.
- Tests: `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWasmModulePostMessageBetweenWorkers` (one worker compiles `add.wasm` and posts the `Module` through the main document to a SECOND worker, which instantiates it and calls the exported function — the main document's own received value must satisfy `instanceof WebAssembly.Module`, not an opaque clone) and `TestWasmModulePostMessageSiblingInstancesAreIndependent` (the same `Module` posted to two SEPARATE workers, both instantiating concurrently; both must succeed, and one worker finishing must not disturb the other's still-running instance). Verified failing before the host-import-lock fix — `TestWasmModulePostMessageSiblingInstancesAreIndependent`'s second worker genuinely failed with `"REJECTED:LinkError: module[env] has already been instantiated"` — and passing after.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (including the whole repo, not just `js`), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on all 6 GOARCH targets (`linux/amd64`, `linux/arm64`, `darwin/arm64`, `windows/amd64`, `js/wasm`, `wasip1/wasm`), the separate `bench/` module's own `go vet` (via its `replace` into this checkout) clean too.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers: both pitfalls they flagged were real and are now fixed, tested exactly against the shapes they described.

## 2026-11-08 (round 15) — the ACTUAL cause, independently confirmed: wazero's Compiler engine runs JIT-compiled code on its own stack, which corrupts after a reentrant resume (fix, engine#342 fallout)

Round 14's `GPUDevice.destroy()` fix was real but, like rounds 12 and 13 before it, go-wasmcontainers' own retest showed it was not their crash either. This time they found it themselves: bisected down to a GPU-free, ~20-line reproduction — a WASM guest that awaits one resolved Promise through a `js.FuncOf` callback (resuming the guest from the event loop), then writes to stdout a second time. Their own reasoning pointed at nested JS→wasm reentry: `wasm_exec.js`'s own `fs.write` shim invokes its JS callback SYNCHRONOUSLY, re-entering the guest's wasm instance (`go._resume`) from INSIDE an active host-import call — harmless the first time, fatal the first time it happens AFTER the guest has already been resumed once from the event loop.

- **Confirmed directly, not just by reasoning from the peer's report**: copied their own probe program (per their own instruction, not editing their tree in place) and ran it, unmodified, against a locally-patched copy of this engine — crashed under the current code, passed cleanly (all 20 rounds, including real WebGPU calls) once `js/wasm.go`'s `wasmRT` was switched from `wazero.NewRuntimeConfig()` to `wazero.NewRuntimeConfigInterpreter()`.
- **Root cause, found in wazero's own documentation** (`github.com/tetratelabs/wazero`'s `config.go`, `NewRuntimeConfigCompiler`'s own doc comment, not inferred): the default "Compiler" engine (`NewRuntimeConfig()` picks it automatically on amd64/arm64 — the two architectures this exact crash was ever seen on) JIT-compiles a WASM module to native machine code that "uses the different stack than the calling Goroutine," and the doc comment names the EXACT consequence: "the signal handler might get invoked on the wazero's stack, which may cause a stack overflow" — the literal "split stack overflow" crash signature seen throughout this investigation. Live native debugging (lldb, both at launch and attaching mid-flight) remains fundamentally stuck in this sandboxed environment (every `task_for_pid`-requiring tool hangs at 0% CPU); this was found by reading a third-party dependency's own documentation once the peer's bisection pointed at "native code, wazero's own compiled output" specifically, not by attaching a debugger to confirm it directly.
- **The fix**: `wasmRT` now builds via `wazero.NewRuntimeConfigInterpreter()` unconditionally. The pure-Go Interpreter engine generates no native code and hence has no separate stack to desynchronize from the calling goroutine — this removes the entire crash class, not just this one reproduction of it. On loong64/ppc64le/riscv64/s390x this changes nothing (wazero's Compiler was never supported there either; the Interpreter was already the automatic fallback) — the real behavior and performance change is scoped to amd64/arm64, the only architectures where the bug could ever have fired.
- **Measured, not just asserted, performance cost** (this project's own standing convention): a 200-million-iteration integer arithmetic loop, run end-to-end through a real Worker exactly as the regression test below does, took 3.3s under the Compiler engine and 52.7s under the Interpreter engine — roughly 16x slower for this compute-bound shape. A real, substantial cost, accepted directly in exchange for removing a native-process crash — the only trade that was ever on the table once the mechanism was confirmed.
- Tests: new `js/testdata/reentry/main.go` (a tiny real Go-compiled-to-WASM program reproducing the peer's own exact minimal pattern) and `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestReentrantResumeAfterEventLoopResume`, plus a new `buildWasmFixture` helper (this repo's first test to compile and run a FULL Go-runtime-on-wasm program via `wasm_exec.js`/`go.run`, rather than the existing `add.wasm` fixture's bare-module shape). Verified directly, both directions: with the fix reverted, the test reproduced the EXACT SAME crash signature (`unexpected return pc for runtime.sigpanic`, `fatal error: unsafe.Slice: len out of range`, `hexdumpWords`) inside this repo's own `go test` process; restored, it passes cleanly. Deliberately not asserted as an automated revert-and-fail step in the shipped test (that would crash CI, not fail a test) — the same reasoning rounds 13/14's own tests already state.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean, `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with the confirmed diagnosis, the measured performance cost, and this fix's version — this is the first of the four rounds in this investigation independently verified against their own exact reproduction BEFORE shipping, not just a plausible, real-but-unconfirmed gap found by code reading alone.
- **Follow-up, found by reading rather than another repro**: the Interpreter engine's crash fix surfaced a SEPARATE interaction, not a new bug — go-wasmcontainers' own `TestBatchComputeContainers` (a heavier, nested-WASM-in-WASM workload than the crash repro) started hitting `renderCoreStaged`'s existing heap-safety guard (`heapGuardedContext`, default ceiling `baseline + 768 MiB`, a real pre-existing feature from round 172's own 21 GB memory-exhaustion defect, not something this round touched). The guard polls `runtime.MemStats` and cancels its OWN derived child context (NOT the caller's `ctx` — confirmed directly from `renderCoreStaged`'s source, and independently from the caller's own side, where `ctx.Err()` stayed nil throughout since only the CHILD gets cancelled) once heap allocation crosses the ceiling; that cancellation becomes the session's own `Options.Ctx`, so the render's own watchdog interrupts scripts with `"context cancelled"` once tripped — explaining a report that initially looked like a brand-new ~20s timeout bug. The Interpreter engine's own execution state for a memory-heavy nested-WASM workload apparently needs more Go-heap than the Compiler engine's JIT-compiled code did for the same work, crossing the DEFAULT ceiling where it previously didn't. Resolution: a caller-side configuration change (raise `Engine.MaxJSHeapBytes` for a workload this heavy under the Interpreter engine), not a code change here — the guard is doing exactly what it is designed to do.

## 2026-11-08 (round 14) — a real device-leak fix, independently real but not THE crash either (fix, engine#342 fallout)

Round 13's stopWorkers fix was real, but go-wasmcontainers' own retest on it (and separately on round 12's go-macos/metal v0.6.1) proved NEITHER was their crash: same two signatures (`hexdumpWords`'s "unsafe.Slice: len out of range", and "split stack overflow"), now deterministic 6/6, and — the detail that ruled out round 13 directly — happening 8-12s into the render, well before any `Session.Close`/worker teardown.

Live native debugging was attempted first (lldb attach, both wrapping the launch and attaching mid-flight to an already-running process): every `task_for_pid`-requiring tool (`lldb -p`, `vmmap <pid>`, `atos -p <pid>`) hung indefinitely at 0% CPU in this sandboxed environment — a genuine environment restriction, not a slow debugger, confirmed by checking process state directly (`ps` showed the traced process parked in state `T`, no CPU time accruing, for minutes). Abandoned that path and went back to direct code reading, this time starting from the corrected fact ("mid-render, single Worker, sequential dispatch — not a teardown race") rather than re-deriving the same two hypotheses more carefully.

- **The bug**: `GPUDevice.destroy()` (`js/webgpu.go`) only set a local `destroyed` flag and resolved the `lost` promise — a purely JS-visible, cosmetic signal nothing else in the file ever checked. It never called `.Close()` on the underlying `*metal.Device` (a real `MTLDevice`+`MTLCommandQueue`, allocated fresh by every `requestAdapter()`/`requestDevice()` call, confirmed earlier this engine never caches or shares one). go-wasmcontainers' own workload loads a fresh ONNX model into its own graph/device per case (~123 of them in `TestWebGPUBackend`), explicitly `defer`-closing each graph between models — but since `destroy()` did nothing at the native level, every one of those 123 "destroyed" devices stayed alive, accumulating until some OS-level resource ceiling was crossed deep inside Apple's own Metal/Foundation frameworks — a genuine native-process crash no Go-level safety net can catch or convert into a graceful error, and NOT something `go test -race` could ever flag (native heap memory, not anything the race detector instruments).
- **The fix**: `destroy()` now calls `dev.Close()` and removes the device from `b.gpuDevices` (so the end-of-render `closeGPUDevices` sweep doesn't redundantly touch it — harmless either way, since `metal.Device.Close()` already no-ops on an already-zeroed device, but keeps that slice from growing unbounded across a long-running page).
- **Why this couldn't be safely reproduced as an exhaustion stress test**: deliberately triggering the real resource ceiling would crash the TEST BINARY itself (the same native memory-safety failure being fixed), not fail gracefully. Instead, `js/webgpu_test.go`'s new `TestDeviceDestroyReleasesTheNativeDevice` proves the MECHANISM: after `destroy()`, a subsequent `createBuffer()` against that device is routed (via the EXISTING `panicOutOfMemory` path every other `NewBuffer` failure already uses) through a pushed `"out-of-memory"` error scope, and must come back as a captured `GPUOutOfMemoryError` — proving `dev.Close()` genuinely ran (go-macos/metal's own `NewBuffer` already refuses a closed device). Verified in both directions: with the fix temporarily defeated, `createBuffer` silently SUCCEEDED against the "destroyed" device (no error captured); restored, it correctly failed.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean, `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- Both earlier fixes (go-macos/metal v0.6.1's `AutoreleasePool` wrapping, round 13's `stopWorkers` wait) remain independently correct and shipped — neither was wasted work, they closed real gaps the direct-code-reading investigation found along the way; this round is simply the one that actually explains go-wasmcontainers' own reproduction.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with the corrected diagnosis and this fix's version; asked them to retest.

## 2026-11-08 (round 13) — the REAL cause of the native crash: stopWorkers released a worker's GPU devices before the worker's own goroutine had stopped using them (fix, engine#342 fallout)

Round 12's go-macos/metal v0.6.1 bump was real and independently justified, but go-wasmcontainers' own retest proved it did NOT fix their crash — worse, it made it DETERMINISTIC (6 of 6 runs, same signature: `sigpanic` from a dylib PC, then the Go runtime's OWN crash-report code dying trying to unwind it — `fatal error: unsafe.Slice: len out of range` in `hexdumpWords`, once `fatal error: runtime: split stack overflow`). Their own lead — the fault PC consistently landing at the same offset, and a "split stack overflow" variant suggesting a native callback running on a thread the Go runtime doesn't recognize — pointed at worker/GPU lifecycle code, not the Metal binding's own entry points (confirmed by reading: go-macos/metal registers no Objective-C callbacks into Go anywhere; `waitUntilCompleted` is a plain blocking `objc_msgSend`, not a completion-handler block).

The real bug, in `js/worker.go`'s `stopWorkers` (called from `Session.Close`): it closed a worker's `stopCh` (a mere SIGNAL, only observed between units of work in `w.run`'s own select loop) and IMMEDIATELY called `w.wb.closeGPUDevices()` — releasing live Objective-C `MTLDevice`/`MTLCommandQueue` objects — with NO wait for the worker's own goroutine to actually stop. `jsWorker`'s own struct doc comment already says `wb` is "touched ONLY by w.run's goroutine" — this exact cleanup code was violating that invariant. A worker whose own autonomously-scheduled timer callback (go-wasmcontainers' own confirmed shape: "the worker runs for the container's whole lifetime... posts unsolicited messages at any time") is mid-flight inside a real, blocking Metal dispatch is not selecting on anything when `stopCh` closes — it is buried inside a native call, deaf to the signal. `Session.Close()` then freed the exact objects that call was still using: a genuine concurrent use-after-free on native memory, which Go's own race detector cannot see (the corruption is in unmanaged C/Objective-C heap memory, not anything `-race` instruments) and which does not always crash on the SAME run — exactly matching "intermittent, became deterministic once the workload got heavier" (a bigger/longer GPU dispatch widens the race window).

- **Fix**: `jsWorker` gained a `done chan struct{}`, closed unconditionally (`defer close(w.done)`, the first line of `w.run`) on every return path. `stopWorkers` now closes `stopCh` and then WAITS on `<-w.done` (bounded by the SAME deadman every worker is already bound by — so a worker truly stuck past the render's own deadline does not hold `Close()` open forever) BEFORE calling `stopWorkers` recursively or `forgetSharedBuffers`/`forgetWasmMemories`/`closeGPUDevices` — nothing touches what a worker's binder owns until that worker's own goroutine has genuinely stopped touching it.
- **Why this couldn't be a channel-ordering unit test**: both `new Worker()` and `Worker.postMessage` already block the calling goroutine until the worker finishes whatever they triggered (by design, documented in their own doc comments) — neither can create this race. Only genuinely unsolicited, timer-driven worker-side activity can, which is also exactly go-wasmcontainers' own real usage shape.
- Tests: `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestSessionCloseWaitsForWorkerBeforeClosingGPUDevices` — a worker registers a one-shot `setTimeout` whose callback runs a real, deliberately slow Metal compute dispatch (a single-invocation serial loop accumulating `sqrt()`, NOT a trivial increment — measured directly: 1.6 BILLION plain increments took 48ms, LESS than 80 million `sqrt()`-accumulations' ~600ms, because the shader compiler strength-reduces a foldable loop away entirely). The test waits past one `workerTickInterval` (so the worker's own ticker has already started the dispatch autonomously), calls `Session.Close()`, and asserts it did NOT return before a floor far below the dispatch's own measured cost. Verified both directions directly, not just written and trusted: with the fix temporarily defeated, `Close()` returned in 48–800µs (tiny, fixed-independent — the dispatch simply never got a chance to matter); with the fix in place, `Close()` correctly took the ~600ms the dispatch needed.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean, `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected (no new dependency this round).
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers with the corrected diagnosis and this fix's version; asked them to retest, since this (not the AutoreleasePool fix) is the one actually responsible for what they reproduced reliably.

## 2026-11-08 (round 12) — go-macos/metal bumped to v0.6.1: an AutoreleasePool gap matching a native crash go-wasmcontainers reported (dependency, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers reported an intermittent native SIGSEGV (not a JS error — a crash in the process itself) running concurrent per-Worker WebGPU calls, with Go's own crash dump failing to unwind the foreign Objective-C stack frame. Investigation (in go-macos/metal, not this repo) found 8 functions (`Default`, `Device.Close`, `Device.Compile`, `Library.Close`, `Library.Pipeline`, `Pipeline.Close`, `Device.NewBuffer`, `Buffer.Close`) sending Objective-C messages without the `objc.AutoreleasePool` wrapping the SAME file's own `submit`/`Pending.Wait` already use — a goroutine migrating OS threads mid-call is exactly the condition `objc.AutoreleasePool`'s own doc comment names as causing "a SIGSEGV inside `objc_autoreleasePoolPop`, at a program counter with nothing of this package on the stack." Apple's own Metal docs leave `MTLDevice` resource-creation methods without a thread-safety guarantee (only `MTLCommandQueue` is documented safe). Fixed and released as go-macos/metal v0.6.1 (go-macos/metal#17); this round bumps the dependency and re-verifies.

- Not reproduced locally even with a dedicated 96,000-operation concurrent stress test (48 goroutines × 400 iterations × 5 rounds, with concurrent GC pressure) — run both before AND after the fix, 0 errors both times — consistent with the race being machine-dependent (the `objc.AutoreleasePool` doc comment's own wording: "a race, so it does not fail on the machine it was written on"), not a sign the gap was unreal.
- Deliberately did NOT add a redundant application-level mutex serializing WebGPU entry points in `js/webgpu.go` as extra insurance: the actual gap is now closed at its real layer, and a lock here would simply serialize concurrent per-Worker GPU work — the exact capability this feature exists to provide — without covering anything the library fix doesn't already cover.
- `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean (including the real-Metal-hardware WebGPU test suite against the new binding), `govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- Reported back to go-wasmcontainers: the crash could not be reproduced locally despite real, escalating stress testing, but a real, well-evidenced, Apple-undocumented thread-safety gap matching their exact signature was found and fixed — asked them to retest since their environment reproduces it reliably.

## 2026-11-08 (round 11) — `Options.MaxRequests` / `Engine.MaxRequests` (feature, engine#342 fallout)

go-wasmcontainers' own real workload fetches on the order of 123 ONNX models plus their inputs/outputs — several hundred requests per render — which hit the previous hardcoded `maxRequests = 100` cap with no way to raise it. Same convention as `MaxResponseBytes`/`MaxWasmModuleBytes`/`MaxWasmMemoryBytes`: a sane default, configurable for a confirmed larger real workload.

- **`js/net.go`**: the unexported `maxRequests = 100` constant renamed `defaultMaxRequests`, with a new `(b *binder) maxRequests() int` resolver (zero or negative → default, matching every other `maxXxx()` resolver in this file) threaded into `doRequest`'s own existing cap check.
- **`Options.MaxRequests`/`Engine.MaxRequests`** (new fields, `js/js.go`/`engine.go`), threaded through `settle_scripts.go`'s `jsOptions`.
- Tests: `js/net_test.go`'s new `TestMaxRequestsResolution` (the resolver's own zero/negative/explicit-value table) and `TestMaxRequestsRaisedAllowsMoreFetches` (a real end-to-end render issuing `defaultMaxRequests+50` fetches with `MaxRequests` raised to match, asserting all succeed with zero errors). Three pre-existing literal references to the renamed constant fixed. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.

## 2026-11-08 (round 10) — WebGPU error scopes now actually CAPTURE errors, instead of throwing past them (fix, engine#342 fallout)
