Round 9's `pushErrorScope`/`popErrorScope` only got the push/pop BALANCE right — every GPU error still threw synchronously at the point of failure, same as before any error scope existed, which go-wasmcontainers caught immediately: WebGPU's own error model (§22 "Errors & Debugging") says `create*`/`submit`/`dispatch` NEVER throw for a GPU content-validation failure — the error is captured by the innermost open scope (or fires `"uncapturederror"` with none open), and the call itself still returns, an "invalid" placeholder standing in for the real result. go-gfx/webgpu relies on exactly that: it wraps creations in `pushErrorScope("validation")`/`popErrorScope()` and turns the popped `GPUValidationError`'s own message into a Go error — so a guest's bad WGSL needs to come back as a captured, positioned error, not a panic that kills the whole worker (in Go's own `syscall/js`, a JS exception re-panics on the Go side, so the previous throw-synchronously design was fatal for them, not just spec-incorrect).

- **A real, WebIDL-aware distinction, not a blanket catch.** A WebIDL-level argument-shape problem (missing descriptor, wrong object type passed) is NOT the same error class as GPU content validation (bad WGSL, a dispatch missing a bind group, an out-of-range `writeBuffer`) — the FIRST still throws a real synchronous `TypeError` regardless of any open scope (this binding keeps that distinction deliberately); only the SECOND is captured. A new Go type, `gpuValidationPanic{err, class}`, marks exactly which panics represent the second kind — `gpuGuard`'s own `recover()` re-panics anything else UNCHANGED, so an ordinary `TypeError` or a genuine Go bug still crashes exactly as before.
- **`gpuGuard`** wraps every create*/`dispatchWorkgroups`/`queue.writeBuffer`/`queue.submit` call site device-wide (keyed by the `*metal.Device` each one already had in scope — no new parameter threading needed anywhere): on a caught `gpuValidationPanic`, it walks the device's own error-scope stack INNERMOST first, looking for a scope whose own declared `filter` ("validation"/"out-of-memory") matches the error's class — captures it there (first error wins; a scope already holding one discards a later one, per spec) — or, if no scope matches at all, fires `"uncapturederror"` on the device. Either way the wrapped call returns normally.
- **Real `GPUValidationError`/`GPUOutOfMemoryError` objects**, not a generic error shape: `newGPUError`/`gpuErrorProto` stamp each with its own `.prototype` so `err.constructor.name` reads correctly — confirmed directly needed (go-gfx/webgpu reads exactly that). Never exposed as globals (no confirmed need for a script to `new GPUValidationError(...)` itself, only to read one `popErrorScope()` already handed it).
- **Which panics actually changed class**: bad WGSL / Metal shader rejection (`gpuBuildComputePipeline`), a dispatch missing a bind group or binding for a declared `@group`/`@binding` (`gpuDispatchWorkgroups`), an out-of-range `queue.writeBuffer` (previously a `TypeError`, now correctly a `GPUValidationError` — WebGPU's own `writeBuffer` algorithm "generates a validation error" for this, not a WebIDL throw), a `queue.submit` execution failure, and a `createBuffer` allocation failure (routed as `GPUOutOfMemoryError`, the other concrete `GPUError` subtype this binding can actually produce). `createComputePipelineAsync` deliberately does NOT go through error scopes at all — per spec it always rejects its own promise directly on a compile failure, a documented special case, not an oversight; its own panic is recovered locally and converted straight to a rejection.
- Tests: `js/webgpu_test.go`'s `TestDispatchWithoutBindGroupRejected` rewritten entirely — it used to assert a synchronous throw, which was the bug; it now asserts the call does NOT throw and the error is captured with `constructor.name === "GPUValidationError"`. New `TestDispatchErrorFiresUncapturedErrorWithNoScopeOpen` (the same failure, no scope open, `"uncapturederror"` fires instead). New `TestCreateComputePipelineBadWGSLCapturedByErrorScope` and `TestQueueWriteBufferOutOfRangeCapturedByErrorScope` reproduce go-wasmcontainers' own first and third named scenarios directly (the second, a dispatch missing a bind group, is `TestDispatchWithoutBindGroupRejected` itself). All on real Metal hardware. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.

## 2026-11-07 (round 9) — WebGPU: feature/limit iterables, error scopes, explicit bind group layouts, `copyBufferToBuffer` (feature, engine#342 fallout)

Round 8's fix got Go's `net/http` fetch path working inside both workers. The next stop was WebGPU itself: go-gfx/webgpu v0.15.0's own `gpu_js.go` panicked reading `adapter.features`/`device.features` with `.values()` — `GPUSupportedFeatures` is `setlike<DOMString>` (WebGPU §3.5: `has`/`size`/`values`/`keys`/`entries`/`forEach`/`[Symbol.iterator]`), and this binding's own always-empty features object only had `has`/`size`. They pre-identified every WebGPU member their own compute subset touches (read from go-gfx/webgpu's source directly, not guessed) before hitting each one a round at a time; a follow-up measurement on v0.41.0, each item checked independently so none hides another, narrowed it to exactly four real gaps — `adapter.features.values()`/`device.features.values()`, `device.pushErrorScope`/`popErrorScope`, the `GPUShaderStage` namespace (not installed at all, unlike its `GPUBufferUsage`/`GPUMapMode` siblings), and `device.createBindGroupLayout` (which also meant `createPipelineLayout`, `createComputePipelineAsync`, and the mapAsync-readback item right behind it were never reached).

- **`GPUSupportedFeatures` as a real `setlike<DOMString>`** (`js/webgpu.go`): `values`/`keys`/`entries`/`forEach`/`Symbol.iterator` all correctly report EMPTY — reusing `js/net.go`'s own `newArrayIterator` helper (round 8) over `nil` — matching `size === 0`; there is nothing to iterate, not a missing method.
- **`GPUShaderStage`** (new `newGPUShaderStage()`, wired into both the main document's `install()` and a Worker's `installWorkerGlobals()`, same opt-in gate as `GPUBufferUsage`/`GPUMapMode`): `VERTEX`/`FRAGMENT`/`COMPUTE` per spec.
- **`device.pushErrorScope`/`popErrorScope`**: push/pop balance (an unmatched `popErrorScope()` rejects with a real `OperationError`). **SUPERSEDED the next day — see round 10**: this round's own first attempt still reported every GPU-surface error SYNCHRONOUSLY as a thrown exception, reasoning there was no async event stream for a scope to CAPTURE instead — that reasoning was wrong (WebGPU's own error model never throws for GPU content validation, scope or no scope) and go-wasmcontainers caught it immediately. Round 10 makes capturing real.
- **Explicit `createBindGroupLayout`/`createPipelineLayout`, and `createComputePipeline`/`createComputePipelineAsync` accepting a real `GPUPipelineLayout`** (not just `"auto"`) — confirmed directly needed: go-wasmcontainers' own batch compute path binds a loop-budget group next to the guest's own groups, which a single pipeline's `"auto"`-inferred layout cannot express. Resource-TYPE validation still happens entirely at dispatch time against `wgslmsl.Result`'s own WGSL-source-derived bindings — unchanged from the `"auto"` path, and the reason an explicit layout's own declared entries carry no enforcement here: a `GPUBindGroupLayout`/`GPUPipelineLayout` object's real job in this binding is being a recognizable handle, not a second source of truth. `getBindGroupLayout(idx)` on an explicitly-layouted pipeline now returns the SAME object identity passed into `createPipelineLayout` at that index, matching spec (a non-`"auto"` pipeline's layout is fixed, not pipeline-specific) — `gpuComputePipelineState` gained an `explicitLayouts []*goja.Object` field for this. The core translate/compile logic was factored out of `gpuCreateComputePipeline` into `gpuBuildComputePipeline` (returns an error VALUE instead of panicking) specifically so `createComputePipelineAsync` could reuse it without duplicating the logic — wrapped in an already-settled Promise, the same "no real background compile to await" shape every other async WebGPU entry point here already uses.
- **`GPUCommandEncoder.copyBufferToBuffer`** — confirmed directly needed for exactly the readback shape go-wasmcontainers described: a storage buffer's result copied into a separate `MAP_READ` buffer (a buffer bound as a compute target typically cannot also be directly mapped), then read via `mapAsync`/`getMappedRange`. The REAL correctness subtlety, worth stating: executing the copy as a plain Go `copy()` **immediately when recorded** (the naive approach) would read STALE bytes, because Metal's own command-buffer execution happens when `dev.Run`'s closure returns and commits, not as each encoder call is made inside it — a copy interleaved inside the SAME `dev.Run` as the dispatch it depends on could run before that dispatch's GPU work has actually finished. Fixed by generalizing the command encoder's own recorded list (`gpuRecordedDispatch` → `gpuRecordedCommand`, a dispatch/copy union in ENCODING order) and having `gpuQueueSubmit` flush (and **block on**, via `dev.Run`'s own documented behaviour) every pending dispatch before each copy, rather than batching everything into one `dev.Run` call — a real, found-not-assumed correctness gap, not a style preference.
- Tests: `js/webgpu_test.go`'s `TestRequestAdapterNeverRejects` extended with `features.values()` and `GPUShaderStage.COMPUTE` checks; new `TestExplicitBindGroupLayoutEndToEnd` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' own exact confirmed minimum end to end (4-invocation storage-buffer compute, explicit layout, `copyBufferToBuffer` + `mapAsync` readback, expecting `0,3,6,9` bit-for-bit on real Metal hardware); `TestDevicePushPopErrorScopeAndUncapturedError`/`TestPopErrorScopeWithoutPushRejects`; `TestCreateComputePipelineAsyncEndToEnd` (both a real successful async creation and a real async rejection, through the async entry point specifically, not assumed from the sync one). `TestComputePipelineRejectsExplicitLayout`'s own doc comment updated — the test itself (a bare, unregistered `{}` layout) still correctly rejects, now for the right documented reason. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- Deliberately NOT built, per their own narrowed list: `getPreferredCanvasFormat`/textures/samplers/render pipelines/bundles (explicitly out of scope for their compute-only subset).

## 2026-11-07 (round 8) — `Headers.entries()`/`keys()`/`values()`, and `AbortController` (feature, engine#342 fallout)

Round 7's fix got Go actually RUNNING inside both of go-wasmcontainers' workers (instantiate 2.5–3.1s for a ~10 MB module, `performance.now()` real). The next stop, in both tests: the first time Go's own `net/http` fetches anything, `net/http/roundtrip_js.go` panicked on `result.Get("headers").Call("entries")` — Headers' own pair-iterable methods did not exist at all. They also pre-identified the REST of what that exact file touches (read from Go 1.27.3's own source, not guessed) before it could be hit one round at a time: `AbortController`, `Headers.append`, the Response body's `getReader()`/`arrayBuffer()` fallback, rejection `.message`/`.cause`. A follow-up measurement on v0.40.2 (in a worker, Chromium passing all the same checks) narrowed it to exactly two gaps: `AbortController` ("is not defined" — Go constructs one before EVERY fetch and passes `.signal`) and `headers.entries()`; `Headers.append` and the Response body path were already working.

- **`Headers.entries()`/`.keys()`/`.values()`, and `Symbol.iterator`** (`js/net.go`): the Fetch Standard's own pair-iterable definition for Headers requires iterating "sorted and combined" by name — a NEW `sortedKeys()` helper is now the one path `forEach`/`entries`/`keys`/`values` all go through, so they agree with each other (Go's own `map` iteration order, which `forEach` alone used before, is deliberately randomised and would otherwise make two calls over the SAME Headers disagree). A new `newArrayIterator` helper builds the real `next()`-protocol iterator object each of the three returns — also self-iterable (`[Symbol.iterator]` returns itself), so `for (const pair of headers.entries())` works, not just a bare `.next()` call Go's own code happens to use. `Symbol.iterator` on Headers itself defaults to `entries()`, per spec, so `for (const pair of headers)` works too.
- **`AbortController`/`AbortSignal`** (DOM Standard §3.1, new `js/net.go`): `new AbortController()` builds a real `.signal` (`.aborted`/`.reason`/`.onabort`/`addEventListener`/`removeEventListener`/`throwIfAborted`) and `.abort(reason?)` (defaulting to a real `DOMException` named `"AbortError"`, firing `onabort`, a no-op on a second call). This engine's own `fetch()` always completes synchronously (the real I/O already happened inline by the time it returns — confirmed, not new behaviour, from `fetch`'s own existing doc comment), so there is no genuinely in-flight request for `.abort()` to interrupt mid-flight — stated directly rather than faked with a half-working cancellation path. What IS real and implemented: a signal already aborted BEFORE `fetch()` is even called now rejects immediately with the signal's own reason (DOM §3.1/Fetch §4.1's own documented check), rather than being silently ignored.
- Tests: `js/net_test.go`'s new `TestHeadersIterableMethods` (sorted-and-combined order across `entries`/`keys`/`values`/`for...of`/`Symbol.iterator` directly), `TestAbortControllerBasics` (default `AbortError` reason, a custom reason, `onabort` firing with the right event target, a second `abort()` call correctly ignored), `TestFetchRejectsAlreadyAbortedSignal`. `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerHasAbortControllerAndHeadersEntries` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' own exact confirmed context end to end: inside a real Worker, a real fetch, `AbortController` constructed and passed as `.signal`, `response.headers.entries()` read via `for...of`. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- Deliberately NOT built, per their own follow-up measurement: genuine mid-flight fetch cancellation (this engine's fetch is synchronous by construction, so there is no "mid-flight" to cancel), and `ReadableStream`/`Response.body.getReader()` (their own probe confirms the `arrayBuffer()` fallback already reads real bytes correctly when `.body` is `undefined`, which Go's own transport treats as an accepted path, not an error).

## 2026-11-07 (round 7) — `WebAssembly.Instance`/`.Module` identity, and a real `performance.now()` (fix, engine#342 fallout)

Round 6's fix unblocked go-wasmcontainers' fetch; their next probe run (9s instead of 5 min, with real errors) found two more, both confirmed and both inside the Worker a guest runs in. Reported back promptly rather than discovered independently this time.

- **`WebAssembly.Instance`/`WebAssembly.Module` identity.** Go's own `wasm_exec.js` (`Go.run`, line 480) does `if (!(instance instanceof WebAssembly.Instance)) throw ...`. Neither global existed at all before this round, so the `instanceof` itself threw `TypeError: Value is not an object: undefined` rather than evaluating to `false` — aborting the guest before it ran a single instruction. Fixed with `installWasmClassIdentity` (`js/wasm.go`): `WebAssembly.Module`/`.Instance` are now real constructor functions, each with its own `.prototype`, and every object `compile()`/`instantiate()` return (one-step AND the two-step `compile().then(instantiate)` path) is stamped with the matching prototype via `SetPrototype`. The file's own top doc comment already ruled out the SYNCHRONOUS constructor forms (`new WebAssembly.Module(bytes)`) as unneeded — that stands: calling either via `new` now throws a clear, named `TypeError` ("the synchronous constructor is not supported; use WebAssembly.compile/instantiate") rather than silently doing nothing or crashing oddly once the globals exist.
- **A real `performance.now()`.** Found to be a pure stub — `o.Set("now", func(...) { return b.vm.ToValue(0) })` — for BOTH the main document and every Worker, not a Worker-specific regression; an existing test's own exact-string assertion (`"perf=0,0,0,0,0"`) had pinned the stub's wrong output as "correct" and never caught it. go-wasmcontainers' own probe measured it precisely: a 2,000,000-iteration loop between two `performance.now()` calls inside a Worker gave the identical value (a real browser's own advances). Go's `wasm_exec.js` uses `performance.now()` for its monotonic clock (`runtime.nanotime1`) — a guest's own `time.Since`/timers would never see elapsed time pass AT ALL under the old stub, however long it actually ran, a much more corrosive failure than a thrown error since nothing visibly breaks. Fixed with a new `binder.timeOrigin time.Time` field (the real wall-clock instant `Begin`/`newWorkerBinder` created that binder — one per render AND one per Worker, matching the HR-Time spec's own per-global-object `timeOrigin`), `now()` returns real `time.Since(b.timeOrigin)` in milliseconds, `timeOrigin` itself reports real epoch-milliseconds rather than `0`.
- Tests: `js/wasm_test.go`'s new `TestWasmInstanceAndModuleInstanceofHold` (one-step path, `instanceof` on both Instance and Module, the sync-constructor TypeError) and `TestWasmCompileThenInstantiateModuleInstanceofHolds` (the two-step path, kept separate — instantiating the SAME compiled module's host-import namespace twice in one render hits an unrelated wazero constraint, out of scope here). `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerWasmInstanceofAndPerformanceNow` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' EXACT context: inside a real Worker, a real compiled module, `instanceof` holding AND `performance.now()` advancing across a real 2,000,000-iteration loop. `js/js_test.go`'s `TestHistoryScreenPerformance` updated to check `now()`/`timeOrigin` as plausible real numbers (the old exact-string assertion was pinning the bug). `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- A related near-miss, not shipped: after round 6's fetch fix, applying the SAME "reject on truncation" change to six OTHER `io.ReadAll(io.LimitReader(...))` sites (main page body, images/fonts, ES module source, form submission, `file:` URLs, `<script src>`) was caught by the existing test suite BEFORE commit — `TestReadFileURLRefusals`/`TestFetcher` both assert truncate-and-succeed as the INTENDED behavior for the engine's own internal resource caps, a genuinely different contract from `fetch()`'s script-facing one. Reverted; see the memory this produced (`a-truncating-read-is-not-always-the-same-defect`) for the full reasoning — not repeated here since no code changed.

## 2026-11-07 (round 6) — fetch() silently truncated a response over 8 MiB; it now REJECTS instead (fix, engine#342 fallout)

Found while investigating go-wasmcontainers' own separate report: `TestWebGPUBackend` (a plain Go-compiled wasm, no container, no shared memory) "did not report within 5 min and logged nothing at all" instantiating a ~10 MiB module via `instantiateStreaming`. Built a real ~9 MiB `GOOS=js GOARCH=wasm` binary locally to reproduce; timed wazero's own `CompileModule` on it directly — **1.6 seconds**, not slow at all. The real cause was upstream of compilation entirely: `js/net.go`'s `doRequest` read `io.ReadAll(io.LimitReader(resp.Body, maxResponseBytes))` with a hardcoded 8 MiB cap — and `io.ReadAll` on a `LimitReader` returns a **nil error** whether the underlying body was exactly that size or far larger and simply cut off. A page's own `fetch()` of anything over 8 MiB — a WASM module, an image, JSON, anything — came back looking like a completely successful response carrying silently truncated, corrupt bytes, with **nothing** in the Response object or any error indicating data was lost. `instantiateStreaming` would then hand wazero a truncated module, which fails to compile — but by then the real cause (a silent, undiagnosable truncation three layers up) is gone from the error entirely.

- **`doRequest`** (`js/net.go`): now reads `limit+1` bytes via the SAME `LimitReader`, and if the extra byte actually arrived (`len(raw) > limit`), REJECTS the request outright with a message naming the real cause and the exact limit — "response body exceeds the configured size limit (N bytes)" — rather than returning truncated-but-"successful" bytes. A page's own `fetch()`/`XMLHttpRequest` now sees a clearly-named `TypeError` rejection instead of a Response it has no way to know is incomplete.
- **`Options.MaxResponseBytes` / `Engine.MaxResponseBytes`** (new fields): the hardcoded 8 MiB cap is now configurable, the SAME "zero selects a sane default" convention as `MaxWasmModuleBytes`/`MaxWasmMemoryBytes`/`MaxJSHeapBytes` — a caller with legitimately larger resources (go-wasmcontainers' own ~10 MiB modules, or any other large fetch) raises it explicitly rather than hitting an unconfigurable wall with no diagnostic.
- Tests: `js/net_test.go`'s new `TestMaxResponseBytesResolution` (the resolver's own zero/negative/explicit-value table, mirroring `TestMaxWasmModuleBytesResolution`), `TestDoRequestRejectsResponseOverTheLimit` (an 11-byte body against a 10-byte limit, asserting the error names "10 bytes" and "exceeds the configured size limit"), `TestDoRequestAcceptsResponseExactlyAtTheLimit` (the boundary control — a body of EXACTLY the limit must succeed intact, guarding the `limit+1` read against an off-by-one), `TestFetchRejectsOversizedResponseVisibleToScript` (the SAME fix proven at the JS-visible `fetch()` layer a real page observes), `TestMaxResponseBytesRaisedAcceptsALargerResponse` (a real 9 MiB round trip, byte-count verified, succeeding once the cap is raised — directly reproducing go-wasmcontainers' own confirmed need). `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- Not yet confirmed: whether raising `MaxResponseBytes` is the FULL explanation for go-wasmcontainers' own "5 minutes, zero log lines" symptom (a rejected `instantiateStreaming` promise should log something if their own test script's `.catch()` does, which this fix cannot verify from here) — reported the finding and the fix, asked them to confirm after retrying with the cap raised.

## 2026-11-07 (round 5) — a module's own DECLARED shared memory, resolving round 4's one open question (feature, engine#342 follow-up)

Round 4 (just below) shipped standalone `new WebAssembly.Memory({shared:true})` but left open whether go-wasmcontainers' guest modules IMPORT memory (unsolvable without an upstream wazero change) or DECLARE their own shared memory locally. Asked directly; answered directly: their guest never imports memory — `wasmbin.LimitMemory` rewrites the module's OWN memory section to set `maximum` and the shared flag before compiling, the worker instantiates with ONLY function imports, reads `instance.exports.memory`, and posts THAT to a second worker. Exactly the smaller path flagged as worth building if confirmed.

- **`moduleHasSharedMemory(raw []byte) bool`** (new, `js/wasm.go`): wazero's own public API (re-confirmed, same inspection as round 4) has no way to ask whether a module's own declared memory is shared once it has compiled — `api.MemoryDefinition` exposes only `Min`/`Max`. Answered by this engine itself, walking the RAW module bytes directly using the EXACT limits-flag rule wazero's own internal decoder applies (read directly from `internal/wasm/binary/limits.go`, not guessed from the spec text: flag byte `0x02`/`0x03` = shared, `0x00`/`0x01` = not) via a small, bounded unsigned-LEB128 reader. Runs only on bytes `CompileModule` has ALREADY validated — a best-effort second read, not a second validator, so any decode trouble reports `false` rather than panicking.
- **`wasmRT`'s `experimental.CoreFeaturesThreads`, gated on `Options.CrossOriginIsolated`**: wazero's own decoder otherwise rejects ANY module declaring shared memory outright ("shared memory requested but threads feature not enabled", confirmed from the same source read) — enabling the feature only inside an isolated context matches the SAME restriction a real browser enforces, and reuses this binding's existing `CompileError` handling rather than a separate pre-check. Without it, instantiating go-wasmcontainers' own module shape fails cleanly with `CompileError`, not a confusing LinkError or a silent wrong answer.
- **`wrapWasmMemory` threading `isShared` through**, from a new `wasmModuleState{compiled, isShared}` pairing (`wrapWasmModule`/`wasmModuleOf`) that survives the two-step `WebAssembly.compile(bytes).then(m => WebAssembly.instantiate(m, imports))` path, where the raw bytes are no longer in scope by instantiate time. A shared module memory's `.buffer` is now stamped `SharedArrayBuffer`-classed and registered (reusing `wrapSharedArrayBuffer`/`wasmMemoryRegistry` from round 4) — the SAME treatment the standalone constructor's own shared path already gets, so `instance.exports.memory` and `new WebAssembly.Memory({shared:true})` are indistinguishable to a script from here on, including both being `postMessage`-able as the Memory object itself.
- Tests: `js/wasm_test.go`'s new `TestModuleHasSharedMemoryDetection` (direct, JS-free, against hand-built module bytes AND the real `add.wasm` fixture, including truncated/empty inputs reporting `false` rather than panicking), `TestWasmModuleDeclaredSharedMemoryEndToEnd` (a hand-built, memory-only module — no functions needed, go-wasmcontainers' own confirmed need is plain-JS reads/writes over the Memory — instantiated, grown, bytes preserved, `instanceof SharedArrayBuffer` holds), `TestWasmModuleSharedMemoryRequiresCrossOriginIsolation` (the SAME module, without COI, rejects `CompileError`). `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerReceivedModuleDeclaredSharedMemoryIsTrulyShared` reproduces their EXACT architecture end to end through THIS code path specifically (not the standalone constructor's). `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- The hand-built module fixtures are inline byte literals in the test file itself (a tiny, fully-commented construction — memory + export sections only), not a committed `.wasm` binary: more legible for a fixture whose entire point IS its exact bytes, and avoids a second generator script for something this small.

## 2026-11-07 (round 4) — standalone `WebAssembly.Memory(descriptor)`, including `shared: true` (feature, engine#342 follow-up)

Round 3 (just below) left `WebAssembly.Memory({shared:true})` as the one still-failing line in go-wasmcontainers' own worker-globals probe, and flagged by them directly afterward as blocking, not deferrable: every one of their guest containers constructs a shared `Memory` in one worker before instantiating anything, posts that SAME object to a second, plain-JS "engine worker" that services host/WASI calls by reading/writing its bytes directly while the guest blocks in `Atomics.wait` on a separate control buffer — without the constructor existing at all, no container gets past its first syscall.

- **`new WebAssembly.Memory(descriptor)`** (`js/wasm.go`): implemented as a standalone constructor, independent of any module — confirmed correct scope by reading wazero's own public API directly (`wazero.HostModuleBuilder`, `api.MemoryDefinition`, `api.Memory`, v1.8.2): there is no `ExportMemory`/`ImportedMemories` of any kind, so a module's memory IMPORT (the common toolchain shape for a module built with `--import-memory`, e.g. Emscripten with pthreads) cannot be satisfied by host-provided bytes through this library at all. That half is a real, upstream wazero limitation, stated in this file's own top doc comment rather than silently left unexplained — not something this round's binding code can route around. What the standalone constructor itself does NOT need wazero for at all: it is pure JS-surface plumbing over this engine's own existing `SharedArrayBuffer`/`sharedBuffer` machinery (M3, round 2026-11-04).
- **Growth without breaking sharing.** goja's own `ArrayBuffer` (confirmed directly: only `Detach`/`Detached`/`Bytes` exist, no resize/grow method) cannot change size in place, so a reallocate-and-copy grow — fine for a private buffer — would silently strand every OTHER agent's existing reference to the old bytes, exactly the failure mode shared memory exists to prevent. Fixed by extending `sharedBuffer` (`js/atomics.go`) with an optional `max` field: a growable buffer pre-allocates its full `maximum` capacity up front, and `grow` only ever RE-SLICES from that same backing array, never reallocates — a wrapper built after a grow sees the larger view over the identical bytes a wrapper built before it already wrote into. Documented, not hidden, limitation: a wrapper built BEFORE a grow keeps reporting the OLD, smaller `byteLength` forever (no live-updating `byteLength`, unlike a real engine's growable `SharedArrayBuffer`) — a reader must re-fetch `.buffer` after being notified a grow happened, the exact same requirement `wrapWasmMemory`'s own non-shared, module-exported path already has.
- **Gated on cross-origin isolation, matching `SharedArrayBuffer`/`Atomics` exactly** (round 2026-11-04): `shared: true` without `Options.CrossOriginIsolated` throws `TypeError`, the same denial a real browser gives — not a more permissive shortcut just because the request arrived through a different global.
- **`postMessage`-able as the Memory object itself, not just its `.buffer`** — confirmed directly needed (go-wasmcontainers posts the `Memory`, not a typed-array view over it, to their "engine worker"). `js/clone.go` gained an early check in `cloneAt`'s `"Object"` case (`lookupWasmMemory`, a new package-level registry mirroring `sharedBufferRegistry`'s own by-object-identity design) — needed BEFORE the generic object/ArrayBuffer paths, since a Memory wrapper carries a real `grow` function property that the existing "a function cannot be cloned" rule would otherwise reject outright. A non-shared Memory posted this way is logged and dropped with a specific, actionable reason ("construct it with shared: true"), the same "logged and dropped, not thrown" contract this engine's structured-clone failures already follow everywhere else — never a silent, wrong-looking clone.
- **Bounded by the same configurable quota as a module's own linear memory** (`Options.MaxWasmMemoryBytes`/`maxWasmMemoryPages`) — a standalone `Memory` is no less able to exhaust the host than a module-declared one, and since a shared Memory's full `maximum` is pre-allocated at construction time (not just checked against at grow time), the quota check happens against that ceiling up front.
- Tests: `js/wasm_test.go`'s new `TestWasmMemoryNonSharedBasics`, `TestWasmMemorySharedRequiresMaximumAndCrossOriginIsolation`, `TestWasmMemorySharedBasics` (growth preserving previously-written bytes, read back via a FRESH `.buffer`, through real `Atomics.load`). `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerReceivedWasmMemoryIsTrulyShared` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' own exact architecture end to end: a shared Memory created on main, posted to a Worker, written there through its own reconstructed wrapper, read back through MAIN's original one — proving genuine sharing, not a copy; `TestNonSharedWasmMemoryCannotBePostMessaged` locks in the clone-rejection message. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` all clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks (amd64/arm64/loong64/ppc64le/riscv64/s390x), the separate `bench/` module unaffected (no new transitive dependency this round).
- Still NOT solved, and now precisely scoped rather than just "pending a wazero feasibility check": making an ACTUAL guest `.wasm` module use a host-provided `WebAssembly.Memory` as its own linear memory via a module import. This needs either an upstream `wazero` change (a public memory-import API does not exist today) or a different WASM engine for that one path — not a gap this binding layer can close alone. Reported precisely to go-wasmcontainers, asking whether their guest modules actually import memory (this gap) or declare their own memory locally with the `shared` flag set (a DIFFERENT, smaller gap: enabling wazero's own `experimental.CoreFeaturesThreads` and exposing a module's shared EXPORTED memory as a `SharedArrayBuffer`-classed wrapper — not yet attempted, since it's only worth building if that's actually their shape).
  **ANSWERED the same day — see round 5 just above**: their guest DECLARES its own shared memory locally (never imports it), which is exactly the smaller, now-shipped path. The memory-IMPORT gap remains real and unsolved, but is now confirmed NOT to be on go-wasmcontainers' own critical path.

## 2026-11-07 (round 3) — `WebAssembly`, `location`, nested `Worker`, and a global `Response` constructor (feature + fix, engine#342 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers' own capability probe, rewritten by them to check every worker global at once rather than stopping at the first `ReferenceError`, reported a precise list on v0.36.0: `WebAssembly` (`instantiate`/`instantiateStreaming`/`Memory{shared:true}`), `location` (`WorkerLocation`'s `search`/`hostname`), and nested `Worker` (their own engine spawns workers from within a worker) — plus `fetch`/`Response`/`Headers`/`URLSearchParams`, which this engine's own existing tests already proved present (shipped in the PRIOR round 2) and were re-verified directly rather than assumed broken from the probe's own bullet grouping.

- **`WebAssembly`** and **`location`**: the SAME recurring gap shape as `DOMException`/`crypto` before them — `newWebAssembly()` and `newLocation()` both touch only `b.vm`/`b.opt` (confirmed by reading each body), no DOM dependency, so both are now wired into a Worker's own global scope directly. `location` reflects the WORKER's own script URL (not the creating document's), thanks to the base-URL fix from round 1 — verified, not assumed, by a worker served from a sub-path with a query string. A stated, deliberate simplification: real `WorkerLocation` has no `assign`/`replace`/`reload` (a worker cannot navigate itself); this reuses the main document's own `Location` object as-is rather than building a stricter read-only type, since nothing confirmed needs the stricter shape yet.
- **Nested `Worker`**: `newWorker` had no hidden assumption limiting it to the top-level binder — it already operates generically on whichever binder it's a method of. The one real fix needed: `stopWorkers`' own cleanup walk was single-level, so a worker that itself created child workers would leak every grandchild's goroutine, GPU device and SharedArrayBuffer registry entry past the session's own lifetime. Fixed by recursing (`w.wb.stopWorkers()`) — caught by `TestNestedWorkerAndCleanupCascade`'s own direct assertion that `Session.Close()` closes the GRANDCHILD's `stopCh` too, not just the immediate child's.
- **A global `Response` constructor**: genuinely missing everywhere, not just in workers — `net.go`'s own `installNet` never exposed `new Response(...)` at all, only ever building one internally from a real fetched `httpResult`. Confirmed needed by the SAME probe (`typeof Response === "function"`, a common runtime-shim feature-detection idiom) — fixed once, in `installNet`, so both the main document and every Worker get it from the same call.
- Tests: `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerHasWebAssembly` (a real compiled module, instantiated inside a worker), `TestWorkerHasLocation`, `TestNestedWorkerAndCleanupCascade`; `js/net_test.go`'s new `TestResponseConstructor`. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected.
- Still deferred, and now confirmed actually needed, not merely "someday": `WebAssembly.Memory({shared: true})` — this engine has NO `WebAssembly.Memory` constructor at all yet, for the main document or a Worker, a gap distinct from (and bigger than) "missing from workers" — this is its own round, pending the wazero feasibility investigation already noted as outstanding. `caches` (the Cache API) is confirmed NOT required by go-wasmcontainers' own compute path (their runtime falls back to compiling from bytes when absent) — not implemented, and no plan to unless that changes.
  **CLOSED the same day — see round 4 just above**: the standalone constructor, including `shared: true` and postMessage support, is now implemented. The remaining, more specific gap round 4 found and disclosed: an ACTUAL guest `.wasm` module importing a host-provided Memory, which wazero's own public API has no way to satisfy.

## 2026-11-07 — `wgslmsl`: uniform buffers and storage `atomic<u32>`/`atomic<i32>` (feature, engine#345 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers' own second confirmed need, read from their real code before writing any of this: every looping kernel in their batch path (`batch/loopbudget.go:35`) rewrites each loop to count against a `@group(3) @binding(1) var<uniform> budget: u32` and sets a `@group(3) @binding(0) var<storage, read_write> flag: atomic<u32>` via `atomicStore` before breaking — alongside the guest shader's own `@group(0)`. Without these, no looping kernel in their batch path could run at all.

- **Bibliography before code, twice**: the WGSL spec's own raw Bikeshed source (the rendered `w3.org` page kept truncating at the identical point regardless of fetch offset — confirmed directly across four separate fetches, not assumed, a fleet-worthy lesson on its own) for the exact atomic built-in signatures, WGSL's own single fixed `relaxed` memory ordering, and the `uniform`-address-space rules (a bare scalar IS legal; a runtime-sized array is NOT, confirmed from `#constructible-types`). Then the MSL side was verified against the REAL Metal compiler directly — `atomic_uint`/`atomic_int`, `atomic_load_explicit`/`atomic_store_explicit`/`atomic_fetch_{add,sub,max,min,and,or,xor}_explicit`/`atomic_exchange_explicit` (note: no "fetch" in exchange's own MSL name) with `memory_order_relaxed`, and `constant T&` for uniform — compiled and run through `go-macos/metal` before any of wgslmsl's own code was written, not assumed from documentation (Apple's own spec PDF exceeded the fetch tool's size limit).
- **`BindingKind`** (new, `wgslmsl.go`): `BindingStorageArray` (the existing, bounds-checked kind), `BindingAtomic` (`device atomic_uint*`/`device atomic_int*`, touched only through the atomic built-ins, never indexed), `BindingUniform` (`constant T&`, read-only, never indexed — WGSL itself forbids a runtime array in uniform address space, so there is nothing to bounds-check). Only `BindingStorageArray` resources occupy a slot in the synthesised `_Sizes` bounds-checking struct now (`Binding.SizesIndex`, -1 for the other two kinds) — a real, caught-by-the-new-test's-own-assertions fix needed once resources stopped being 1:1 with Metal buffer indices: `indexExpr`'s own `_sizes.sN` reference was keyed off `MetalIndex`, which silently coincided with the right field only because every binding used to be a storage array; now keyed off the dedicated `SizesIndex`.
- **`atomicLoad`/`atomicStore`/`atomicAdd`/`atomicSub`/`atomicMax`/`atomicMin`/`atomicAnd`/`atomicOr`/`atomicXor`/`atomicExchange`** (`expr.go`): each requires its first argument be `&resource` (WGSL's own pointer-parameter requirement) — since the atomic resource is ALREADY emitted as a real MSL pointer parameter, translating `&resource` is simply the bare parameter name, never an actual address-of in the generated code. `atomicCompareExchangeWeak` is deliberately not supported yet (its own struct return type has no story in this package's bounded scope) — no confirmed need for it either.
- Confirmed directly, not just reasoned about: `layout: "auto"` already correctly builds a SEPARATE `GPUBindGroupLayout` per group number a kernel actually uses — a module using `@group(0)` and `@group(3)` together, bound via two independent `createBindGroup`/`setBindGroup` calls, dispatches correctly with no changes needed to the pipeline/bind-group machinery at all. This was go-wasmcontainers' own explicit open question, answered with a real, dispatched, bit-for-bit-checked test, not by reasoning about the code in the abstract.
- Tests: `wgslmsl/wgslmsl_test.go`'s new `TestUniformBudgetAndAtomicFlag` (the exact loop-budget pattern, dispatched and checked), `js/webgpu_test.go`'s new `TestComputePipelineWithUniformAtomicAndMultipleGroups` (the SAME pattern through the full public `navigator.gpu` API, with the real `@group(0)`+`@group(3)` split). `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, the separate `bench/` module unaffected (no new dependency this round).
- Still out of scope, stated: `var<workgroup>` shared memory, `workgroupBarrier()`, and an exact `@workgroup_size`-matching dispatch shape — go-wasmcontainers' own SECOND ask, needed by their LLM kernels (`argmax`/`q4`), and still blocked: `go-macos/metal` v0.6.0 (checked directly, not assumed stale) still has no way to specify an explicit threadgroup shape for `Dispatch` — it always auto-picks one from the hardware's own reported limits. Making this correct needs an upstream `go-macos/metal` change first, not just a `wgslmsl` one.

## 2026-11-07 — `crypto`/WebCryptoAPI reachable from a Worker's own global scope (fix, engine#342 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers' lane cleared the `importScripts` blocker, then hit the next one in the same file: `Error: globalThis.crypto is not available ... at wasm_exec.js:85:9` — Go's own `wasm_exec.js` calls `crypto.getRandomValues` at startup, inside the worker, before anything else in their script runs.

- **The same recurring shape as `DOMException`'s own earlier gap**: `installCrypto` (`getRandomValues`, `randomUUID`, `subtle`) was only ever called from the main document's DOM-specific `install()`, which a Worker correctly never calls (no DOM in a Worker) — but `Crypto` itself is `[Exposed=(Window, Worker)]` per the WebCryptoAPI spec, general-purpose plumbing with no DOM dependency of its own (confirmed by reading `installCrypto`'s own body: it only ever touches `b.vm` and Go's `crypto/rand`), so it had no business being gated behind that call either. Fixed the same way `installDOMExceptionOnly` fixed the earlier case: `installWorkerGlobals` now calls `wb.installCrypto(g)` directly.
- Test: `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerHasCrypto` — a real Worker calling `crypto.getRandomValues` (checking the filled bytes are not all still zero) and `crypto.randomUUID()` (checking the real 36-character UUID shape), both from inside the worker's own top-level script. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...` clean.
- This is the second time this exact shape of gap (general-purpose spec surface accidentally scoped to the DOM-specific install path) has been found via a real go-wasmcontainers workload rather than a synthetic test — see the memory lesson this session already recorded after the first one; worth a quick audit of `install()`'s own remaining calls (`installNet`, `installEncoding`, `installURLSearchParams`, `installIntl`) for the same pattern before the THIRD one surfaces the same way.

## 2026-11-05 — `WorkerGlobalScope.importScripts()`, and a worker's own relative-URL base fixed to its OWN script URL (feature + fix, engine#342 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers' own compute lane on v0.34.0 blocked at `importScripts("wasm_exec.js")` — their worker's literal first statement — with `ReferenceError: importScripts is not defined`. M2's own Worker support never implemented it.

- **`importScripts(...urls)`** (HTML Standard §10.3.4): every url is resolved and syntax-checked FIRST — a bad url throws `SyntaxError` before anything is fetched, matching the spec's own "import scripts into worker global scope" algorithm order — then each is fetched and run IN TURN, sharing the worker's own global scope exactly like multiple top-level `<script>` tags (the same `goja.Runtime`, via a new `runRethrow` that mirrors `execute` exactly except it RETURNS a compile/runtime error instead of logging-and-swallowing it). A fetch failure or an uncaught exception in an imported script propagates to the CALLING script (spec's own "rethrow errors: true") — the one case this package needs that for; every OTHER script it runs (a worker's own top-level script, a queued timer callback) still has its own errors caught and merely logged, unchanged. A script that already ran stays in effect if a LATER url in the same call fails.
- **A real, independently-found bug, not assumed**: writing `TestImportScriptsSharesGlobalScopeAndRunsInOrder` (a worker served from one httptest origin, `importScripts('/a.js', '/b.js')` with no leading-origin — the realistic shape, matching `wasm_exec.js` itself) failed with a network error on first run. Root cause: `newWorkerBinder` inherited `Options.PageURL` UNCHANGED from the creating document's binder, so every relative-URL resolution inside a worker (not just `importScripts` — anything a worker might ever fetch) resolved against the MAIN PAGE's own URL instead of the worker's own script URL, exactly backwards from both the spec (a worker's own settings object has its own base URL) and from what a worker not co-located with its page's own directory would need. Fixed in `newWorker` (`js/worker.go`): the worker's own script URL is resolved once, there, and threaded into `newWorkerBinder` as that worker's own `PageURL` — the creating document's own `PageURL` is untouched. This is exactly the kind of gap this session's own established practice is built to catch: a confirmed real-world worker script shape (fetch-a-sibling-file), not a synthetic unit-test shape (same-origin relative URL happens to be ambiguous between "resolves against the page" and "resolves against the worker" when both are served from the same place), surfaced it immediately.
- Tests (`js/worker_test.go`): `TestImportScriptsSharesGlobalScopeAndRunsInOrder` (doubles as the regression test for the base-URL fix — it would still fail with a network error against the wrong origin if that fix regressed), `TestImportScriptsFetchFailureThrowsCatchably`, `TestImportScriptsUncaughtErrorPropagatesButWorkerSurvives` (the imported script's own uncaught error still reaches the worker's own top-level `execute`'s existing "a script error never propagates" catch-and-log, exactly as any other top-level worker error would), `TestDOMExceptionAvailableInWorkerForImportScripts` (the `SyntaxError` path, via a NUL byte `net/url` rejects). `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, the full existing Worker test suite unaffected by the `PageURL` base-URL fix.

## 2026-11-05 (M5, part 3 of 3 — final) — `GPUShaderModule`/`GPUComputePipeline`/`GPUBindGroup`/`GPUCommandEncoder`/`queue.submit()`: a real compute shader, dispatched end to end (feature, engine#345)

Closes out M5 (WebGPU compute via Metal/go-macos) in the go-wasmcontainers WebGPU-readiness roadmap. Wires `wgslmsl` (part 1) and the `GPUDevice`/`GPUBuffer` surface (part 2) together into the actual dispatch path, read from the W3C WebGPU specification (and its raw Bikeshed source on GitHub, once the rendered spec page's own conversion started truncating past a fixed point — confirmed directly, not assumed, when four separate fetches of the rendered page all cut off at the exact same section regardless of requested offset) before writing any code.

- **`createShaderModule`** translates the given WGSL eagerly via `wgslmsl` (this binding settles everything synchronously) but — matching spec — a translation failure does NOT reject or throw: shader module creation always succeeds with a usable (if invalid) module object, and the error surfaces via `getCompilationInfo()` or, in this binding's own simplified synchronous model (no device-timeline validation-error/device-lost event stream to report it through otherwise), a clear throw from `createComputePipeline`.
- **`createComputePipeline`** supports only `layout: "auto"` — the one layout mode every realistic simple compute shader actually needs, since `wgslmsl` already infers the group/binding → Metal-buffer-index map directly from the shader source. An explicit `GPUBindGroupLayout`/`GPUPipelineLayout` is a clear, named `TypeError`, not a silent fallback. A module declaring more than one `@compute` entry point (which makes `wgslmsl`'s own eager translation ambiguous, by design) is resolved correctly by re-translating against `compute.entryPoint`'s WGSL source name, ignoring the module's own cached (ambiguous) result in that case.
- **`pipeline.getBindGroupLayout(group)`** + **`createBindGroup`**: a `GPUBufferBinding` must cover the WHOLE bound buffer (no offset/size) — the one shape `wgslmsl`'s storage-array-only resource scope ever needs; anything else is a clear, named rejection.
- **`GPUCommandEncoder`/`GPUComputePassEncoder`**: `setPipeline`/`setBindGroup` persist across multiple `dispatchWorkgroups()` calls within one pass, exactly as spec requires. Deliberately scoped to AT MOST ONE compute pass per encoder — the realistic shape this round's own verification needs — a second `beginComputePass()` is a clear, named rejection rather than a silent no-op. Each `dispatchWorkgroups()` call fully resolves its own dispatch (every bound resource mapped to its real `*metal.Buffer`, including a freshly synthesised bounds-checking sizes buffer — see `wgslmsl`'s own security section) at the moment it is called, not deferred to `queue.submit()`, so a missing bind group fails immediately with a clear `OperationError` naming the missing `@group`/`@binding`.
- **A real, stated gap inherited from `go-macos/metal` itself, not from this binding's own choices**: `go-macos/metal.Encoder.Dispatch` takes a TOTAL thread-grid size and picks the threadgroup SHAPE itself from the hardware's own reported limits — there is no lower-level call this binding could use to force it to match the WGSL shader's own declared `@workgroup_size`. Harmless for the realistic subset `wgslmsl` supports today (a kernel keyed purely off `global_invocation_id`, where dispatch-threads semantics always gives the correct GLOBAL thread position regardless of internal grouping — confirmed correct end to end, including by this round's own real-hardware test), but `local_invocation_id`/`local_invocation_index`/`workgroup_id` — though accepted as entry-point parameters — may not reflect the actually-executed threadgroup shape when it differs from the shader's own declared size. Documented in code and here rather than silently risked.
- **`queue.submit()`** replays every recorded dispatch, across every command buffer given, through ONE real `go-macos/metal` `Device.Run` call, which blocks until the GPU has genuinely finished — which is also why `onSubmittedWorkDone()` can correctly resolve immediately: nothing can ever be "still pending" by the time a synchronous `submit()` call has returned.
- Tests (`js/webgpu_test.go`): `TestComputePipelineEndToEnd` is the roadmap's own final verification step — a real compute shader created, compiled, bound and dispatched entirely through the public `navigator.gpu`-shaped JS API a real page would use (`createShaderModule` → `createComputePipeline` → `createBuffer` → `createBindGroup` → `createCommandEncoder`/`beginComputePass`/`setPipeline`/`setBindGroup`/`dispatchWorkgroups`/`end` → `queue.submit`), output read back and checked bit-for-bit against real Metal hardware. `TestComputePipelineRejectsExplicitLayout` and `TestDispatchWithoutBindGroupRejected` cover the two scope boundaries above. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks, including the separate `bench/` module (which needed its own `go.sum` updated twice this round, caught directly by CI both times, for `js`'s new transitive dependencies on `go-macos/metal` and `go-gfx/wgsl`).
- **M5 is now complete**: `navigator.gpu` → `GPUAdapter` → `GPUDevice` → `GPUBuffer`/`GPUShaderModule`/`GPUComputePipeline`/`GPUBindGroup`/`GPUCommandEncoder`/`GPUQueue`, off by default (`Engine.EnableWebGPU`), backed by a pure-Go WGSL→MSL translator with real bounds-checking security and a pure-Go, no-cgo Metal binding — a full, if deliberately scoped-to-compute-only, WebGPU compute path with no Rust toolchain or vendored binary blob anywhere in it.

## 2026-11-05 (M5, part 2 of 3) — `navigator.gpu`/`GPUAdapter`/`GPUDevice`/`GPUBuffer` via go-macos/metal (feature, engine#345)

Second piece of M5 (WebGPU compute). Wires `go-macos/metal` — a pure-Go, no-cgo Metal binding already in this fleet, confirmed to cross-build and cross-run on every architecture this repo's CI already checks (amd64/arm64/loong64/ppc64le/riscv64/s390x), reporting `ErrUnsupported` everywhere but darwin — to the WebGPU spec's own `GPU`/`GPUAdapter`/`GPUDevice`/`GPUBuffer` interfaces, read from the W3C WebGPU specification before writing any code (`requestAdapter`'s own "never rejects, resolves null" contract; `GPUBufferUsage`/`GPUMapMode` flag values; the exact `mapAsync`/`getMappedRange`/`unmap`/`destroy` algorithm steps; the limit-default table).

- **Off by default**: `Engine.EnableWebGPU` (new field; `js.Options.EnableWebGPU`), false by default — `navigator.gpu` and the `GPUBufferUsage`/`GPUMapMode` globals are not installed at all, zero behaviour change. Raised and confirmed directly with the user before wiring this in: every other capability this engine has shipped that grants a page access to a REAL host resource (`ForceCrossOriginIsolated`'s `SharedArrayBuffer`, `OPFSRoot`'s real disk) is opt-in; arbitrary compute access to the host's real GPU the instant a page renders — with no opt-in, as a real browser does it — is a comparable capability grant (GPU compute cycles, driver-crash surface), so it gets the same treatment rather than defaulting on.
- **`requestAdapter()` genuinely never rejects**: it resolves `null` when `go-macos/metal.Default()` reports no usable GPU (`ErrNoDevice`, a VM with no Metal device) or the platform has none at all (`ErrUnsupported`, confirmed on every non-darwin build this repo's CI checks) — exactly the spec's own defined behaviour, not an approximation. `TestRequestAdapterNeverRejects` proves the resolve-never-reject contract on whichever kind of machine actually runs it, and exercises the adapter's own shape (`features`/`limits`/`info`/`requestDevice`) when one is available.
- `GPUAdapter`/`GPUDevice` are two distinct JS wrapper objects over the SAME underlying `*metal.Device` — `go-macos/metal` has no separate adapter/device split, so the one real distinction worth keeping (the spec's own "`requestDevice()` is one-time per adapter") is enforced by this binding itself (`TestRequestDeviceIsOneTimePerAdapter`: a second call rejects `OperationError`), not derived from the underlying library.
- **`GPUSupportedLimits` reports the spec's own documented DEFAULT values** (§3.6.2's "Limit default and better values" table, cited not fabricated) rather than querying Metal for the hardware's real per-limit capability — stated honestly as a limitation in a doc comment (`gpuLimitDefaults`), not silently passed off as measured. `GPUSupportedFeatures` is empty (no optional WebGPU feature implemented yet): `adapter.features.has(x)` correctly reads `false` for every `x`.
- **`GPUBuffer`**: `createBuffer`/`mapAsync`/`getMappedRange`/`unmap`/`destroy`, each validated per spec (alignment, range, mode-vs-usage — `TestGPUMapAsyncRejectsBadRange`) and implemented over the buffer's own REAL, zero-copy backing memory (`metal.Buffer.Bytes()`, unified memory on every Apple Silicon Mac): `getMappedRange` returns a real `ArrayBuffer` view over it (no upload/download, matching `go-macos/metal`'s own stated advantage), and `unmap`/`destroy` `Detach()` every such view, per spec ("all the matching `ArrayBuffer` objects are detached"). `queue.writeBuffer()` is also wired (a direct copy into that same memory, valid at any time regardless of the buffer's own map state, exactly as spec requires); `queue.onSubmittedWorkDone()` resolves immediately — vacuously correct, since this binding has no `queue.submit()` yet (M5's own next, final round) for anything to be pending on. `TestGPUBufferLifecycle` exercises the full real lifecycle — create, map-write through a real view, unmap, `queue.writeBuffer` overwrite, map-read back, destroy — against real Metal hardware when one is available, checking the exact resulting bytes.
- `GPUDevice.lost`/`destroy()`: `destroy()` resolves `lost` with `{reason: "destroyed"}`, proven by `TestGPUDeviceDestroyResolvesLost`.
- Every `*metal.Device` a render's own `requestAdapter()` calls create is tracked (`binder.gpuDevices`) and released by a new `closeGPUDevices`, called from `Session.Close`/`stopWorkers` exactly like `forgetSharedBuffers`/`stopWorkers` already do for `SharedArrayBuffer`/`Worker` — a real GPU handle must not leak across many renders in a long-lived host process.
- Tests: `js/webgpu_test.go` — disabled-by-default, the never-rejects contract, the one-time-per-adapter rule, the full buffer lifecycle, `destroy()`/`lost`, and `mapAsync`'s own range validation. Every test gracefully `t.Skip`s (not fails) when the machine running it has no usable GPU, rather than assuming one — this repo's own CI may run its `build · vet · test · coverage` job on a non-darwin or GPU-less runner. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test ./...`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` clean, cross-build verified on every GOARCH this repo's CI checks.
- Last piece of M5: `GPUShaderModule` (through `wgslmsl`), `GPUComputePipeline`/`GPUBindGroupLayout`/`GPUBindGroup`/`GPUCommandEncoder`/`GPUComputePassEncoder`, and `queue.submit()` — the actual dispatch path tying everything shipped so far together.

## 2026-11-05 (M5, part 1 of 3) — `wgslmsl`: a pure-Go WGSL→Metal Shading Language compute-shader translator (feature, engine#345)

First piece of M5 (WebGPU compute via Metal/go-macos) in the go-wasmcontainers WebGPU-readiness roadmap: the shader-translation question the original plan deliberately flagged as its one open architectural risk, resolved by a real spike before any `navigator.gpu` code was written.

**The spike, and why its own result was NOT what shipped**: compiling `naga` (the reference WGSL compiler every production WebGPU implementation uses) to `wasm32-unknown-unknown` and running it through this engine's own WebAssembly support **worked, end to end** — the compiled module instantiates with zero host imports under this engine's own wazero runtime (no WASI needed), and the MSL it emitted (after fixing a real gap: naga's default binding-map behaviour emits a placeholder `[[user(fakeN)]]` attribute Metal cannot compile, unless given an explicit per-entry-point group/binding→Metal-buffer-index map) compiled and ran correctly on real Metal hardware, verified bit-for-bit. That path was NOT shipped: it was superseded by the discovery that `go-gfx/wgsl` — a pure-Go, CTS-conformant WGSL parser/type-checker already in this fleet (confirmed zero CTS false rejects by go-wasmcontainers) — needed only a code-generation backend, not a frontend, to produce the same result with no Rust/wasm32 toolchain dependency and no vendored binary blob. The user chose this path explicitly after being shown the tradeoff.

- **New package `wgslmsl`** (`wgslmsl/`, no build tags — pure string generation, testable on every platform): `Translate(src string, opts Options) (*Result, error)` parses and type-checks src with `go-gfx/wgsl` (`wgsl.Parse`/`wgsl.Check`), consuming its `*wgsl.Info` exactly as that package's own doc comment describes a back end should, then emits a `kernel void` MSL function for the module's one `@compute` entry point.
- **Security: every array index is bounds-checked, because Metal performs none of its own.** A resource-array index is clamped against the real element count of its bound buffer at dispatch time, via a synthesised `_Sizes` struct passed as one extra kernel argument (one `uint` per resource, in Metal-buffer-index order) — `Result.SizeMetalIndex` tells the host which buffer index to fill, `Result.Bindings[i].ElemBytes` tells it how to compute each count from the real bound `GPUBuffer`'s byte length. A vector's own dynamic-index access (`v[i]`) is clamped against its statically known component count. `TestOutOfBoundsIndexIsClamped` proves this on real hardware: an index of 1,000,000 into a 4-element buffer reads/writes the buffer's own last element, not adjacent GPU memory — without this, a page's own untrusted WGSL computing an out-of-range index would be a real memory-safety bug, not a hypothetical one.
- **Deliberately bounded scope, stated honestly rather than silently gapped**: a single `@compute` entry point reaching exactly one function (no helper-function calls — `EntryPoint.Functions` from go-gfx/wgsl's own reachability analysis makes this trivial to detect and reject cleanly); resources limited to `var<storage, read|read_write>` runtime-sized arrays of scalar (`bool`/`i32`/`u32`/`f32`) or vector element type — no uniform buffers, textures, samplers, atomics, matrices or user structs yet; statements cover block/assign/if/for/while/`loop`+`continuing`+`break if`/return/break/continue/increment-decrement/call-statement (no `switch`, no `discard`); expressions cover literals (type-resolved via go-gfx/wgsl's own `Conversions`/`Types` maps, never a literal's bare source-text suffix, since the SAME unsuffixed literal can concretize to `i32`, `u32` or `f32` depending entirely on context), binary/unary/paren, vector swizzles, type constructors, and a fixed allow-list of built-in math functions mapped to their `metal_stdlib` equivalents (never an unmapped name passed through verbatim — Metal could define a same-named symbol with different semantics). Each rejected construct produces a clear, named `unsupported: …` error, never a silently wrong or partial translation — `TestUnsupportedConstructsAreRejected` locks this in for a vertex-stage entry point, a helper-function call, a uniform-buffer resource, and a `switch` statement.
- A WGSL entry point literally named `main` (legal WGSL, since `main` is not a WGSL keyword) is renamed to `main_` in the emitted MSL — `main` is Metal/C++'s own reserved name — and the same mangling rule covers any WGSL identifier colliding with an MSL/C++ keyword this package's generated code itself relies on (`device`, `constant`, `kernel`, …).
- Tests (`wgslmsl/wgslmsl_test.go`): `TestVectorAddEndToEnd` (the roadmap's own verification step — a real compute shader, translated, compiled and dispatched against real Metal hardware via `go-macos/metal`, output checked bit-for-bit), `TestOutOfBoundsIndexIsClamped` (the security proof above), `TestControlFlowAndBuiltinMath` (`loop`/`for`/`while`/`sqrt`/`min`/`max` together, dispatched and checked against an independently computed expected value), `TestUnsupportedConstructsAreRejected`, `TestNoComputeEntryPoint`, `TestMultipleComputeEntryPointsNeedASelector`. `go-macos/metal` cross-builds cleanly on every architecture this repo's CI already checks (amd64/arm64/loong64/ppc64le/riscv64/s390x) — confirmed directly, not assumed from its own doc comment — so no build tag was needed to keep the existing cross-build matrix green. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test -race ./...`/`govulncheck` all clean.
- Next in M5: wiring `go-macos/metal` to `navigator.gpu`/`GPUAdapter`/`GPUDevice`/`GPUBuffer` (darwin-real, elsewhere `undefined`), then `GPUShaderModule` (through this package) and the rest of the compute pipeline surface (`GPUComputePipeline`/`GPUBindGroupLayout`/`GPUBindGroup`/`GPUCommandEncoder`/`GPUComputePassEncoder`/`GPUQueue`), each its own round.

## 2026-11-05 (M4) — Origin Private File System: `navigator.storage.getDirectory()`, file/directory handles, `FileSystemSyncAccessHandle` (feature, engine#344)

Fourth milestone of the go-wasmcontainers WebGPU-readiness roadmap, read directly from the File System Standard (`navigator.storage.getDirectory()`, `FileSystemDirectoryHandle`, `FileSystemFileHandle`, `FileSystemSyncAccessHandle`) before writing any code. Sequenced after Workers (M2) on purpose: `createSyncAccessHandle` is spec'd `[Exposed=DedicatedWorker]` — meaningless without a real, independent event loop to block synchronously, which only a Worker has in this engine.

Off by default (`js.Options.OPFSRoot` / `engine.Engine.OPFSRoot` empty): `navigator.storage.getDirectory` is not installed at all, zero behaviour change, in particular zero real-disk writes a page's own script could ever trigger. This needed its own security pass before shipping, per the roadmap's own stated caveat — a headless engine fetching arbitrary untrusted URLs and letting their script write to a REAL directory on the host's disk is meaningfully larger attack surface than anything else this engine exposes to page content (images, fetched bytes, localStorage all stay in memory and die with the render):

- **Path-traversal immunity by construction, not denylist**: every name `getFileHandle`/`getDirectoryHandle`/`removeEntry` is given is validated BEFORE ever reaching a path join (`opfsInvalidName`, `js/opfs.go`) — empty, `.`, `..`, or containing `/`, `\`, or a NUL byte are all rejected with a `TypeError`. A validated name can never contain a path separator, so `filepath.Join(parent, name)` can never leave `parent` — this is structural, not a list of attack strings to keep in sync. `TestOPFSInvalidNamesRejected` proves both the rejection AND, directly on disk, that a `"../escaped"` attempt created nothing outside the origin's own directory.
- **Origin isolation**: each origin (`scheme://host[:port]`) gets its own subdirectory under `OPFSRoot`, named by a SHA-256 hash of the origin string rather than the origin text itself, so two different pages fetched by the same `Engine` can never read or write each other's tree — confirmed by `TestOPFSOriginIsolation` (a file written under one origin is `NotFoundError`, not silently visible, from another).
- **A hard per-origin byte quota** (`Engine.MaxOPFSBytes`, the same zero-selects-default/negative-disables convention as `MaxWasmMemoryBytes`): tracked by walking an origin's real on-disk usage once, lazily (a durable `OPFSRoot` may already hold bytes from a previous process), then updated incrementally under one lock per write/truncate/delete, closing the TOCTOU window a separate check-then-write would leave between two goroutines sharing one origin directory. A write/truncate that would exceed it throws `QuotaExceededError` and makes NO partial change. `TestOPFSSyncAccessHandleQuotaExceeded` confirms both the throw and that the file's size is unchanged.
- **`createSyncAccessHandle`'s own exclusive lock** (package-level registry keyed by real file path, `js/opfs.go`): a second attempt on the same file while one handle is open rejects with `NoModificationAllowedError`; closing the first lets a later attempt succeed (`TestOPFSSyncAccessHandleExclusiveLock`).
- `read`/`write`/`truncate`/`getSize`/`flush`/`close` map directly onto `*os.File`'s `ReadAt`/`WriteAt`/`Truncate`/`Stat().Size()`/`Sync()`/`Close()`, operating on the REAL zero-copy backing slice of whatever `ArrayBuffer` or typed-array/`DataView` view a script passes (`bufferSourceBytes`, reusing `clone.go`'s own typed-array/DataView kind table) — a worker's write through the handle is visible through the script's own buffer with no intermediate copy. A write past EOF relies on the OS's own sparse-file behaviour rather than manually zero-filling, matching the spec's "pad with NUL bytes" note.
- A real, confirmed bug found getting there (not hypothetical): a Worker's own binder never calls `install()` (no DOM/BOM tree makes sense in a Worker), so `DOMException` — a general ECMAScript exception type the spec exposes in EVERY script context, not a DOM-specific interface — did not exist there at all; every OPFS error from within a Worker surfaced as a generic `TypeError` instead of its real name (`NotFoundError`, `QuotaExceededError`, …), caught live by `TestOPFSSyncAccessHandleExclusiveLock`/`TestOPFSSyncAccessHandleQuotaExceeded` failing on first run. Fixed with a new, minimal `installDOMExceptionOnly` (`js/interfaces.go`) a Worker's own global scope now calls.
- `getFileHandle`/`getDirectoryHandle`/`removeEntry` are NOT Worker-only (only `createSyncAccessHandle` is) and work from the main document too; a handle obtained there simply has no `createSyncAccessHandle` property at all (`typeof fh.createSyncAccessHandle === "undefined"`), matching a real browser's `[Exposed=DedicatedWorker]` restriction exactly rather than a bespoke runtime error (`TestOPFSSyncAccessHandleRequiresWorker`).
- Tests: `js/opfs_test.go` — disabled-by-default, the full getFileHandle/getDirectoryHandle/removeEntry round trip (including `TypeMismatchError` both ways, `NotFoundError`, `InvalidModificationError` on a non-empty directory without `recursive`), path-traversal immunity (including the on-disk negative check), origin isolation, the Worker-only gate, the real read/write/truncate/flush/close round trip, the exclusive lock, and the quota. `go build`/`go vet`/`go test -race ./...` green, including the `webengine_nojs`/`webengine_nonet` build-tag variants.

## 2026-11-04 — `SharedArrayBuffer` reports its own identity (`instanceof`, `Object.prototype.toString`), not plain `ArrayBuffer`'s (fix, engine#343 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers confirmed `instanceof SharedArrayBuffer` matters for real: their own e2e probe (and code in the wild) branches on it to choose a shared-memory path over a copy path. M3's own stated simplification — the wrapper's prototype chain was plain `ArrayBuffer`, so `instanceof SharedArrayBuffer` read `false` — meant such code would take the WRONG branch even though the underlying memory genuinely is shared.

- **`sharedArrayBufferPrototype`** (new, `js/atomics.go`): one `SharedArrayBuffer.prototype` object per VM (binder-cached, lazy), itself prototyped on the real `ArrayBuffer.prototype` (inheriting `slice`/`byteLength`/every other real behaviour for free, since the wrapper genuinely is a goja ArrayBuffer under the hood) with `Symbol.toStringTag` overridden to `"SharedArrayBuffer"`. A real, confirmed gotcha found getting there: `Object.SetSymbol` performs ordinary `[[Set]]` semantics, which correctly REFUSES to shadow `ArrayBuffer.prototype`'s own non-writable `toStringTag` — the same "can't shadow a non-writable inherited data property via plain assignment" rule real JS itself follows. `DefineDataPropertySymbol` (`[[DefineOwnProperty]]` semantics) is what actually works; confirmed via a standalone probe against the pinned goja build before touching the real code.
- **`wrapSharedArrayBuffer`** (new, `js/atomics.go`): the one path every SharedArrayBuffer wrapper object now goes through — `new SharedArrayBuffer(n)` AND a postMessage'd buffer/view reconstructed into a receiving VM (`js/clone.go`, both `clonedSharedBuffer` and `clonedSharedView` cases now call it instead of duplicating the wrap-and-register logic) — so the identity fix applies on the RECEIVING side of a `postMessage`, not just the creating side; that's the exact shape of go-wasmcontainers' own failing probe case (`d instanceof SharedArrayBuffer` checked on the value a worker just received).
- `SharedArrayBuffer`'s own `.constructor`/`.name`/`.prototype` are wired so `x.constructor === SharedArrayBuffer`, `x.constructor.name === "SharedArrayBuffer"` (not the wrapped Go closure's own derived name — another thing confirmed, not assumed, by direct testing), and `Object.prototype.toString.call(x) === "[object SharedArrayBuffer]"` all hold.
- **Stated, still-remaining simplification**: `x instanceof ArrayBuffer` is also `true` (the prototype chain includes real `ArrayBuffer.prototype`), where the spec's two classes are siblings, not related by inheritance. No confirmed need to separate them: the common `if (x instanceof SharedArrayBuffer) … else if (x instanceof ArrayBuffer) …` idiom (go-wasmcontainers' own probe included) already takes the correct branch regardless, since `instanceof SharedArrayBuffer` is checked first.
- Tests: `js/atomics_test.go`'s new `TestSharedArrayBufferHasItsOwnIdentity` covers `instanceof`, `Object.prototype.toString`, `.constructor`/`.constructor.name`, `ArrayBuffer.isView`, and the peer's own "branch on instanceof to pick a path" idiom directly. `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerReceivedSharedArrayBufferReportsCorrectIdentity` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' EXACT failing probe script byte-for-byte and confirms it now reports "shared" (not "copied") and the write is visible back on main. All green under `go test -race`.

## 2026-11-04 — a one-shot `setTimeout`'s first firing now honours real wall-clock delay under `EnableWorkerStreaming` (fix, engine#342 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers' own e2e probe still reported "only 1/100 messages in 15 s" even after the `setInterval` fix above. Their own guess (a worker-timer-draining bug) didn't hold up — reproducing their exact "streaming started from onmessage" shape worked correctly. Root-caused by reproducing their EXACT probe scripts directly (cloned `go-wasmcontainers/gpu` PR #43, ran their real e2e test, then isolated the two relevant scripts into a standalone Go program against this engine): the render completed in **3.68ms**, not the 15 real seconds their own JS-side safety-net `setTimeout(giveUp, 15000)` expected. The earlier `setInterval` fix only gated a REPEATING interval's own re-firings by real wall-clock time; a plain ONE-SHOT `setTimeout`'s first (and only) firing still fired at "the next opportunity" — this package's own long-documented, deliberate "not a real clock" model, unchanged since before `EnableWorkerStreaming` existed. Their safety-net timer — the "race a near-immediate retry against a much-later give-up timeout" idiom `drainTimers`' own doc comment already describes — therefore fired almost instantly, terminating every worker and resolving the promise before more than one streamed message could arrive.

- **`js.Options.RealTimeTimers`** (new field, mapped from `Engine.EnableWorkerStreaming` in `jsOptions`): gates a timer job's FIRST firing by a real `readyAt` (`time.Now()+delay`), the same mechanism the earlier fix already gives a repeating interval's own re-firings — but now applied at the moment ANY job (one-shot or repeating) is first queued, not just on an interval's own re-queue. Off by default: an ordinary bounded, one-shot render keeps today's exact "fire at the next opportunity" behaviour unchanged, since no page can observe the difference within one settle pass anyway. Workers inherit it automatically (the same `Options` their own binder is built from).
- Tests: `enginejs_test.go`'s new `TestRealTimeTimersHonorSafetyNetDelay` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' own exact scenario — a worker streaming 20 messages over ~100ms of real time, racing a 2-second one-shot safety-net `setTimeout` — and asserts the stream finishes first, the safety net never fires, and the whole render completes in well under the safety net's own delay. `go test -race ./...` green throughout; the full suite's own wall-clock cost rose modestly (the streaming tests now genuinely wait their requested real delays instead of bursting instantly), an expected, correct trade-off, not a regression.

## 2026-11-04 (M3 completeness) — real COOP/COEP gating, and sharing a typed-array view (not just the raw buffer) via `postMessage` (feature + fix, engine#343 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers' own real ring buffer uses three things M3 didn't yet cover, found by asking them directly rather than guessing: non-`Int32Array` views (`Uint8Array`, `DataView`) over the same `SharedArrayBuffer`, posting the VIEW itself (not the raw buffer) through `postMessage`, and `crossOriginIsolated` matching what their pages actually check before using SAB at all.

- **Real COOP/COEP gating, not a process-model shortcut.** An earlier draft of this round reported `crossOriginIsolated` unconditionally `true`, reasoning that this engine's one-render-one-process model made the cross-origin-Spectre threat moot. go-wasmcontainers corrected this, with two arguments that hold: (1) **test parity** — this engine runs as a lane beside real Chromium/Firefox/Safari; a page missing COOP/COEP must see SAB denied here exactly as there, or the lane is a false green, the worst outcome for a test lane; (2) **the threat model is real anyway** — a render can load cross-origin subresources (scripts, images, fetch) into the SAME runtime, so the Spectre-adjacent risk COOP/COEP guards against isn't actually eliminated by "one process per render." Implemented the spec rule directly: `net_http.go`'s new `crossOriginIsolatedFromHeaders` checks the real response's `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy: same-origin` + `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy: require-corp` (or `credentialless`), case-insensitively; `Document.CrossOriginIsolated` carries it from `Fetch` through to `js.Options`; `SharedArrayBuffer`/`Atomics`/`window.crossOriginIsolated` are only installed/true when it holds — a plain `Document` built any other way (tests, a `Fetcher` with no headers) defaults to `false` unless the new `Engine.ForceCrossOriginIsolated` escape hatch (off by default, exactly as requested) opts in. Workers inherit the creating binder's own resolved value, per spec.
- **Sharing a typed-array/DataView VIEW via `postMessage`, not just the raw `SharedArrayBuffer`** (`js/clone.go`): a real, confirmed gap — their ring posts the view itself. Detected by constructor name FIRST (checked against a full 11-kind table, not just Atomics-eligible ones), deliberately not by probing for a `.buffer` property (a coincidental plain object with a property literally named `buffer` would false-positive) nor by `Export()` type (which cannot tell a `DataView` from a plain object at all). A view over a registered `SharedArrayBuffer` clones as `(sb, kind, byteOffset, byteLength)`; reconstruction rebuilds the exact view kind over a freshly-wrapped buffer in the receiving VM — a `DataView` takes byte length directly, every `TypedArray` kind converts byte length to element count by its own element size first (two genuinely different constructor shapes, both now tested).
