- Tests: `fetch_test.go`'s new `TestCrossOriginIsolatedFromHeaders` (missing/wrong/case-varied headers) and `TestFetchCrossOriginIsolatedEndToEnd` (a REAL httptest response with and without the headers, through `Fetch`→`RenderDocument`, asserting `window.crossOriginIsolated` and `typeof SharedArrayBuffer` on the actual rendered page). `js/atomics_test.go`'s new `TestSharedArrayBufferAndAtomicsGatedOnCrossOriginIsolation` locks in the gate itself. `js/clone_test.go`'s new `TestCloneRoundTripsSharedTypedArrayView`/`TestCloneRoundTripsSharedDataView` prove both reconstruction shapes genuinely share memory (write through the reconstructed view, read the ORIGINAL `[]byte` directly). `js/worker_test.go`'s new `TestWorkerPostMessageSharesTypedArrayView` is the full real round trip: a `Uint8Array` posted directly to a Worker, written through there, read back via a `DataView` over the identical buffer (go-wasmcontainers' own item 1, cross-view consistency) AND observed back on main once the blocking `postMessage` call returns (item 2, genuine sharing). One provably-dead branch (an "unknown kind" check in `reconstructValue` that can never see a kind `cloneAt` didn't already validate against the identical table) removed rather than fake-tested, matching this session's own established discipline. All green under `go test -race`, and under every build-tag combination (`webengine_nonet`, `webengine_nojs`).
- Not yet done, explicitly deferred: `WebAssembly.Memory({shared:true})` — go-wasmcontainers' own lower-priority item 3; needs a wazero feasibility check before committing to it.

## 2026-11-04 — `setInterval` genuinely repeats; `clearTimeout`/`clearInterval` actually cancel (fix, engine#342/#343 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers reported v0.27.1 (`EnableWorkerStreaming`) only ever delivering "1/100" streamed Worker messages to their page, guessing the live loop didn't drain each worker's own timer queue. Checked before accepting the guess: I reproduced their exact "worker streams, started from onmessage" shape directly, and it worked correctly end to end — the real cause was elsewhere. Root-caused and reproduced precisely: this engine's `setInterval` has always fired only ONCE (`clearTimeout`/`clearInterval` were hardcoded no-ops) — a simplification from long before `EnableWorkerStreaming` existed, since nothing ran long enough for repeating to matter before. Their page uses `setInterval` to periodically poll "has everything arrived yet"; that poll ran exactly once, very early, and reported a stale snapshot (reproduced directly: `data-checks="1" data-received-at-check="1"`, matching their report exactly) — not evidence that messages stopped arriving.

- **`setInterval` now genuinely repeats** (`js/js.go`): a new `timerJob.repeat`/`id`/`readyAt` make `drainTimers` re-queue a fired interval job (same id) gated by a REAL wall-clock `readyAt` (`time.Now()+delay`) — the one deliberate exception to this package's own long-documented "not a real clock" timer model (every one-shot job's first — and only — firing is unchanged). Without this gating, a repeating interval would burst-fire thousands of times in a single drain call instead of at its own requested cadence.
- **`clearTimeout`/`clearInterval` now genuinely cancel by id** (`js/window.go`'s new `cancelTimer`): removes a still-pending job, and records the id so an interval currently mid-callback (already removed from the queue, about to re-queue itself) honours a `clearInterval` the callback calls on its own id — the standard "run N times, then stop yourself" idiom.
- Tests: `js/js_test.go`'s existing `TestTimers` had its own `clearTimeout(1)`/`clearInterval(2)` calls fixed (they assumed permanent no-ops and, now that ids are real, happened to collide with and cancel the very jobs the test wanted to observe — a real but expected test-expectation update, not a product bug). New `TestClearTimeoutClearIntervalActuallyCancel` and `TestSetIntervalCancelledFromWithinItsOwnCallback` lock in the real cancellation behaviour. `enginejs_test.go`'s new `TestSetIntervalPollsRepeatedlyWithWorkerStreaming` reproduces go-wasmcontainers' own exact scenario (a streaming Worker, a `setInterval`-based completion poll) and confirms it now observes the true, final count rather than a stale one. All green under `go test -race`.
- Not addressed here, noted for later: a sufficiently long-lived page with a fast, never-cleared `setInterval` will eventually hit `maxTimerJobs` (2000, now logged once rather than silent — see the earlier cap-logging fix above) sooner than before, since a genuinely-repeating interval contributes many more job runs over a long render than its old "fires once" self ever could. Whether the cap itself should become a rate/backlog bound instead of a lifetime total remains the deliberately separate, undecided question flagged in that earlier entry.

## 2026-11-04 (M3) — `SharedArrayBuffer` + `Atomics`: cross-VM shared memory and a real wait/notify handshake (feature, engine#343)

Fourth milestone of the go-wasmcontainers WebGPU-readiness roadmap. The confirmed hot path: "a SharedArrayBuffer ring with Atomics.wait in the worker and Atomics.notify from main."

- **`SharedArrayBuffer`** (new `js/atomics.go`): reuses goja's own real, zero-copy-Go-`[]byte`-backed `ArrayBuffer` (`Runtime.NewArrayBuffer` — the same mechanism `wasm.go`'s `wrapWasmMemory` already relies on) for every byte-level operation; the only new piece is registering its identity in a package-level `sharedBufferRegistry` so `Atomics.*` and a cross-VM `postMessage` (`clone.go`) recognize it. Stated, not hidden, simplification: the result's prototype chain is plain `ArrayBuffer`, so `instanceof SharedArrayBuffer` reports false — no confirmed need for that distinction; the shared bytes and Atomics eligibility are what's real. Bounded by a fixed `maxSharedBufferBytes` (64 MiB, not yet an `Engine` field — open an issue if a workload needs more).
- **`Atomics`**: `load`/`store`/`add`/`compareExchange` across the plain 8/16/32-bit integer TypedArray kinds, `wait`/`notify` restricted to `Int32Array` (spec also allows `BigInt64Array`; out of scope — no confirmed 64-bit-counter need yet), `isLockFree` always `true`. Deliberately restricted to typed-array views over a REGISTERED `SharedArrayBuffer` — a plain `ArrayBuffer` throws a named `TypeError`, since the one confirmed need for Atomics at all is cross-VM shared memory. `wait` genuinely blocks the calling goroutine (a Worker's own, by construction) via a per-buffer `sync.Mutex` + waiter-channel registry; `notify` wakes up to `count` waiters, returning how many.
- **A real, confirmed, pre-existing bug found and fixed while wiring this through** (`js/clone.go`): `obj.ClassName()` reports `"Object"` — not a distinct name — for `ArrayBuffer`, `Map`, `Set`, and EVERY TypedArray kind in this pinned goja build (confirmed directly via a small Go probe against the actual build). Before this round's fix, each of those silently cloned as an empty plain object via the `Keys()`-based path instead of this file's own documented explicit rejection — losing the value's content with no error at all. `cloneAt`'s `"Object"` case now branches on `Export()`'s own per-type Go wrapper (`goja.ArrayBuffer`, `[][2]interface{}` for Map, `map[string]interface{}` for a genuine plain object, anything else rejected) before trusting `ClassName()` for anything but `Array`/`Date`/`RegExp`.
- **A second real bug found via the SAME path, in `worker.go`**: `deliver` used to drain the worker's own queued timers synchronously, inline, right after dispatching a message — meaning a handler that deferred a blocking `Atomics.wait` via `setTimeout(fn, 0)` specifically to avoid holding the sender's blocking `postMessage` round trip hostage would have that very escape inlined right back into the same call stack, deadlocking the first real `wait`/`notify` pair this round tried to exercise. Fixed by removing that inline drain entirely — a worker's own queued timers, from ANY source, now only ever drain via the real-cadence ticker (`run`'s own `select` loop), never synchronously inside message delivery.
- Tests: `js/atomics_test.go` covers `load`/`store`/`add`/`compareExchange` directly, a plain `ArrayBuffer` throwing the named `TypeError`, a mismatched `wait` returning `"not-equal"` immediately (no blocking), and a real timeout. `js/clone_test.go`'s new `TestCloneRejectsMapSetAndTypedArray` locks in the `ClassName()`/`Export()` fix. `enginejs_test.go`'s `TestAtomicsWaitWokenByNotifyAcrossWorkers` is the full, real, two-goroutine integration proof: a `SharedArrayBuffer` created on main, shared with a Worker via `postMessage`, a genuine cross-goroutine `wait`/`notify` handshake (deterministic via a "I'm about to wait" message rather than a timing guess — this package's own timer queue has no real wall-clock pacing within one drain burst, so a `setTimeout`-based delay cannot approximate "the worker should be waiting by now" reliably), and the woken wait observing the new value through the SAME underlying memory. `TestAtomicsWaitAcrossWorkersTimesOut` is the bounded-timeout control. All green under `go test -race`.

## 2026-11-04 — maxScripts/maxTimerJobs caps now log once when they actually stop work (fix, engine#342 follow-up)

go-wasmcontainers flagged, directly and correctly, that `EnableWorkerStreaming` (above) makes hitting `maxTimerJobs` (2000) a REACHABLE outcome for a legitimately long-running render, not just a pathological one — their own worker loop plus a page's own UI timers can exceed it within a minute at a ~15ms cadence — and that every bounded cap in this package (`maxScripts`, `maxTimerJobs`) stopped work completely silently. Fixed the minimum they asked for: both caps now log once, via the existing `JSLog` sink, the first time they actually stop further scripts/timers — never a silent drain stop, never spammed on every subsequent check for the rest of a render that might run for minutes. Whether the cap MODEL itself (a fixed per-render total vs. a rate/backlog bound, since `JSTimeout` already backstops total wall-clock cost) should change is a separate, bigger question, not decided here. New `binder.scriptsCapWarned`/`jobsCapWarned` (js.go); `TestMaxScripts`/`TestMaxTimerJobs` (js_test.go) extended to assert the log fires exactly once.

## 2026-11-04 — `Engine.EnableWorkerStreaming`: a live event-loop mode so unsolicited Worker messages are actually seen (feature, engine#342 follow-up)

Shipped same-day as M2 (below), ahead of M3, after go-wasmcontainers flagged — with precise detail, not a vague complaint — that M2 alone could not support their real usage: a Worker running for a container's whole lifetime, posting UNSOLICITED stdout/stderr/exit-status/GPU-frame messages at any time, not replying within a specific `postMessage` call. Checked directly rather than assumed: the settle loop is fixpoint-bounded (`maxSettlePasses=3`, `dynamic.go:26`) and breaks the instant a pass produces no DOM change — which is exactly what happens the moment a page's main script merely registers `onmessage` and returns. M6's `EnableDoneSignal` only lets that loop end EARLY; it never extends it. Confirmed: a streaming Worker's messages posted after that point were simply never looked at again.

- **`Engine.EnableWorkerStreaming bool`** (new field, `engine.go`): off by default, zero behaviour change (not even the extra real-time polling cost). On, `settle()` (`settle_scripts.go`) calls a new `liveLoop` after the bounded pass loop reaches its own fixpoint: on a real ~15ms cadence (`liveLoopPollInterval`), it drains timers/Worker inboxes (`sess.RunPending()`) and re-checks layout (`relayoutPass` — cheap when nothing changed, a DOM-signature compare short-circuits before any real work), until the page signals `__webengineDone` (new `Session.Expired()` and the existing `Session.Done()`) or the session's own `JSTimeout` deadman elapses — always bounded, never open-ended, even if a caller turns this on without ever firing the done signal.
- **A worker needed the same real pacing internally, not just main** (`js/worker.go`): `jsWorker.run`'s initial synchronous block used to drain its OWN top-level timers unpaced, right before signalling ready — a self-rescheduling `setTimeout` chain (the exact shape of an autonomous streaming loop) would burn its WHOLE run in one synchronous burst before the constructor even returned, let alone before the calling script's next line (`w.onmessage = fn`) registered a listener. Fixed by removing that premature drain entirely; a new `time.Ticker` (`workerTickInterval`, same 15ms cadence) inside `run`'s own select loop paces timer draining in real time instead, so a streaming chain behaves like genuine streaming rather than an instant, unobserved burst.
- **A second instance of the same bug, found by writing the test, not just reasoning about it**: `newWorker`'s own constructor drained the worker's `mainInbox` immediately after the worker signalled ready — losing any message a worker's top-level script posts synchronously before construction even returns (an everyday "I'm ready" handshake idiom, not just the streaming case). Fixed the same way M2's own fetch-failure dispatch already was: queued via the existing job mechanism so the REST of the calling script (including `w.onmessage = fn`) runs first.
- Tests (`enginejs_test.go`): `TestEnableWorkerStreamingDeliversUnsolicitedMessages` — the peer's own named probe shape, reproduced directly: a Worker streams 100 unsolicited messages via a self-rescheduling `setTimeout` chain while main runs its OWN independent timer chain concurrently; asserts all 100 are observed AND main's own timers were not starved. `TestEnableWorkerStreamingOffLeavesStreamingWorkerUnseen` is the control: the identical page, feature left off, does NOT observe the full stream — proving the mode is additive, not a behaviour change for every existing caller. Both tests caught the two draining bugs above before they shipped (the first version of this round failed both new tests against the peer's own scenario until fixed). `go test -race ./...` green throughout.

## 2026-11-04 (M2) — `new Worker(scriptURL)`: dedicated workers via a second goja runtime, cross-VM `postMessage` (feature, engine#342)

Third milestone of the go-wasmcontainers WebGPU-readiness roadmap (M1 engine#341, M6 engine#346, both above). goja's own documented contract is one `Runtime` per goroutine, with no passing object values between runtimes — so a Worker is architecturally a second, independent `binder`+`goja.Runtime` on its own goroutine, not a second thread sharing the first.

- **Cross-VM structured clone** (new `js/clone.go`): `cloneValue`/`reconstructValue` walk a value into (and back out of) a VM-agnostic intermediate form (`clonedValue`/`clonedArray`/`clonedObject`), supporting the "basic" subset this round needs — undefined, null (kept distinct from undefined), booleans, numbers, strings, plain Array, plain Object. A function, Symbol, or an unsupported object class (Date, RegExp, Map, Set, ArrayBuffer/TypedArray) errors explicitly, matching the real algorithm's own `DataCloneError` contract rather than silently losing the value or misrepresenting it as `{}`. Bounded by `cloneMaxDepth`(64)/`cloneMaxValues`(20000), the same "always bounded" convention as `maxCloneNodes`/`maxRequests` — a cycle is caught by the value-count bound rather than a separate visited-set, since the format has no cross-clone identity to preserve anyway. Note: `structuredClone` itself (`js/window.go`) remains today's no-op — this is a new primitive built for `postMessage`, not an extension of existing clone logic (there wasn't any to extend).
- **`new Worker(scriptURL)`** (new `js/worker.go`, wired into `install()`): fetches the script synchronously (reusing `fetchScript` exactly as external `<script src>` does — a fetch failure queues an "error" dispatch on the wrapper, mirroring `runScripts`' own failed-external-script precedent, so the calling script's very next line, almost always `w.onerror = fn`, still gets to register first). The worker's own top-level script runs to completion (bounded by the render's shared deadman budget) before the constructor returns, on a dedicated goroutine+binder built by `newWorkerBinder` — no DOM, no document, just the worker's own cut-down global scope (`console`, timers, `postMessage`, `onmessage`/`addEventListener`, `close()`), reusing the SAME sentinel-node event-dispatch machinery (`addListener`/`dispatch`) `windowNode`/`docNode` already prove is generic.
- **The `postMessage` round trip is deliberately synchronous/blocking**, not truly async: this package has no background event loop to deliver an async reply into (the main binder's own timers/microtasks only drain when the engine's settle loop or `RunPending` calls in), so `Worker.postMessage` clones on the caller's own VM, hands off via a bounded channel, and blocks until the worker has fully processed that message (bounded by the remaining deadman budget), then delivers whatever the worker replied with in that same call — a deterministic, testable round trip without inventing a second clock. A worker's own messages posted outside that window (e.g. during its top-level script) are still delivered, best-effort, the next time `drainTimers` runs (a new `drainWorkerInboxes` call there, a true no-op when no page ever creates a Worker).
- `w.terminate()`, `self.close()`, and the session itself closing (`Session.Close`'s new `stopWorkers`) all stop the worker's goroutine — no render can leak one past its own session's lifetime.
- Tests: `js/clone_test.go` round-trips primitives/arrays/nested objects across two independent `goja.Runtime`s (undefined vs. null preserved), and proves a function, a `Date`, an over-deep object, and an over-wide object each error rather than silently misclone. `js/worker_test.go`'s `TestWorkerPostMessageRoundTrip` is the issue's own verification shape — two independent binders on two goroutines, a round trip observed via `console.log` on both sides — plus fetch-failure error dispatch, an unclonable value logged (and not crashing) from EITHER side, `addEventListener`/`removeEventListener` on both the main wrapper and the worker's own self scope, a worker closing itself mid-handler, and `Session.Close` stopping a still-live worker. All green under `go test -race`. One provably-dead branch (a `w.inbox`-closed receive case nothing ever triggers, since nothing ever closes that channel) was removed rather than fake-tested, matching `Session.Done`'s own earlier fix (M6, above).

## 2026-11-03 (M6) — `Engine.EnableDoneSignal`: a page can end the settle loop early by posting "done" (feature, engine#346)

Second milestone of the go-wasmcontainers WebGPU-readiness roadmap (first was M1, engine#341, above), requested exactly as the peer phrased it: "Fetch + RenderDocument + JSLog + the DOM... provided a render can wait for the page to post 'done', meaning async completion with a timeout." No WebDriver/CDP needed, and no new goroutine or protocol — this rides entirely on the existing settle loop and `JSTimeout`/deadman budget.

- **`Engine.EnableDoneSignal bool`** (new field, `engine.go`): false (the default) installs nothing at all — not even the global's name exists — so a caller not using this feature sees no behaviour change. True installs `__webengineDone(value)` as a global (`js/window.go`'s `install()`), which a page's own script calls once its async work is actually finished; the FIRST call is the one that counts (subsequent calls are no-ops).
- **The settle loop itself** (`settle_scripts.go`'s `settle()`): after each pass's `RunPending()`, a new `if _, ok := sess.Done(); ok { break }` ends the loop immediately once the signal has fired, instead of running out `maxSettlePasses`/`JSTimeout` — exactly the "post done" half of the peer's own ask (the timeout half, `JSTimeout`, already existed and is unchanged either way).
- **`Session.Done()`** (new method, `js/session.go`): reads the signal back, converting the page's own argument via goja's `Export()` the same way `Eval`'s own return value is.
- Tests: `enginejs_test.go`'s new `TestEnableDoneSignalStopsSettleEarly` reuses `TestSettleFixpointCap`'s own self-replicating script chain (dynamic_test.go) — proven to reach exactly 4 generations when run to completion — and has the chain call `__webengineDone()` on generation 2; with the option on, the loop stops right there (`data-d="2"`); with it left at its default, the identical page still reaches `data-d="4"`, proving the feature is a true no-op for a caller not using it. `js/js_test.go`'s new `TestSessionDoneSignal` covers `Session.Done()` directly: a page that calls it with a value, one that never calls it, one that calls it with no argument at all (goja's own `Undefined()`, not Go `nil`, exercising a real branch rather than a defensive one), and — with the option left off — the same call throwing `ReferenceError`, matching every other opt-in stub's "absent, not a no-op" contract.

## 2026-11-03 — WebAssembly at real sizes: configurable module/memory quotas, `instantiateStreaming`/`compileStreaming` (feature, engine#341)

Requested directly, not corpus-found: go-wasmcontainers asked whether go-webengine could validate their WebGPU stack's own WebAssembly layer — their own Go-compiled modules reach several hundred MiB, toward the wasm32 4 GiB address-space ceiling, which this engine's own hardcoded 32 MiB module cap and 16 MiB linear-memory cap (round 189) could never accommodate. First milestone of a longer roadmap (Dedicated Workers, SharedArrayBuffer/Atomics, OPFS, and WebGPU compute are each their own planned, larger future round — see the roadmap plan for the full sequencing and the one genuinely open architectural question it flags, WGSL shader translation).

- **`MaxWasmModuleBytes`/`MaxWasmMemoryBytes`** (new `Engine` fields, `engine.go`): the compile-input-size and linear-memory-growth caps are now configurable, following this engine's own established convention exactly (`MaxJSHeapBytes`'s own "zero selects a sane default" shape) — zero keeps today's unchanged behaviour (32 MiB / 16 MiB), any higher value raises the ceiling toward real module sizes. A byte value is rounded UP to WebAssembly's own 64 KiB page size and clamped to the wasm32 ceiling (65536 pages = 4 GiB) — wazero's own `WithMemoryLimitPages` panics past that value, so the clamp is load-bearing, not cosmetic. There is deliberately no "disable" value for either cap (unlike `MaxJSHeapBytes`'s own negative-disables option): a module always has SOME finite bound, matching wazero's own API, which has no unlimited-size mode either.
- **`WebAssembly.compileStreaming`/`instantiateStreaming`** (`js/wasm.go`): did not exist at all before this round. Not genuine incremental streaming — this engine's own `fetch()` already fully buffers a response before resolving it (no `.body` ReadableStream exists anywhere in `js/net.go`), and wazero's own `CompileModule([]byte)` takes a complete byte slice regardless — but the JS-facing contract (Promise-based, accepting a `Response` or a promise of one, the overwhelmingly common real shape `instantiateStreaming(fetch(url))`) is satisfied exactly. Resolving the `Response` argument reuses a new small primitive, `awaitSettled`, which reads goja's own `Promise.State()`/`Result()` directly rather than driving real job-queue continuations — correct because every promise this engine's own code produces (including `Response.arrayBuffer()`) is already synchronously settled by construction (the real I/O already happened inline before `resolve()` was ever called), the same property `fetch()`'s own doc comment already establishes.
- Tests: `js/wasm_test.go`'s new tables cover the byte-to-page rounding/clamping math directly (`TestMaxWasmMemoryPagesResolution`), the quota actually being consulted end-to-end (`TestWasmModuleSizeQuotaEnforcedEndToEnd`), the real streaming round trip against a genuine `fetch()`-returned `Response` (`TestInstantiateStreamingAndCompileStreaming`, a new `/add.wasm` case on the shared `netServer()` test fixture), and every error shape the Response-argument resolution can hit — a rejected promise, a promise resolving to a non-object, a direct (non-promise) object with no `arrayBuffer()` method, and `arrayBuffer()` itself throwing or returning a rejected promise (`TestStreamingWasmBytesRejectsVariousShapes`) — each correctly rejecting with a `TypeError`, never panicking.

## 2026-11-02 (round 201) — `window.postMessage` did not exist as a global at all — a real ReferenceError via Google's own widely-deployed reCAPTCHA script (defect, engine#340)

Continuing the JSLog-sweep method (rounds 199-200) in parallel with a dedicated investigation of round 200's own flagged `<canvas>.getContext()` lead (stripe.com) — correctly declined this round: `grep -rn "getContext|CanvasRenderingContext|canvas" js/` found zero rasterization machinery anywhere to build a narrow stub on (unlike round 194's `clip-path`, which reused existing mask/group-buffer paint infrastructure), the real-world usage pattern (a "squeezy-carousel" canvas effect) strongly suggests per-frame pixel manipulation needing genuine 2D rasterization semantics from scratch, and — a separate, flagged puzzle for a future round — rendering stripe.com through this engine's own pipeline produced ZERO JSLog lines at all (none of its 85 `<script>` tags executed), blocking confirmation of the actual call pattern before any fix attempt.

- **Confirmed real trigger**: Google's own `gstatic.com/recaptcha/.../recaptcha__en.js` — one of the most widely-deployed third-party scripts on the real web — calls bare `postMessage(...)` unconditionally at load. Reproduced live on spotify.com: `ReferenceError: postMessage is not defined`, the same whole-script-abort shape round 50's missing `customElements` had.
- **Confirmed absent, not merely overlooked**: `grep -rn postMessage js/` found `postMessage` existing ONLY as a method on `MessagePort` objects (`MessageChannel`, `net.go`'s `wirePort` — which already delivers for real, via the bounded event-loop job queue) — there was no bare GLOBAL `window.postMessage` at all.
- **Fix, matching rounds 199/200's own size class**: a single no-op entry in `js/window.go`'s existing `installStubs` bucket (the established home for best-effort/state-only window-level functions — `scrollTo`, `alert`, `open`, `window.location.assign()`'s own precedent). HTML Standard §9.4.3's `postMessage` queues a task firing a `message` event on a target window; this engine's single-document model has no real second window/frame to deliver to (`window`/`self`/`top`/`parent` already alias the one global object), so — same precedent as `location.assign()`/`.reload()` not performing a real navigation — this stops the crash without simulating delivery no confirmed trigger needs.
- Tests: `js/js_test.go`'s new `TestWindowPostMessageDoesNotThrow` (the real trigger's own call shape, both 2-arg and object-options forms) and `TestWindowStubs` extended to cover it alongside the other no-op globals. Verified end to end against the real live trigger (spotify.com, re-rendered through this engine's own pipeline before/after): the `ReferenceError` line disappears from `JSLog`.

## 2026-11-01 (round 200) — `document.referrer` did not exist at all — a real TypeError via Fathom Analytics' own widely-served script, confirmed on two independent sites (defect, engine#339)

Bibliography first: HTML Standard §7.8.1 (`Document.referrer` returns the referring document's URL, or the empty string when there is none — not undefined). Method shift that paid off twice running now: instead of cold-grepping CSS/JS feature names across sites, render real live pages through this engine's own `Fetch`+`RenderDocument` with `JSLog` capturing every compile/runtime error, then look at what actually fired. A sweep of 10 real sites (news, SaaS, framework-marketing homepages) this round found `document.referrer.indexOf(hostname)` throwing `TypeError: Cannot read property 'indexOf' of undefined or null at trackPageview` identically on BOTH vuejs.org and astro.build — the same third-party script (`cdn.usefathom.com/script.js`) on two otherwise-unrelated sites, the same "one popular library, many sites break the same way" shape `document.location` (round 199) had.

- **Confirmed genuinely absent**: `grep -rn referrer js/` (excluding tests) returned nothing at all.
- **Fixed**: a new read-only accessor on `document`, `b.accessor(d, "referrer", func() goja.Value { return b.vm.ToValue("") }, nil)` (`js/dom.go`, next to the existing `hidden`/`visibilityState` accessors) — empty string, the spec's own "no referring document" case, which this engine's single-URL fetch model always is (no navigation history to have arrived from).
- Verified end to end against both real live sites, before/after: the `TypeError` line disappears from `JSLog` on both; vuejs.org's own separate, unrelated, already-documented dynamic-`import()` `SyntaxError` (a genuine upstream goja parser gap) is untouched, confirming the fix's effect is precisely scoped.
- **A second lead found by the same sweep, NOT pursued this round**: `stripe.com`'s own bundle calls `<canvas>.getContext(...)`, which this engine's `js/` package does not implement at all (`TypeError: Object has no member 'getContext'`, fired repeatedly on that one page). Larger in scope than a single property stub — a real `CanvasRenderingContext2D` surface, even a minimal inert one, is a bigger decision than `document.referrer`'s own size — flagged for a dedicated future round rather than bundled in here.
- Also confirmed, correctly out of scope: `Unexpected token await` (bbc.com, nextjs.org — top-level `await` in an ES module, a goja parser gap) and `Unexpected reserved word` (vuejs.org, svelte.dev — dynamic `import()`, the same gap round 199 already documented) are genuine upstream limitations with no parser in this repo to patch.
- Tests: `js/js_test.go`'s new `TestDocumentReferrerIsEmptyString` — calls `.indexOf()` on the result directly, the real trigger's own exact shape, not just a property-exists check.

## 2026-11-01 (round 199) — `document.location` did not exist, only `window.location` — a real TypeError on an enormous number of real sites via Cloudflare's own analytics beacon (defect, engine#338)

Bibliography first: HTML Standard (`Document.location` returns the browsing context's `Location` — the SAME object `window.location` returns, not an independent one). The fifth feature-survey attempt this session (round 198, a targeted container-query-units lead on `lab.ishadeed.com/container-queries`) found the lead itself a dead end (no element on that page actually carries the `cqi`/`cqw`-styled classes its own CSS defines), but surfaced two live JS failures while checking why an Astro `client:visible` island never hydrated there. Both were run down to their exact root cause by reproducing them byte-for-byte OUTSIDE the engine first — fetching the real third-party scripts, replaying this engine's own `esbuild` bundling options via its public API, then compiling/running the result through the pinned `goja` version directly — not guessed from the log lines alone.

- **`inline#1`'s `SyntaxError` is a genuine, confirmed, UPSTREAM goja limitation, not fixed here**: Astro's own universal client-hydration bootstrap script (a classic, non-`module` `<script>` that defines the `<astro-island>` custom element) does `await import(e)` — a **dynamic** `import()` with a runtime-computed URL, which lazy-hydration fundamentally cannot avoid (the whole point is deferring the load until hydration time, so a build-time bundler cannot resolve it ahead of time the way `modules.go`'s own esbuild pass resolves static `<script type="module">` imports). Verified directly against the pinned goja version: `goja.Compile` rejects `import(...)` in ANY position with `SyntaxError: Unexpected reserved word` — confirming `modules.go`'s own existing doc comment, and matching the exact precedent already in Known Gaps for the unrelated `for await` (ES2018 async-iteration) gap: no JS parser lives in this repo to patch. **This is the actual reason `<astro-island>` elements never hydrate in this engine on ANY site using Astro's `client:*` directives** — the custom-element definition itself never compiles, so `customElements.define("astro-island", ...)` never even runs (a more fundamental blocker than the IntersectionObserver-stub theory rounds 195/196 raised, which is never reached).
- **`inline#2`'s `TypeError` IS a small, confirmed, fixed engine bug**: `inline#2` is Cloudflare's own web-analytics beacon (`static.cloudflareinsights.com/beacon.min.js`), bundled via this engine's EXISTING esbuild `<script type="module">` path — entirely unrelated to Astro. It reads `document.location.href` directly and threw reading `.href` off `undefined`: `js/window.go` set `location` on the global/`window` object only; `document` never got one at all. Fixed by building the `Location` object once and setting it on both `window` and `document` — NOT by calling `newLocation()` twice, which would have created two independently-mutating objects (its own `assign()`/`replace()` closures only update the one instance they were built around), silently diverging after a script called either accessor's navigation methods.
- Cloudflare's beacon is served on an enormous share of real-world sites — a disproportionately high-reach fix for its size (one line + the shared-object reasoning).
- Verified end to end against the real live page (not just the unit test): the `TypeError` line is gone from this engine's own `JSLog` output on `lab.ishadeed.com/container-queries` after the fix; the unrelated, unfixable `inline#1` `SyntaxError` and the astro-island's continued non-hydration remain exactly as diagnosed, confirming the fix's effect is precisely scoped.
- Tests: `js/js_test.go`'s new `TestDocumentLocationIsWindowLocation` (identity, and that a mutation through either accessor is visible through the other).

## 2026-11-01 (round 198) — `layout.commonAncestor`'s zero-allocation rewrite, a fifth feature survey running in parallel again found nothing shippable (performance, no engine# — no behaviour change)

Continuing round 197's profiling in parallel with another feature survey. The fifth feature-survey attempt (targeting the one real lead round 196 left open — container query units, `cqw`/`cqh`/`cqi`/`cqb`, confirmed genuinely unimplemented) found a real site using them inside `@container` blocks (`lab.ishadeed.com/container-queries`) — but personally rendering it through this engine's own `Fetch`+`RenderDocument` (not just grepping its compiled CSS) found ZERO elements anywhere in the settled DOM carrying any of the relevant CSS-module class names, even static ones before any JS runs — the component markup simply isn't on this page at all. Closed, not reachable here. That same verification surfaced a separate, unrelated finding (Astro's own client-hydration runtime failing to compile/run in this engine — `SyntaxError: Unexpected reserved word` and a `TypeError` reading `.href` of undefined) that a dedicated follow-up round is now chasing to a root cause before any fix is attempted.

- **Fixed**: `layout.commonAncestor` (called once per adjacent inline-item pair in `markNoWrap` — once per word/line-item boundary, document-wide) built a `map[*dom.Node]bool` from `a`'s whole ancestor chain, then scanned `b`'s. Replaced with the textbook zero-allocation tree-LCA: equalize depth via a new `nodeDepth` helper, then co-walk comparing pointers — no map, no slice. **Measured on a tree with REALISTIC markup nesting depth (16 levels — a shallow random-attachment tree was tried first and understated the gap, since such a tree has only O(log n) depth; rebuilt once that was noticed rather than trusting the first number): 1508ns/936B/5 allocs → 43.6ns/0B/0 allocs**, a clean win on every axis. Correctness cross-checked against the original (kept as a test-only oracle, `commonAncestorOriginal`) over 2000 random node pairs on a random ~500-node tree, plus hand-checked basic shapes (self, parent/child, siblings, cousins, disjoint trees) and nil inputs.
- **An inner nil-check inside the co-walk loop was provably dead code, removed rather than tested around**: after depth-equalization, both pointers are mathematically guaranteed to reach `nil` on the exact same iteration (by `nodeDepth`'s own definition — the exact distance to `nil`), so the loop's own `a != b` condition always catches the both-nil case before the body re-enters with either one already nil. Caught by the coverage-gate ratchet (`layout` dropped 100.0%→99.9%) rather than left in as untestable defensive code.
- **Also investigated this round, declined**: `appendWords`/`appendCollapsed`'s per-word `&InlineItem{...}` allocation (the dominant real cost there) is structurally unavoidable without replacing `[]*InlineItem` with a value slice throughout `layout/` — a much larger, riskier refactor, out of scope. A smaller presizing attempt measured only a 3-of-124-allocation (~2%) improvement with no clean time separation under this machine's current load — not shipped, the same bar round 197 set (must win on time AND allocations, not maybe-allocations-alone). `paint.go`'s per-element group-buffer (`image.NewRGBA`, 31% of the heap profile) pooling is feasible (painting is confirmed single-threaded) but sits underneath a more fundamental issue — the buffer is sized from canvas row 0 to the element's own row, not from the element's own top, so its waste is proportional to the element's absolute page position, not pooling's own target (allocation count) — flagged for a dedicated future round's bibliography-first look, not attempted here.
- `BenchmarkCommonAncestor` and `BenchmarkRuleIndexCandidates` (round 197) both now keep this session's performance claims re-checkable, the same "committed benchmark" precedent `svg_test.go`'s own gradient-heavy benchmarks established (round 168/169).

## 2026-11-01 (round 197) — first profiling-based performance pass this session, run in parallel with a fourth (also empty) feature survey (performance, no engine# — no behaviour change)

With feature surveys running dry (four attempts in a row across rounds 196-197 found nothing confirmed — cold marketing sites, the engine's own proven corpus, a different site genre, and finally JavaScript APIs specifically, which turned out to already be far more thoroughly covered than expected after 190+ prior rounds: full `Intl`, `crypto.subtle`, `navigator.*`, `performance.*`, `structuredClone` were all already implemented), this round instead ran this session's first genuine CPU/allocation profiling pass — `bench/REPORT.md` has always been a visual-fidelity (SSIM/pixdiff) report, never a profiled performance one.

- **Method**: `go test -cpuprofile`/heap profiling of a full `engine.Render` (fetch→parse→cascade→layout→paint) against real live pages (pkg.go.dev/net/http, tailwindcss.com), `GOMAXPROCS(1)`, single core. Load average was checked first (38.99/27.75/21.56, far above the ~8 ceiling) — wall-clock/throughput numbers from that run are correctly discarded as meaningless; only this process's OWN sampled CPU/allocation *proportions* (not absolute throughput) are reported, since those are unaffected by contention from other processes.
- **Both pages are overwhelmingly I/O-bound (only 19-24% of wall-clock even on-CPU), matching this engine's own already-disclosed network-latency findings** — not a new discovery, a confirmation.
- **Of the CPU time actually spent, GC/allocation pressure dominates over arithmetic** (`runtime.madvise` alone: 14.7%/21.9% of sampled CPU). Heap profile: ~1GB allocated per render, led by `image.NewRGBA` (31%, largely the unavoidable canvas + per-element group buffers filter/opacity/mask-image/clip-path already need), `css.(*ruleIndex).candidates` (7% — a concrete, isolated allocation site, not a generic "most work happens here" attribution) and the cascade/layout tree-walk's own helpers.
- **The one SIMD-shaped hotspot found (image resampling, ~17% on tailwindcss.com) is already SIMD-accelerated** — in two separate DEPENDENCY libraries (go-images, go-gfx), each shipping its own independent NEON kernel. A real duplication, but a go-images/go-gfx consolidation concern, not a go-webengine/engine change. `paint.blendPixel`'s scalar compositing (structurally SIMD-amenable) measured too small in practice (0.8-1.2%) to be worth a kernel.
- **Fixed, the one clean win this round's profiling pointed to**: `css.(*ruleIndex).candidates` built its output slice via `var out []int` left to grow from nil through repeated `append` — 3-4 reallocations per call, once per element in the WHOLE document. An exactly-presized version (summing every `ix.byClass[c]` length up front) was tried and measured, honestly, as SLOWER despite fewer allocations (165.6ns/64B/1 alloc vs the original 153.6ns/120B/4 allocs) — the extra map lookups needed to size it exactly cost more than the allocation they saved. A cheap, O(1) head-start (`len(ix.always)+4`, no extra lookups) won on every axis instead: **116-120ns/96B/2 allocs**, faster AND fewer allocations than the original. `css/ruleindex_test.go`'s new `BenchmarkRuleIndexCandidates` keeps this re-checkable, the same "committed benchmark, not a one-off git-stash comparison" precedent `svg_test.go`'s own gradient-heavy benchmarks already established (round 168/169).
- Scratch profiling harness and raw profiles left in the scratchpad (not committed — one-turn tooling, not reusable infrastructure for this repo). No behaviour change; no `engine#` issue number.

## 2026-11-01 (round 196) — chaos pass on round 194/195's new parsers, after three empty feature surveys in a row (security, no engine# — fuzz coverage only, no behaviour change)

Three independent survey strategies this round — cold-searching fresh marketing sites, re-mining 190+ rounds' own proven corpus against a site→feature→round table, and a deliberately different site population (a live Discourse forum, VitePress/Sphinx docs, a news site) — all failed to find a confirmed, reachable, small feature gap. Rather than a fourth cold search, this round pivoted to the standing security/chaos mandate: round 194's `ResolveClipPathPolygon` and round 195's `firstSrcsetCandidate` are both parsers reached directly from untrusted page content (an author's own `style="clip-path:..."`, a `srcset` attribute) that had shipped with zero fuzz coverage.

- **`FuzzResolveClipPathPolygon`** (`css/clippath_test.go`): seeded with the real vercel.com trigger, both fill-rule keywords, a 300-deep unbalanced-paren string, a 2000-deep nested `calc((((...1...))))` (well past `calcParser`'s own `maxCalcDepth` bound, reused unchanged by `evalCalcWithBasis`), and assorted malformed input. Checks the invariant `len(points) <= strings.Count(s, ",")+1` — a valid result can never manufacture more vertices than the input had commas to separate them. 16.6M executions in 60s, 0 crashes, 0 invariant violations.
- **`FuzzFirstSrcsetCandidate`** (`picture_test.go`): checks its result is always either empty or a literal substring of the input — this function only ever selects a slice of its own input, never synthesises bytes. 1.36M executions in 30s, 0 crashes.
- Both clean. No code behaviour changed — this round is test-only (new `_test.go` fuzz functions), so no `engine#` issue number and no version bump beyond the fuzz-corpus addition itself.

## 2026-11-01 (round 195) — `<picture>`/`<source>`/`srcset`, entirely unimplemented until now (feature, engine#334)

Bibliography first: HTML Standard §4.8.2 (the `<picture>` element itself renders nothing; its `<source>` children offer candidates, its `<img>` child supplies the fallback) and §4.8.5.1 (source selection: a `<source>` whose `media` doesn't match is skipped; the first one that does wins). Confirmed real trigger: en.wikipedia.org's own footer — present, unconditionally, in every article's static server-rendered markup — `<picture><source media="(min-width: 500px)" srcset="/static/images/footer/wikimedia-button.svg" width="84" height="29"><img src="/static/images/footer/wikimedia.svg" width="25" height="25" alt="Wikimedia Foundation" lang="en" loading="lazy"></picture>` (and an identical second one for the MediaWiki logo).

- **A clean miss, not a bug**: `grep -rl srcset` across the whole Go source returns nothing at all — this engine always read an `<img>`'s own bare `src`, with zero awareness a `<picture>` ancestor could override it. At this engine's own default 1024px render width (above the 500px breakpoint), a real browser shows the wide 84×29/88×31 branded button; this engine always showed the tiny 25×25 fallback icon instead — confirmed visually different, not merely a missing attribute.
- **Fix**: a new `effectiveImgSrc(n *dom.Node, viewportW float64) (string, bool)` (`images.go`), called at both existing bare-`src` read sites (the budget-gate's classification and `loadOneImage`'s own fetch) instead of a direct `n.Attribute("src")`. An `<img>` with no `<picture>` parent takes none of this and returns its own `src` unchanged — the only path a plain `<img>` can ever take, so every other page's image loading is byte-for-byte unaffected. For a `<picture>` child, it walks the sibling `<source>` elements in document order, skipping any whose `media` doesn't match (reusing `css.MediaMatches` — a PRE-EXISTING but until this round uncalled-in-production function, simply renamed from lowercase `mediaMatches`, not a new matcher written from scratch), and takes the first comma-separated candidate of the first matching `<source>`'s `srcset`.
- **Deliberately narrower than the full algorithm, matching the confirmed trigger exactly**: a `<source>`'s `type` attribute (skip one whose declared MIME type can't be decoded) and a `srcset`'s width/density descriptors (picking the best of several candidates) are not evaluated — Wikipedia's own two triggers have neither, one type-less, descriptor-less `srcset` each. A real `srcset` with multiple comma-separated candidates picks the first one unconditionally, not genuine density/width selection — the same kind of disclosed "one deliberate simplification" this engine already makes for `mask-image`'s stretch-to-fill.
- **Found via three failed survey attempts before a fourth succeeded**: `accent-color` and `object-position` (both confirmed unimplemented, neither with a confirmed statically-reachable live trigger after checking GOV.UK, linear.app, tailwindcss.com, vercel.com's pricing page), native CSS nesting (confirmed genuinely unimplemented — `parse.go`'s declaration splitter never tracks `{`/`}` depth — but every real build tool checked flattens it before shipping, so no reachable trigger), `text-wrap:pretty` (treated identically to `auto`, plausible but unconfirmed). The fourth attempt's own method — re-mining this engine's OWN proven corpus pages (the ones FIDELITY.md's own 190+ prior rounds already cite) for a feature DIFFERENT from whatever round already fixed there, rather than cold-searching fresh sites — is what found this one, on a page this session had never fetched before.
- Tests: `picture_test.go` (the exact confirmed shape, above/below the breakpoint, a plain `<img>` with no `<picture>` parent taking none of this, and `firstSrcsetCandidate`'s own narrow parse); `css/parse_test.go`'s pre-existing `TestMediaMatches` (unchanged in substance, just following the function's rename to exported).

## 2026-10-31 (round 194) — `clip-path: polygon()`, reversing three prior "declined, no confirmed visible trigger" verdicts (feature, engine#330)

Bibliography first: CSS Masking Module Level 1 §8 (clip-path's value grammar, and "If no reference box is specified, the border-box will be used" — the only reference box this round models) and CSS Shapes Module Level 1 §2.2 (`polygon(<'fill-rule'>? , [<length-percentage> <length-percentage>]#)`: the x half of each pair resolves against the reference box's width, the y half against its height; fill-rule defaults to `nonzero`). Confirmed real trigger, and the FIRST one this property has ever had: vercel.com's own homepage sets, in static server-rendered markup, on an always-present decorative background `<div>` — `clip-path:polygon(0 0, 100% 0, 100% calc(100% - 112.00px), calc(100% - 112.00px) 100%, 0 100%)`, a "paper-peel" corner cut. No interaction needed.

- **This is a reversal, not a fresh gap**: `clip-path` was investigated and correctly DECLINED at least three times before (see the round-44/103-era entry near line 899, and the two corpus-survey entries near lines 1177 and 1226) — every prior occurrence found was either the `sr-only`/`visually-hidden` accessibility idiom (already invisible via `width:1px;height:1px;overflow:hidden` regardless of clip-path) or an SVG `clip-path="..."` ATTRIBUTE false positive from keyword-only grepping, a completely different mechanism from the CSS property. vercel.com's instance is neither: a real, rendered, visually load-bearing decorative shape.
- **The non-obvious complication**: two of the five polygon vertices are `calc(100% - 112.00px)` — mixing a percentage with an absolute length. Read directly in `css/calc.go`: `calcParser.factor()`'s `+`/`-` already requires both sides to be the SAME kind (all lengths, all percentages, or all bare numbers), so a plain `calc(100% - 112px)` is unresolvable at cascade time, same as it is for every other property (`vars_test.go` already asserts this) — a pre-existing, separately-disclosed limitation this round does not lift in general. The fix is scoped specifically to clip-path, which (unlike width/height) has no existing "percentage deferred to layout" mechanism to reuse, because its box is known at PAINT time, not layout time.
- **Fix shape**: `css/cascade.go`'s Pass 2 gives clip-path its own resolution path (`resolveClipPathValue`, `css/vars.go`) that substitutes `var()` but deliberately SKIPS `resolveCalc` — the raw `polygon(...)` text (with any `calc()` still literal) is stored on the new `Style.ClipPath` field. At PAINT time, `css.ResolveClipPathPolygon` (new, `css/clippath.go`) resolves each coordinate against the element's own border-box width/height — a `calc()` term going through `evalCalcWithBasis` (new: `calcParser` gained an optional `basis`/`hasBasis`, converting a `%` term straight to a pixel length instead of carrying it unresolved, which is what lets it combine with an absolute length in the same sum). `paint/clippath.go`'s `applyClipPathMask` then stencils the element's existing group-opacity/mask buffer (the same offscreen path `mask-image`'s own `applyMask` already uses — see `paint/paint.go`'s `hasClipPath` gate) with a hard-edge point-in-polygon test (ray-casting; nonzero via signed winding number, evenodd via crossing parity — they agree on every simple, non-self-intersecting polygon, which is all a hard edge needs).
- **A real bug caught by writing the exact confirmed-trigger text as a test, not a synthetic one**: `strings.Fields` on a coordinate pair like `"100% calc(100% - 112.00px)"` splits on EVERY space, including the ones calc()'s own `-` operator needs — four tokens instead of two. Fixed with a new `splitTopLevelFields` (paren-depth-aware, `css/clippath.go`) before this ever reached a real page; caught by `TestResolveClipPathPolygonVercelTrigger` failing on first run, not by inspection.
- **Not modelled, and not needed by the confirmed trigger**: a `<geometry-box>` keyword (always border-box here), `circle()`/`ellipse()`/`inset()`/`path()`/`round <length>`, and a `url()` reference to an SVG `<clipPath>` element (a completely different, pre-existing code path — see `svg.go` — for embedded SVG images, not ordinary HTML elements). `::before`/`::after` are not extended either: no confirmed trigger puts clip-path on a pseudo-element, and `computePseudoElement` is deliberately left untouched.
- Tests: `css/clippath_test.go` (the exact confirmed-trigger polygon through `ResolveClipPathPolygon`, fill-rule keywords, invalid/unsupported shapes, `var()` substitution and its failure-to-resolve path through the real cascade, `apply()`'s unsupported-value-leaves-unchanged convention) and `css/calc.go`'s own `evalCalcWithBasis` tests (including a regression guard that the ordinary, basis-less `resolveCalc` path every other property uses still rejects a mixed calc() exactly as before); `paint/clippath_test.go` (a plain triangle, the exact vercel.com trigger painted and probed on both sides of the cut, the border-box-only masking convention mask-image's own tests already establish, the zero-sized-box guard, and the `evenodd` keyword reaching paint intact).

## 2026-10-31 — SVG stays vector in the PDF and on editable slides (feature, v0.22.0)

Bibliography first: SVG 1.1 §7.6 (transform; `scale(s)` is `scale(s s)`, lengths in the transformed user space), §11.3–11.4 (fill and stroke), §13.2 (gradients), §14.3 (clipping paths); ISO 32000-1 §8.10 (form XObjects), §14.7.4.2 (a form painted many times belongs to the marked content of each `Do`, and holds none of its own), §14.9.4 (ActualText); ISO/IEC 29500-1 §19.3.1.22 (`p:grpSp`); ODF 1.3 Part 3 §10.3.15 (`draw:g`), §20.406 (`svg:fill-rule`); go-tex/math's own source (glyphs are emitted as outline `<path>`s, rules as `<rect>`, not as text).

- **Before**: `PaintVector` placed every SVG (an inline `<svg>`, `<img src=svg>`, an SVG background) as its bitmap, so a formula, an emoji or a logo was pixels in the "vector" PDF and in the editable PPTX/ODP, blocky at zoom.
- **Now**: the same parsed document gives its shapes (go-gfx/oksvg v0.3.1 `Shapes`), drawn through the canvas: `pdfcanvas` as one form per distinct drawing per document, painted at each use inside its Figure/Formula, a formula with its TeX as `/ActualText`; `officecanvas` as a group. PDF against PNG at 96 dpi: under 3 % of the drawing's pixels differ (`TestSVGFormLooksLikeTheBitmap`), veraPDF PDF/UA-1 still passes. The PNG path is untouched and never builds the form (lazy).
- **Two oksvg defects found on the way, both changing the bitmap too, both now as a browser draws them**: a stroke ignored its transform (a 3-unit stroke under a 2× viewBox was 3 px, Chrome draws 6 — `svg_golden.png` regenerated, `TestSVGGolden` now asserts the width); `scale(2)` with one number was `scale(2, 0)`. And a hostile `stroke-dasharray` (tiny dashes on a long path) hung the stroker: past 65 536 dashes it is now drawn solid, and has no vector form.
- **Declined, written down**: a gradient whose stops change opacity, a reflected/repeated or focal one, a clipPath of clipped pieces — no exact vector form, placed as the bitmap; on a slide also any clip and any radial gradient (DrawingML/ODF have neither the clip nor the radius).

## 2026-10-31 (round 193) — `dvh`/`svh`/`lvh`/`dvw`/`svw`/`lvw` silently failed to parse at all — a suffix-matching ordering bug, not a missing feature (defect, engine#329)

Bibliography first: CSS Values and Units Module Level 4 §6.5.1 (the small/large/dynamic viewport-percentage units — `svh`/`lvh`/`dvh` and their width counterparts — differ only in how a mobile browser's own collapsing address-bar chrome affects the viewport size a browser samples; this engine has no scrolling or chrome show/hide at all, so a single static render has exactly one viewport size and all three variants resolve identically to plain `vh`/`vw`). Confirmed real trigger: chakra-ui.com's own compiled stylesheet sets `body{min-height:100dvh}` directly — unconditionally, on page load, no interaction needed.

- **The actual bug, found reading the real code rather than assuming it was simply unimplemented**: `css/value.go`'s `parseLength` already had a `case strings.HasSuffix(s, "vw"), strings.HasSuffix(s, "vh")` — and `strings.HasSuffix("100dvh", "vh")` is TRUE (`HasSuffix` only checks the trailing characters). The existing code then sliced off exactly 2 characters (`s[:len(s)-2]`) to get the number, turning `"100dvh"` into `"100d"`, which fails `strconv.ParseFloat` — so the WHOLE declaration silently dropped, the standard "unsupported value" convention, not a crash or a wrong number. `dvh`/`svh`/`lvh`/`dvw`/`svw`/`lvw` were never a documented, declined gap; they simply fell through an existing case meant for something else.
- **Fixed with a new case checked BEFORE the existing 2-character one** (ordering matters: a 3-character suffix like `dvh` also satisfies `HasSuffix(s, "vh")`, so the more specific case must run first), stripping 3 characters and resolving identically to the existing plain `vw`/`vh` path.
- **The identical bug, independently reopened in a sibling function**: round 190's `resolveFontSizeLength` (font-size's own EAGER vw resolution, against the viewport width directly rather than the generic `Length.IsPercent`-against-parent-font-size path every other property uses) has its OWN separate `HasSuffix(lv, "vw")` check, run BEFORE ever reaching `parseLength`'s now-fixed case — so `font-size:13dvw` would have hit the exact same slice-by-2 failure, just in a different function. Fixed proactively in the same PR: not a speculative new capability, the same known failure mode in the function already fixed for this exact unit family once this round.
- **Not touched, and correctly so**: `calc()` already rejects ANY percentage-kind term (`calc.go`'s `factor()` explicitly returns failure for a percent-kind sub-expression outside its own dedicated `%` branch) — `calc(100vh - ...)` was already unresolvable before this round for PLAIN `vh`, a pre-existing, separately-disclosed limitation this fix does not change or need to touch, confirmed by reading `factor()` directly rather than assuming the fix needed to extend there too.
- Tests: `css/value_test.go` (`TestParseLength`'s own table extended with `vw`/`vh` — previously, surprisingly, untested at all — plus the six new three-character variants and an invalid case); `css/clamp_test.go` (`resolveFontSizeLength`'s own `dvw`/`svw`/`lvw` fix). Live end-to-end check (scratch repro, not committed) confirmed `body{min-height:100dvh}` resolves through the real cascade to `Percent:1.0, IsPercent:true` — the exact confirmed trigger shape.

## 2026-10-31 (round 192) — `HTMLFormElement.requestSubmit()`/`.submit()`, scoped to the near-universal real reason a page calls either (feature, engine#328)

Bibliography first: HTML Standard §4.10.21.3 (`requestSubmit(submitter)` runs constraint validation, then dispatches a real, cancelable, bubbling "submit" Event before any actual submission; the legacy `submit()` fires no event at all and skips validation). Confirmed real trigger: linear.app's own fetched JS bundle calls `form.requestSubmit()` directly — before this round, an uncaught `TypeError: requestSubmit is not a function`, the same whole-script-abort shape round 50's missing `customElements` global had.

- **Read the actual cross-package boundary before assuming the fix shape, not just a comment** (the lesson round 190's own survey needed and round 191's correctly applied): `formsubmit.go`'s real submission flow (`LiveDocument.Submit`) is `package engine`, which imports `js` — the reverse direction `js` would need to call it from a native-bound method is not available (no import cycle allowed), and `js.Options` has no existing outbound callback field to bridge it either. Rather than building new cross-package plumbing for a capability this engine's own single-document settle architecture cannot complete anyway (real submission is a page navigation), the fix stays entirely inside `js/dom.go`.
- **Scoped to what every real caller actually needs**: `requestSubmit()` dispatches the "submit" Event through the EXISTING `b.dispatch`/`b.newEvent` machinery every other DOM event already uses — the near-universal reason a modern SPA calls it at all is to run its OWN `onsubmit` handler, which almost always calls `preventDefault()` itself and submits via `fetch()`, never actually waiting on a real browser navigation. Per-control constraint validation (checking every control and firing "invalid" on failures) is NOT implemented: the per-element `checkValidity`/`setCustomValidity` this engine already has does not generalise to "check every control in a form" without a form-level pass that does not exist, and no confirmed trigger needs it. Neither method performs an actual network submission when the event is not cancelled — the SAME disclosed limitation `window.location.assign()`/`.reload()` already have (this engine's single-document settle has no later page to navigate to), not a new one introduced here.
- Tests: `js/js_test.go` — the event's own bubbles/cancelable/target fields (what a real handler actually inspects), the optional `submitter` argument, `submit()`'s own no-event behaviour (matching spec exactly, not a gap), and that calling either on a non-form element throws a real TypeError rather than silently no-opping — this engine's "present but inert" convention is for a missing GLOBAL, not for a real method called on the wrong element type.

## 2026-10-30 (round 191) — CSS Grid Level 2 `subgrid`, investigated and correctly declined — not a narrow parser addition (documentation only, no code change, engine#327)

Bibliography first: CSS Grid Layout Module Level 2 §2 (a subgridded axis's own tracks are the PARENT grid's tracks sliced to the child's own line range, not independently sized at all). Confirmed real trigger, bibliography-grade not assumed: stripe.com/pricing's own inline stylesheet sets `grid-template-columns:subgrid` four times, each on an element that is itself a grid item (`grid-column:span N`) meant to align its own children to the parent's column boundaries — e.g. `.ProductsCallout--desktop{grid-column:1/span 4;display:grid;grid-template-columns:subgrid;...}`.

- **The actual blocker, read directly rather than trusted from a comment**: `css/grid.go`'s `parseTrackList` already drops the whole declaration on the literal `subgrid` token (correctly, if tersely, disclosed in its own doc comment) — but recognising the token would be a no-op on its own. `layout/grid.go`'s `grid()` lays out each item via `l.layoutIsolated(it.node, it.st, spanW-it.hEdges-it.hMargin)` (line 72) — a single scalar WIDTH, with no path at all for the parent's own already-computed per-track `colW`/`colX` arrays (from `sizeColumns`, line 66, two lines above) to reach a subgrid child's own SUBSEQUENT `grid()` call, which would otherwise try to size its own columns from its own (by definition absent) template. Real support needs a genuinely new data-flow path threading parent track geometry into a child's own layout call — comparable in scope to the already-deferred multi-column balancing work, not a few-line fix.
- **A previous survey's own "minimal fix shape" lesson, applied in the SAME round it was learned**: this round's own fork survey was explicitly told to read the actual function bodies it cites before proposing a fix shape (round 190's own `font-size:vw` survey had trusted a doc comment instead and got the architecture wrong, though it still found a real bug by accident). This time the fork read `placeGridItem`/`l.grid()` directly and correctly reported subgrid as architecturally large — a working application of the lesson, not just a retelling of it.
- **No code change shipped this round** — the finding is real and precisely characterised, but forcing an oversized change to have something to ship would be the opposite of this project's own "narrower than it looks, or correctly declined" discipline. Documented here so the NEXT survey that stumbles onto `subgrid` again finds this entry instead of re-deriving the same investigation from zero.

## 2026-10-30 (round 190) — `font-size: clamp()`, closed for the no-percentage case — and a pre-existing, independent bug it surfaced: a bare `font-size:13vw` was already silently resolving against the wrong basis (feature + defect, found by a fork survey of carbondesignsystem.com)

Bibliography first: CSS Values and Units Module Level 4 §10.4 (`clamp(MIN, PREFERRED, MAX)` = `max(MIN, min(PREFERRED, MAX))`, applied in that exact order — not a sequential clamp, which differs when an author gives MIN > MAX) and CSS Fonts Module Level 4 §5.4 (a `<percentage>` on `font-size` resolves against the PARENT element's computed font-size, an entirely different reference than a `<percentage>`/`vw` on a width-like property, which resolves against a containing block only known at layout time).

- **The real confirmed trigger, re-checked bibliography-first against the earlier `min()`/`max()` decline** (FIDELITY.md's own log, "nearly every real instance mixes a viewport-relative unit with an absolute length — the exact same 'cannot resolve without layout-time context' wall `calc()` hits"): carbondesignsystem.com's own compiled stylesheet sets `font-size:clamp(4rem,13vw,12rem)` for fluid heading sizes — `rem` and `vw` only, no percentage. That earlier decline's reasoning is about a WIDTH-LIKE property's percentage (deferred to layout); font-size's own percentage/`vw` resolve EAGERLY at cascade time (there is no later pass to defer to), so the wall that sank `min()`/`max()` for everything else genuinely does not apply here.
- **Found investigating it, not assumed**: `font-size: 13vw`, with no `clamp()` at all, was ALREADY silently wrong before this round. `css/value.go`'s `parseLength` folds `vw` into the exact same `Length{IsPercent:true,...}` representation a genuine `%` uses (correct for width-like properties, where vw is itself only an approximation of the containing-block-relative case) — but `font-size`'s own cascade-time resolution then multiplied that fraction by `emRef` (the PARENT's font-size), the correct basis for a real percentage, not for `vw`, which means the VIEWPORT width. A page using `font-size:13vw` got "13% of the parent's font-size" instead of "13% of the viewport width".
- **`css/value.go`**: `resolveFontSizeLength` (the single fix point for both the bare-`vw` bug and each term of `clamp()`) gives `vw` its own branch, resolved against a NEW `vw` argument, before falling through to the existing `parseLength`/`Length.IsPercent`-against-`emRef` path for everything else (`%`, `rem`, `px`, …) unchanged. `parseClampFontSize` resolves `clamp()`'s three comma-separated terms through it and combines them via the spec's own `max(min, min(pref, max))` formula, exactly as written (not a sequential clamp — verified against the degenerate MIN > MAX case, where the two orders disagree).
- **`css/cascade.go`**: `computeElement`/`computePseudoElement` gained a `vw float64` parameter — ONE real call site each (`walk`'s own single recursive closure already had `m.Width` in scope) — threaded into `Style.apply`.
- **`css/parse.go`**: `Style.apply` gained `vw` as an OPTIONAL variadic trailing argument, not a required one — this engine's own test suite has 60+ existing direct `.apply(...)` call sites across nine files with no reason to care about a viewport width; a required parameter would have forced a mechanical edit to every one of them for zero behavioural gain. Every PRODUCTION call site passes the real value; `applyOn` (the dominant test helper nearly all of them actually go through) needed no change at all, since it simply never supplies the new, optional argument.
- **Deliberately narrower than it could be**: `clamp()` on properties OTHER than `font-size` and `min()`/`max()` anywhere remain unimplemented — no confirmed trigger needs either, and width-like properties genuinely do still hit the layout-time wall `calc()` already documents. A `clamp()` term mixing a real percentage with `vw`/`rem` (not just the confirmed no-percentage shape) is not attempted — no confirmed trigger uses it.
- Tests: `css/clamp_test.go` — `resolveFontSizeLength`'s own vw-vs-percent distinction (including a regression guard that genuine percentages still resolve against `emRef`, unaffected), `parseClampFontSize` at the exact confirmed trigger plus a narrow viewport (the min wins) and a wide one (the max wins) and the degenerate MIN>MAX case, `Style.apply`'s variadic `vw` argument (both supplied and omitted — omitted correctly resolves vw-against-0 rather than crashing), and `CascadeFontSizeClampEndToEnd` through the real cascade. Live end-to-end check (scratch repro, not committed) at three viewport widths (320/1024/2560px) confirmed the full floor→fluid→ceiling range matches a real browser's own fluid-typography behaviour for this exact pattern.

## 2026-10-30 (round 189) — `window.WebAssembly`, executing a page's own `.wasm` modules rather than just recognising the global (feature, requested directly, engine#325)

Not a corpus-found gap: asked directly ("go-webengine est-il capable de faire tourner nos conteneurs webassembly?"). Before this round, `WebAssembly` did not exist as a global at all, so a page's own `WebAssembly.instantiate(...)` threw an uncaught ReferenceError and silently aborted the whole script it appeared in — the same shape `newCustomElementRegistry`'s own doc comment already documents for round 50's missing-global class of bug.

Bibliography first: the WebAssembly JS API spec (webassembly.github.io/spec/js-api/) for the exact method/property shapes and the ToWebAssemblyValue/ToJSValue type-coercion rules (i32 via ToInt32, i64 requires an actual BigInt — ToBigInt does not implicitly convert a plain Number, f32/f64 via ToNumber); wazero (github.com/tetratelabs/wazero, pure Go, zero dependencies beyond golang.org/x/sys, Apache-2.0) for the actual execution, checked specifically for this fleet's own multiarch CI matrix before adopting it: its "Compiler" engine (native code generation) only covers amd64/arm64, but its "Interpreter" engine has no architecture-specific code at all and wazero's own default already falls back to it where the Compiler isn't supported — confirmed by cross-compiling for loong64/ppc64le/riscv64/s390x locally before shipping, not just trusting the docs.

- **`js/wasm.go`** (new): a JS-facing binding layer over wazero, the same shape `js/net.go` is over `net/http` or `js/interfaces.go` is over the `dom` package — not a WASM interpreter written from scratch. `WebAssembly.compile`/`instantiate`/`validate`; a module's own exported functions (JS callable, numeric args/results marshalled per the spec's own coercion rules) and its own exported linear memory (`instance.exports.memory.buffer` is a REAL, zero-copy `ArrayBuffer` view over wazero's own backing bytes via `goja.Runtime.NewArrayBuffer`, re-fetched after a `memory.grow` the same way a real browser transfers a fresh buffer); function imports (`importObject[module][name]`) are wired as real wazero host functions, validated at `instantiate()` time — a missing/non-callable import rejects with a LinkError immediately, not lazily at the first call.
- **`js/js.go`/`js/session.go`**: `binder.wasmRuntime`/`wasmModules` — the wazero runtime is created lazily on a page's first WebAssembly call (a page that never touches it pays nothing) and released by `Session.Close` (confirmed via wazero's own docs that `Runtime.Close` tears down every `CompiledModule`/`Module` it ever created, not just what the caller explicitly closed).
- **Security, audited as its own category** (this round is the largest new attack surface this session has added: arbitrary bytecode execution from an untrusted page): a module size cap (`maxWasmModuleBytes`, 32 MiB) bounds `compile()`'s input; a memory-growth cap (`maxWasmMemoryPages`, 256 pages = 16 MiB) via wazero's own `WithMemoryLimitPages` bounds a hostile module's `memory.grow` loop; every WASM call is deadlined at `b.deadman` — the SAME wall-clock budget JS execution is already bound by — via wazero's `WithCloseOnContextDone`, so a runaway module cannot outrun the page's own time budget the way an infinite JS loop already cannot. No WASI (`wasi_snapshot_preview1`) is wired: a module assuming a WASI host fails to instantiate with a LinkError naming the exact missing import — the honest outcome for a capability this engine does not model, not a silent wrong answer, and a smaller surface than modelling stdio/filesystem/clocks would be.
- **Deliberately narrower than the full spec**, no confirmed container needs more yet: `new WebAssembly.Module(...)`/`new WebAssembly.Instance(...)` (the synchronous constructor forms — the async `compile()`/`instantiate()` cover the same need); Table and Global imports/exports; standalone `new WebAssembly.Memory(...)`/`new WebAssembly.Table(...)` (wazero has no API to allocate memory/a table outside a module context — only a module's OWN declared memory, reached through `instance.exports`, is modelled); `compileStreaming`/`instantiateStreaming` (this engine has no Response/ReadableStream-based streaming fetch path to stream from).
- **Fixture built without an external toolchain**: `wat2wasm` was broken in this environment (a missing dylib from a stale Homebrew install, left alone rather than touching an unrelated system package to fix it) — `js/testdata/gen_add_wasm.go` hand-assembles a tiny real module instead (two exported functions, one function import, one exported memory with a data segment), bibliography-first against the binary format spec's own section layout and opcode table. Caught its own encoding bug via independent verification BEFORE writing any JS binding test: a standalone program ran the hand-assembled bytes through wazero directly and got `addf(1.5,2.25) = -0.75`, not `3.75` — `f64.add` is opcode `0xA0`, not the `0xA1` first guessed.
- Tests: `js/wasm_test.go` — the full round trip (host import called FROM wasm, exported i32 and f64 functions called BY script, the module's own linear memory read back) against the real fixture, `compile()`-then-`instantiate(module)` (the two-step form), `validate()` on both real and garbage bytes, `compile()`'s CompileError rejection, `instantiate()`'s LinkError rejection for a missing import (caught by this round's own first test run: a first draft deferred the failure to the first CALL instead of instantiate time, which a real browser's own LinkError timing rules out), and `toWasmValue`'s own i64-requires-BigInt rule at the unit directly responsible for it, both directions (a plain Number rejected, a BigInt — including a negative one, exercising the two's-complement wraparound — accepted).

## 2026-10-28 (round 188) — `transform: scale()`/`scaleX()`/`scaleY()` and the standalone `scale` property, the third transform subset this engine understands (feature, engine#324)

Bibliography first: CSS Transforms Module Level 1's own formal grammar for `scale()` (`<number>, <number>?` — a single argument scales both axes uniformly, `sy` defaulting to `sx`; percentages appear informally in some references but not in the Level 1 grammar, so not attempted without a confirmed trigger needing them). Confirmed the real trigger live (a fork survey): vuejs.org's own compiled stylesheet sets a plain, static `transform:scale(.8)` on its light/dark theme-toggle switch — not inside `:hover` or an animation, so it is the element's ordinary computed style any static render must honour.

- **`css/value.go`**: `HasScale`/`ScaleX`/`ScaleY` — a `HasScale` bool disambiguates the Go zero value from a real `scale(0)` (CSS's own valid "collapse to a point", not a safe default), the same role `HasOpacity` already plays for `Opacity`.
- **`css/parse.go`**: `parseTransformScale` (mirrors `parseTransformRotate`'s single-function, no-mixing scope exactly) plus the standalone CSS Transforms Level 2 `scale` property (`case "scale"`, a bare `<number>{1,2}`) — the same two-spellings-one-field shape `rotate`'s own standalone property already has, once one spelling is evidenced the other is not a distinct speculative addition. `transform`'s three branches (translate/rotate/scale) now each reset the OTHER two's fields, keeping the existing "a valid transform replaces the whole computed value" rule exact. A negative factor (CSS's own axis-flip) is rejected at parse time — no confirmed trigger needs a flip, and `paintScaled` only resizes pixels, it does not mirror them.
- **`paint/paint.go`**: `paintScaled`, the scale sibling of `paintRotated` — identical offscreen-buffer shape (render the box's own border-box rectangle, transform the PIXELS, composite back centred on the box's original centre), swapping go-images' `Rotate` for its `Resize` (bilinear, matching Rotate's own choice in the same dependency). Scoped to scale ALONE, the same reason rotate is: no confirmed real trigger combines it with filter/opacity/mask-image, or with rotate itself (mutually exclusive by construction, since both write through the same `transform` property parsing).
- **Caught by the coverage gate, not assumed fine**: the first `Resize` error-check branch (`w,h` already clamped to ≥1 before the call, so the only error condition it guards is provably unreachable) left `paint` at 99.9%, one statement under its 100.0% floor — removed the dead branch (discarding the error, matching `paintRotated`'s own sibling call to `Rotate`, which has no error to check at all) rather than writing a test to force unreachable code.
- Tests: `css/props_test.go` (`TestApplyTransformScale`, `TestApplyStandaloneScaleProperty` — including the `inherit` keyword, which needed its own explicit case in `inheritProperty`, the same as `rotate`'s); `paint/scale_test.go` (shrink/grow/non-uniform-axes/zero-collapse/opacity-exclusion/outside-clip, all green on the first run except one test's own channel-comparison bug, caught and fixed before trusting a false pass). Live end-to-end pixel check (scratch repro, not committed) against the real trigger shape: a scaled box's centre stays covered while its former corners reveal the background, confirming real pixel-level resizing, not a no-op.

## 2026-10-28 (round 187) — list markers missing from html2pdf, CSS string escapes painted literally, `counter()` unsupported (defects, found by rendering a report through go-pdfkit/html2pdf)

Bibliography opened first: CSS Syntax 3 §4.3.5 (consume a string token), §4.3.7
(consume an escaped code point) and §4.3.8; CSS Lists and Counters 3 (editor's
draft: §3 markers, §4 counters, §4.4.2's reversed-counter algorithm, §4.6 the
implicit `list-item` counter, Appendix A); CSS Counter Styles 3 §6; HTML's
rendering §15.3.7 (lists); and eight WPT css-lists tests, ported as Go tests:
`counter-001`, `counters-scope-001`, `counter-set-001`,
`counter-reset-increment-set-display-none`,
`counter-reset-increment-overflow-underflow`, `li-value-counter-reset-001`,
`counter-list-item` (ordinary, `start`, `value`, `reversed` and `ul` lists).

- **Markers**: the cascade now resolves each list item's `::marker` (its text
  from the `list-item` counter in `list-style-type`'s counter style plus its
  suffix, or `::marker` `content`) in a counters pass over the styled tree. An
  outside marker carries its text in `layout.Marker` — a bullet too ("•"), with
  `TextX`/`TextY`/`Ascent` — so a backend that only draws text gets it; an
  inside marker is an inline box at the start of the item's first line. The
  raster and vector painters still draw a disc, circle or square as a shape.
  go-pdfkit/html2pdf never read `Box.Marker`; it now draws `Marker.Text`.
- **Escapes**: decoded once where text enters the parser: every declaration
  value (`normalizeEscapes`, a canonical form that is a fixed point) and every
  selector prelude (`.\31 0` is the class `10`; its trailing space is not a
  combinator). `content` strings, `font-family`, counter names and quoted
  attribute values are read with the spec's string-token algorithm. A `}` or
  `;` inside a string no longer ends a block or a declaration.
- **Counters**: `counter-reset` (with `reversed()`), `counter-increment`,
  `counter-set`, inheritance from the parent and the preceding sibling, values
