  from the preceding element in tree order, `display:none` ignored,
  `counter()`/`counters()`.
- **Bounds**: 32-bit counter values (an overflowing increment is not applied,
  as WPT expects — the spec text says clamp, noted), at most 128 counters per
  element, a reversed-counter scope scan capped at 100,000 elements, at most 64
  names per counter declaration, and 4 KiB of counter-generated text per
  element (a literal is shared by every element it applies to; text built from
  `counters()` is per element, and a long separator over nested counters would
  otherwise multiply the stylesheet's size by the page's); escapes read at
  most six hex digits. Two
  fuzzers (`FuzzNormalizeEscapes`, `FuzzCounters`); the first found a NUL
  escape that was not a fixed point (now U+FFFD, as preprocessing makes it).
- **Not done**: `@counter-style`, the other predefined styles (greek, armenian,
  CJK…), `list-style-image`, `attr()`/quotes in `content`, a `::marker`'s own
  counter properties, markers on list items that are flex or grid items.

## 2026-10-25 (round 186) — percentage padding, and percentage margin-top/-bottom, were kept as 0; a fixed-height block lost its margin-top (defect, found by go-slides' `bg right` work)

Bibliography opened first: CSS Box 3 §5 and CSS 2.1 §8.3-8.4. Padding and margin
percentages, on all four sides (top and bottom included), refer to the WIDTH of the
containing block's content box. Before this, `padding-left:60%` and every other
percentage padding parsed to 0, and only `margin-left`/`margin-right` percentages
were resolved.

Fix: the cascade keeps a percentage side as a fraction (`css.Pct4`,
`Style.PaddingPct`, `Style.MarginVPct`; a later length or a shorthand reset clears
it) and `layout.resolvePercentEdges` turns it into pixels in `place` and `placeFloat`,
where the containing block's width is known, so nesting, `calc(100% - 40%)` and
`box-sizing:border-box` (the padding comes out of the width) all follow. The
shorthands, `padding-inline/-block` and `margin-block` are covered.

A second defect surfaced while testing `margin-top:20%`: a block with an explicit
`height` and no top border or padding dropped its own `margin-top` (px too), because
its content top was taken from before the collapsed margin was committed. It is
committed now (an empty box) or read from the first content edge.

Known limits, still 0: a percentage padding on a flex or grid item, an inline-block
(their layout is isolated from the containing block) and on `display:inline` boxes.

Follow-up (open): resolve those too. `layoutIsolated` would take the containing
block's width and resolve `PaddingPct`/`MarginVPct` there instead of clearing them;
inline boxes take their padding in the inline layout, not in `place`.

Tests: `fixtures_percent_edges_test.go` (four sides, shorthands, logical
properties, calc, overrides, border-box, margins, float, nested containing blocks,
isolated layouts).

## 2026-10-25 (round 185) — a word that filled its shrink-to-fit box exactly was broken inside it: gaia's inline `code` wrapped as "chapte" and "r" (defect, found by go-slides' examples gallery, go-slides/slides#53 follow-up)

Bibliography first (what was opened): CSS Sizing 3 §5 (max-content size, shrink-to-fit: `min(max(min-content, available), max-content)`); CSS Text 3 §8.2 (letter-spacing is added after each typographic character unit, so a text's width grows by that per unit: the layout already adds it to every measure of a run, `letterSpacingWidth`) and §5.5 (`overflow-wrap:break-word` breaks a word only when it does not fit its line); Chrome's LayoutUnit (1/64 px), which is why a browser never sees two sums of one text differ.

- **The report's hypothesis was wrong.** go-slides' gaia sets `letter-spacing:1.25px` on the section, which suggested a box measured without it and painted with it. It is not that: the code box sets `letter-spacing:0`, and the same deck with the section's spacing set to 0 breaks the same way. The box was sized exactly to its text: the debug output of `layoutNestedInlineBlock` and `splitOverlongWord` read a box content width of 117.619 px and a word of 117.619 px, equal to a thousandth.
- **Cause**: they are not equal to the last bit. An inline-block is given its max-content width as `preferredWidth(el) - edges` (padding and border added by one sum, taken off by another), and the line then measures the word again (`Measure` plus `letterSpacingWidth`). Two float64 sums of the same advances differ by an ulp, so `runW > maxW` was true for a word that fills its box, and `overflow-wrap:break-word` (set by gaia on the section, inherited into the box) broke it in two. `quote` fitted and `chapter` did not, by the luck of the bits. In default and uncover the same sums sent a word to a line of its own early ("a line breaks earlier than it needs to next to an inline code box").
- **Fix** (`layout/layout.go`): `fitEpsilon`, a sixty-fourth of a pixel, the precision of a browser's layout units, in the three fit tests of a line: `wrapOneLine`'s two (a run after others, a run alone) and `splitOverlongWord`'s loop. A run that reaches its line by less than that fits; nothing visible moves.
- Not changed, and by design (see `Fonts.Measure`): the raster painter draws with whole-pixel advances while the layout measures the fractional ones, so in a PNG a monospace word is up to a fifth of a pixel per glyph wider than its box; the vector PDF and PPTX draw at the measured advances. The padding of the code box absorbs it. Letter-spacing counts runes, where CSS Text counts typographic character units (grapheme clusters): a combining sequence is over-spaced; no trigger found.
- Tests: `fixtures_inlineblock_fit_test.go` (gaia's `code{display:inline-block;font-family:Roboto Mono}` in a paragraph of `overflow-wrap:break-word`, 24 to 48 px: the box is one line high, as it is with `white-space:nowrap`; it fails at 35 px (gaia's own size) and 37 px before the fix), `layout/fit_test.go` (a run an ulp, a hundredth, a sixty-fourth of a pixel too wide fits; a tenth does not; the same after another word).

## 2026-10-25 (round 184) — `calc()` over percentages was dropped, so Marp's `![bg right:40%]` drew its image on the left (defect, found by go-slides' examples gallery, go-slides/slides#53)

Bibliography first (what was opened): CSS Values and Units 4 §10.1 (a calculation of percentages is a percentage) and §10.9 (a `calc()` mixing types resolves at layout, against the property's percentage basis); CSS Box 3 §5 (a margin's percentage is of the containing block's width); Marpit's `advanced-background` style sheet and go-slides' own copy of it (`theme/pack.go`).

- **Cause**: Marpit offsets the background layer of a right split with `margin-left: calc(100% - var(--marpit-advanced-background-split, 50%))`, and the split is a custom property of the section (`40%`). After `var()` the value is `calc(100% - 40%)`. `css/calc.go` evaluated `calc()` at the text level and refused every percentage, so the declaration was dropped, the layer stood at the left edge, and the image of `right:40%` was drawn there, in the PNG, the vector PDF and the PPTX alike. (The HTML in a browser was right: the markup was always Marpit's.)
- **Fix** (`css/calc.go`): the parser carries a kind with each term, a number, a length or a percentage. A sum of percentages is a percentage (`calc(100% - 40%)` is `60%`), a percentage times or over a number is one, and the value goes on to the property, which resolves it at layout like a plain `60%`. Unchanged and still dropped: a percentage mixed with a length (`calc(100% - 10px)`: layout would have to resolve it), a percentage plus a bare number, percentage times percentage, a number over a percentage. A media query's `calc()` is pixels: a percentage there is still no breakpoint.
- Not touched, found on the way: a plain `padding-left:60%` is not resolved either (a percentage padding is the same width basis as a margin).
- Tests: `css/calc_test.go` (the sums, the refusals), `fixtures_bgsplit_test.go` (a 100px box in a 500px container, offset by 60% in nine spellings: plain, `calc()` of percentages, with `var()` and its fallback, in a flex container, after `all:initial`, under an attribute selector; the mixed case still dropped). It fails before the fix in all but the plain spelling.

## 2026-10-25 (round 183) — the vector PDF had no logical structure: a screen reader read it as a heap of words (feature, asked by go-slides)

Bibliography first (what was opened): ISO 32000-1:2008 14.7.2, 14.7.4.2-4, 14.8.2.2, 14.8.5.7 (Table 349), Table 321 and 14.9.2. veraPDF's PDF/UA-1 validation profile (ISO 14289-1 rules). Skia's `SkPDFTag.cpp` and `SkPDFDevice.cpp`, which tag Chrome's printed PDFs: a mark per element id, MCIDs per page, a parent tree, artifacts by kind.

- **paint**: `Mark` and `StructuredCanvas`. `PaintVector` says, before each drawing, which DOM node it belongs to.
  - Artifacts: box decoration (shadows, backgrounds, borders, inline fragments), generated content (`#pseudo`), `aria-hidden` subtrees, `user-select:none` text, and the bitmap of an element drawn as one (its text is drawn again over it as content).
  - A list marker is its item's label, decimal or drawn.
- **pdfcanvas**: `Tag`, `Mark`, `Finish`, `Element`, `TextOf`. Elements come from the DOM: ARIA role first, then the tag (HTML-AAM's mappings, ISO 32000-1's standard types).
  - Plain containers are transparent. `thead`/`tbody`/`tfoot` are too: a THead left without a TBody, when the body is clipped, fails PDF/UA.
  - A `TH` gets its `Scope`. An `<img alt="">` or an unlabelled `<svg>` is decoration.
  - An element is made at its first drawn content, after its ancestors, so the structure is in drawing order: the reading order for normal flow.
  - Marks open lazily at the next drawing, so no empty sequence is written, and anything drawn before the first mark is an artifact.
- **Link** gains `Node`.
- **go-pdfkit** v0.18.0/v0.19.0: structure tree, artifacts, tagged links, `Attributes`.
- Found while doing it: a decimal list marker went through `item`, which re-marked it as content of no node. `marker` now draws it under its caller's mark.
- Measured: veraPDF 1.30.3 `--flavour ua1` passes this round's test page and 12 of go-slides' 13 oracle decks. The 13th skips a heading level in its own source (H1 then H3), which PDF/UA forbids and which the author has to fix. Chrome's PDF of the same deck (marp-cli 4.5.1) fails 7 rules.
- Tests: `paint/mark_test.go` (every kind of mark), `pdfcanvas/tags_test.go` (the structure read back with go-pdfkit/reader; an untagged canvas marks nothing; veraPDF when installed).

## 2026-10-23 (round 182) — in PowerPoint and OpenDocument, a line that began with a smaller run was split into two text boxes in the middle of a sentence (defect, found by go-slides' callout notes after round 181)

- **officecanvas**: a text shape's baseline was its top plus its FIRST run's ascent. Once a larger word joined the line, the shape's top rose to that word's, so the computed baseline was above the text and the next word was refused, opening a second box: "2 Reads" / "the input.". The shape's ascent is now the largest of its runs' ascents (they share one baseline, and the top is the highest of theirs). Round 181 exposed it: a note's callout disc became the same line's first run once inline-blocks sat on the baseline.
- Tests: `officecanvas/officecanvas_test.go` `TestAMixedSizeLineIsOneBox`. It fails without the fix with exactly the split above.

## 2026-10-23 (round 181) — an inline-block's baseline was always its bottom edge, raising its text above the line's by its descent and half its leading (defect, found by go-slides' line numbers)

Bibliography first (what was opened): CSS 2.1 §10.8 ("The baseline of an 'inline-block' is the baseline of its last line box in the normal flow, unless it has either no in-flow line boxes or if its 'overflow' property has a computed value other than 'visible', in which case the baseline is the bottom margin edge") and §10.8.1 (A' = A + L/2 above the baseline, D' = D + L/2 below).

- **Layout** (`layout/inlineblock.go`, `layout/layout.go`): an inline-block's `Ascent` was `box.H` in every case, the spec's fallback for a box with no line box. It is now the baseline of its last in-flow line box (looking into its last in-flow children, skipping floats and absolutely positioned boxes), and the bottom edge only without one or when `overflow` is not `visible`. go-slides' line numbers, drawn as inline-blocks in a code block at line-height 1.45, sat 4.7 px above the code at 1280×720.
- Not done: the bottom *margin* edge (vertical margins of an inline-block are not part of its item here, as before); `display:inline-flex` keeps its own rule.
- Tests: `layout/inlineblock_baseline_test.go`: last line box, last of several lines, a line in a block child, no line box, `overflow:hidden`. The first three fail without the fix (Ascent 20 vs 8, 60 vs 48, 40 vs 28).

## 2026-10-26 (round 183) — imperative `attachShadow()`/`customElements.define()`, closed for the confirmed real trigger: an already-parsed element upgraded to attach a shadow tree cloned from a `<template>` (feature, engine#319)

Bibliography first: the HTML Standard's own "upgrade an element" algorithm (whatwg.org/multipage/custom-elements.html#upgrades) — specifically its per-realm construction-stack trick, which is what makes a custom element's `super()` call end up mutating an element that already exists rather than creating a new one. Confirmed the real trigger live (a fork survey): developer.apple.com's own vendor bundle defines a declarative-shadow-DOM polyfill custom element, AFTER the matching tag is already parsed into the static HTML, whose constructor calls `attachShadow()` then clones a `<template>`'s content into it.

- **`js/interfaces.go`**: `newInterfaceCtor`'s structural-interface branch (HTMLElement/Element/Node/…) now checks a new `b.pendingUpgrade` — when set, it returns that (the real element's own wrapper) instead of the fresh object goja's `[[Construct]]` already allocated for `call.This`, exactly mirroring the HTML spec's construction-stack trick: it makes `super()` inside a `class X extends HTMLElement` constructor, invoked during an upgrade, bind to the REAL element.
- **`js/customelements.go`** (new): `upgradeExisting`/`upgradeElement` — `customElements.define(name, ctor)` (`js/window.go`) now walks the document (light DOM and shadow trees) for elements already matching `name` and constructs each one with `b.pendingUpgrade` set to its wrapper, via `goja.AssertConstructor` so the real `[[Construct]]` semantics (and `super()`) run. A throwing/panicking constructor is contained (`recover` + `logf`), the same best-effort convention `callSafely` already uses for event callbacks — one bad custom element must not abort the rest of the script.
- **`js/dom.go`**: `attachShadow()`/`shadowRoot` added to every element wrapper. `attachShadow()` ignores its `mode` option (not recorded, matching the existing declarative-shadow-DOM policy) and is idempotent (a second call returns the same root rather than throwing or replacing it — no confirmed trigger needs the spec's `InvalidStateError`).
- **`dom/shadow.go`**: `(*ShadowRoot).AppendChild` — the imperative counterpart to `attachDeclarativeShadowRoots`'s own construction, used by the JS `ShadowRoot` wrapper's `appendChild`. Unwraps a `DocumentFragment` child per spec, same as the light-DOM `AppendChild`'s existing case — the confirmed trigger's own `<template>.content.cloneNode(true)` returns exactly that shape (a synthetic `#fragment` wrapping the real clone), and appending it without unwrapping left the fragment itself as the one shadow child, with the actual content one level too deep for `layout.renderedChildren` to ever reach (caught by the round's own first test run, not assumed).
- **Deliberately narrower than the full spec, because no confirmed trigger needs more**: only an element ALREADY in the document at `define()` time is upgraded — a later `document.createElement(name)` or a node inserted afterward is not; `observedAttributes`/`attributeChangedCallback`/`connectedCallback`/`disconnectedCallback` are not implemented; a constructor that itself calls `customElements.define()` reentrantly is not handled (this engine never upgrades two elements at once, so `pendingUpgrade` is a single slot, not the spec's own stack).
- Tests: `js/customelements_test.go` (the confirmed trigger shape end-to-end, checked against the real Go `dom.Node` tree — not just JS-side `console.log`s — plus a not-upgraded-without-a-match case, a throwing-constructor case, and attachShadow idempotency); `dom/shadow_test.go` (four new `ShadowRoot.AppendChild` cases, including the fragment-unwrap one that would have shipped silently broken without it — the FIRST test run caught the un-unwrapped case exactly as cloneNode's own `.content` handling predicted). `js/js_test.go`'s existing `TestCustomElementsRegistry` updated: `get()` now reflects what `define()` recorded, where it used to be an unconditional no-op.

## 2026-10-26 (round 182) — `oklab()`/`oklch()` were recognised only inside `color-mix()`, never as a plain colour value (feature, engine#318)

Bibliography first: CSS Color 4 §9.2/§9.4 (`w3.org/TR/css-color-4/#specifying-oklab-oklch`) for the exact grammar and percentage reference ranges (L: 0%=0.0/100%=1.0 for both; a/b: ±100%=±0.4; C: 0%=0.0/100%=0.4; H a `<hue>`, a bare number read as degrees) before writing any parsing code.

- **`css/value.go`**: `parseColor` now dispatches `oklab(`/`oklch(` to a new `parseOKColor`, which parses the three whitespace-separated components (each a `<number>` or `<percentage>`, scaled per the ranges above) and an optional `/ alpha` (reusing the existing `parseAlpha`), then calls `go-gfx/gfx/color`'s `OKLabToSRGB`/`OKLCHToSRGB` — the SAME conversion `colormix.go`'s `fromMix` already used for `color-mix(in oklab/oklch, ...)`, but previously unreachable from a bare colour value.
- **Confirmed live, not assumed**: the CSS Color 4 spec's own page (`w3.org/TR/css-color-4/`) uses both directly in inline `style` attributes — `oklch(65% 0.15 270)` on a custom property, and a three-stop `linear-gradient()` built entirely from `oklch()` colours. Re-rendered the exact gradient shape locally: the painted pixels differ meaningfully end-to-end (light lavender at one end, dark magenta at the other, consistent with the shared ~326.5° hue) rather than falling back to black or being dropped.
- **Not done**: `lab()`/`lch()` (CIE Lab — a different, not-yet-written conversion from Oklab's) and the `none` component keyword (CSS Color 4 §4.4) — no confirmed real-world trigger for either, dropped the same way any other unsupported value is in this codebase.
- Tests: `css/modern_test.go` (`TestParseColorOKLabOKLCH`, `...Cascade`) — the grey/white/black cases reuse the exact numbers `TestParseColorMix`'s `color-mix(in oklab/oklch, white, black)` cases already established at the same lightness, so a passing color-mix() case and a failing bare `oklch()` one would point at the new dispatch, not the shared conversion math.

## 2026-10-26 (round 181) — a `::before`/`::after` rule's own `background-image`/`mask-image` was never fetched, closing round 177's own explicitly-flagged follow-up (feature, engine#317)

Bibliography first: re-read round 177's own closing entry (this file) and round 55's Shadow DOM image-loading fix it cites as "the same shape" — both are the identical class of bug: a pre-layout walk reading `n.Children` directly, blind to content a LATER pass (layout) synthesises.

- **`images.go`**: `loadBackgroundImages`'s document-order walk now also collects a `background-image`/`mask-image` URL off `sm[n].Before`/`.After` (the ::before/::after computed styles `css.Style` already carries, round 177's own `computePseudoElement`), not only off `sm[n]` itself. No change to the fetch/decode/budget machinery below it — a pseudo-element's image competes for the same raster/vector budget as everything else, keyed by the same URL, deduplicated the same way.
- **Confirmed live, not assumed**: fetched developer.mozilla.org's current breadcrumb stylesheet directly (`styles-breadcrumbs.*.css`) rather than trusting this file's own prior citation of the rule (which turned out to describe a DIFFERENT page's pattern — see the corrected Known Gaps entry). The real, current rule: `.breadcrumbs li:not(:last-child):after{content:"";mask-image:url(chevron-right.svg);...}` — a plain `<li>`, no form control involved, so this is a genuine, reachable, non-form-control trigger for the fix (unlike the hamburger-menu/theme-toggle triggers round 177 named, both confirmed this round to be real `<button>` elements, still blocked by the separate atomic-box limit).
- **Verified two ways**: `bgimages_test.go` (`TestLoadBackgroundImagesPseudoElementMaskImage`, `...BackgroundImageDedup`, `...NoPseudoElementUnchanged`) exercises the fetch/collect layer directly; a separate pixel-level check (`RenderHTML` end-to-end, scratch repro, not committed) confirmed a `::after{content:"";background-image:url(...)}` rule's bitmap actually reaches the painted canvas, not just the fetch map.
- **Not done**: a form control's `::before`/`::after` (round 55-56's "atomic box" limit — unchanged); `background-position`/`background-size`/multiple layers on a pseudo-element were not specifically re-tested here, though they go through the exact same `css.Style` fields and paint path as an ordinary element's, so there is no reason to expect a difference.
- Tests: `bgimages_test.go` (3 new cases, all fail without the `images.go` change — confirmed by reading the diff, not re-run against a reverted copy, given the change is a 2-line addition with no branching to miss).

## 2026-10-22 (round 180) — `user-select:none` text was extracted from the vector PDF like any other (feature, found by go-slides' code callouts)

Bibliography first (what was opened): CSS UI 4 §6.1 (user-select: `auto | text | none | contain | all`, not inherited, but `auto`'s used value is `none` on `::before`/`::after` and under a parent whose used value is `none`), and Poppler's `pdftotext` 26.10, tried on a hand-written PDF: a run inside `/Span <</ActualText ()>> BDC … EMC` is drawn and not extracted.

- **Cascade** (`css/parse.go`, `css/cascade.go`, `css/value.go`): `user-select` and `-webkit-user-select` set `Style.UserSelectNone`; it passes to descendants (`inheritFrom`), `text`/`all`/`contain` take it back, `auto` follows the parent; a pseudo-element is none unless its own rules say `text`, `all` or `contain`.
- **Vector paint and PDF** (`paint/vector.go`, `paint/canvas.go`, `pdfcanvas`): such text becomes a `GlyphRun` marked `Unselectable`, which pdfcanvas wraps in an empty ActualText (go-pdfkit v0.17.0 `BeginActualText`). go-slides' line-number gutter and code-callout discs no longer come out of `pdftotext` interleaved with the code.
- Not done: the raster paths have no text layer, and the screen has no selection, so nothing changes there; `contain`'s containment is not modelled.
- Tests: `css/userselect_test.go`, `pdfcanvas/userselect_test.go` (the spans in the content stream; pdftotext's output when it is installed).

## 2026-10-22 (round 178) — SVG rasterisation's unbounded, area-proportional cost (rounds 168/169, deferred twice) closed by capping output resolution, per an explicit user decision (performance, engine#305)

FIDELITY.md's own entry had twice declined to pick between capping resolution or capping time, since either changes rendering output, not just performance — correctly left as a decision for a human, not guessed at. Asked directly this round; told to cap resolution.

`maxSVGRasterPixels` (`svg.go`) bounds the pixel AREA `svgToBitmap` (the screen-render path) actually rasterises, separate from the pre-existing `maxSVGDim` per-axis clamp — which, confirmed again, does nothing for the documented case (1158×598 ≈ 692K px is nowhere near either axis's 4096-px limit). Past the budget, `svgToBitmap` rasterises at a reduced resolution (same aspect ratio) but returns the SAME reported display size as always: only the actual bitmap shrinks, exactly the shape a differently-sized background image or `<img>` already legitimately has, so paint's existing `scaleTile`-based upscale (already exercised on every such size mismatch, no new code there) stretches it to the intended size at paint time.

Deliberately scoped to `svgToBitmap` alone, NOT the separately exported `RasterizeSVG`: that function's own doc comment and a pre-existing test (`TestRasterizeSVGHonoursTheSizeAsked`) explicitly promise the exact size asked for, because `pdfcanvas`/`officecanvas`'s print-quality vector export (#229) needs true resolution at the paper's own density — a PDF export job's time budget is not a screen render's, and silently downgrading its output would be a correctness regression dressed as a performance fix.

Found live, calibrated, not assumed: the two original real-world triggers (docs.docker.com's `dot-pattern.svg`, foundation.zurb.com) could not be re-measured — both pages have changed since and no longer serve the originally-profiled asset. A first synthetic repro (many small tiled circles, matching "~2657 tiny `<path>` elements") measured a SEPARATE, unrelated bottleneck instead (over a second at a mere 5,200 output pixels — per-path setup cost, not per-pixel area) before being discarded in favour of one matching the ACTUALLY profiled hot path: 20 stacked radial-gradient-filled layers over the full 1158×598 viewBox, landing in the same cost category round 168/169 identified (`rasterx.ColorFuncImage.At`, per-pixel gradient-colour sampling). Committed as a permanent benchmark pair (`BenchmarkSVGToBitmapGradientHeavy`/`...Uncapped`, rather than a one-off git-stash comparison) so the fix's real effect stays re-checkable: ~250ms capped vs ~683ms uncapped, ~2.7× for this repro, consistent with the predicted area ratio (budget/original area ≈ 0.36, and 683ms×0.36≈246ms).

Three new tests: the pre-existing `TestSVGToBitmapMaxDim` updated (it had silently assumed bitmap bounds always equal the reported size, true before this round — now correctly checks only that the AREA stays within budget), plus a dedicated area-cap regression (reported size unchanged, bitmap smaller, aspect ratio preserved, still correctly filled not blank) and a below-budget no-op guard (an ordinary icon-sized SVG takes the byte-identical path as before this cap existed).

**Honest limit, not claimed away**: this bounds worst-case cost, it does not eliminate it — a sufficiently expensive per-pixel SVG (more gradient layers, a costlier custom paint) can still cost meaningfully more than an ordinary icon even at the reduced resolution. No confirmed real-world case needs a tighter (or looser) budget than `maxSVGRasterPixels`'s current value yet; revisit if one turns up.

## 2026-10-22 (round 179) — a column of numbers, one per line, was as wide as all its digits side by side (max-content summed through forced breaks), and the `ch` unit did not exist (feature, found by go-slides' line numbers)

Bibliography first (what was opened): CSS Sizing 3 §5.1 (max-content size is the size when laid out in an infinitely sized containing block), CSS Text 3 §5 (a preserved newline under `white-space:pre`/`pre-wrap`/`pre-line`, or a `<br>`, is a forced line break) and CSS Values 4 §6.1.1 (`ch` is the used advance of the "0" glyph in the element's font; 0.5em where that is impossible or impractical to determine).

- **max-content is the widest line, not the sum** (`layout/floats.go`, `preferredWidth`): the loop that totals an inline container's items skipped a `LineBreak` item on purpose (round 42 kept "a `<br>` forced break is deliberately NOT a line boundary", pinned by `TestPreferredWidthInlineWithBreak`). Laid out in an infinite containing block only forced breaks break, so the widest line decides: a break now flushes the running line, exactly as `minContentWidth` always did. Found by go-slides' gutter of line numbers (`<span style="display:block;flex:none">1\n2\n…</span>` under `white-space:pre`), whose flex item came out 5 digits wide for a 1-digit column. `TestPreferredWidthInlineWithBreak` had encoded the old sum and now asserts the widest line (20, not 40). Tests: `layout/maxcontent_forced_break_test.go` (pre, pre-wrap, pre-line, `<br>`; widest line wherever it sits; a trailing newline adds no line) and, through the real fonts, `TestGutterWidthFromLinesAndCh`.
- **`ch`** (`css/chunit.go`, new): a length has no font when it is parsed, so the cascade rewrites each `<number>ch` of a declaration as pixels (after var() and device scaling, before calc()) against the element's own font: `Media.Advance` (the layout's `Measurer.Measure`, passed by `Engine.cascade`) measures "0" in the element's family, weight, style and font-size. Font family/weight/style are applied in a pass of their own after font-size, so a `width:3ch` sees a `font-family` declared after it. With no measurer, or a font that measures to nothing, it is the spec's 0.5em. Tests: `css/chunit_test.go` (spec definition, own font size, fallback, device scale, quoted text / `url()` / identifiers untouched) and `chunit_layout_test.go` (a box of 7ch is as wide as seven zeros in monospace, serif and sans-serif).
- **Not done**: `ic` (the advance of the CJK water ideograph "水"): the bundled faces carry no CJK, so the measurement would be the fallback glyph's; it stays unsupported rather than wrong. `ch` in the `font` shorthand's own size, and in a `font-size` declaration, uses the inherited font (the element's own is not known yet).
- Controls: removing the one-line `flushLine()` makes `TestMaxContentIsTheWidestLineNotTheSum` and `TestGutterWidthFromLinesAndCh` fail with the predicted widths (60 for 20; 48 for 9.6).

## 2026-10-21/22 (round 177) — `::before`/`::after` generated content, closed for the confirmed real trigger (a literal `content:"<string>"`), after six rounds (44/56/57/87) filed it as "comparable in scope to Shadow DOM's own multi-round build-out" (feature, engine#303)

Found, this time, while drafting a PLAN to implement the OTHER gap this same search turned up (round 38's own block-in-inline promotion) and discovering it had already shipped — see the docs correction above. Checking the Known Gaps section against the current code the same way (round 141/78's own precedent) before trusting any of its "not attempted" claims turned up a SECOND one: generated content really was still unimplemented, but the scope every prior round had assumed ("comparable to Shadow DOM") turned out to be bigger than the actual confirmed need, once read against the one thing every citing round actually wanted — a literal separator string.

Four pieces, each at the layer the existing architecture already has a slot for:

- `css.Style.Content`/`ContentSet` (`css/value.go`): a plain string, set only by a recognised `content:"<literal>"` value (`css/parse.go`'s `apply`). `attr()`/`counter()`/`url()`/`open-quote` and anything else leave `ContentSet` false — this engine's existing "an unsupported value drops the whole declaration" convention, not a special case.
- `Selector.MatchesPseudoElement(n, which)` (`css/selector.go`): a selector whose key compound carries `::before`/`::after` matches NOTHING through the ordinary `Matches`/`MatchesHost` entry points (by design, since round ~25/48's own `TestPseudoElementMatchesNothing` — a pseudo-element targets a generated box, not the real element, and applying its declarations to the real one is exactly the clearfix-collapse bug that test guards). This is a SEPARATE entry point, used only by `computePseudoElement`, that checks the SAME compound's other constraints (tag/class/attrs/`:not()`/…) against the real element, then delegates to the same `matchLeft` ancestor-combinator chain `MatchesHost` already uses.
- `computePseudoElement` (`css/cascade.go`): resolves a ::before/::after rule's own cascade (specificity, `!important`, `var()`/`calc()`/`light-dark()` — all reusing the exact machinery `computeElement` already has) with the real element's OWN finished style as the inheritance parent, per spec. No UA declarations, presentational hints or inline `style=""` apply to a generated box, so none of those three sources are consulted — deliberately narrower than `computeElement`. Returns "no pseudo-element at all" (not an empty one) when `content` never resolves, so every other layer only ever has to check one nil.
- Layout: `renderedChildren` (`layout/shadow.go`) — already documented as "the ONE substitution point every shadow-aware render walk goes through instead of reading n.Children directly" — synthesises a leading (`::before`) or trailing (`::after`) element when the cascade set one, caching it by the pseudo-style's own pointer identity so repeated layout passes over the same node (intrinsic-width estimation runs before actual placement) see the identical node. The synthetic element's own Display defaults to `inline` (CSS's real initial value), so it flows through `appendElementInline`'s EXISTING dispatch exactly like a real inline child would — including round 38's own block-promotion and round 63's own inline-block mechanism, with no new code, if a page's rule happens to set `display:block`/`inline-block` on it.

Verified live, not just in unit tests: a breadcrumb fixture (`<li class=sep>Home</li><li class=sep>Docs</li><li>Page</li>` with `li.sep::after{content:" > ";color:red;font-weight:bold}`) renders "Home > Docs > Page" with the separators in the right colour, at the right position, in the actual painted PNG.

**Deliberately NOT implemented**, each for a specific, checked reason rather than left open by omission:

- `attr()`/`counter()`/`url()`/multi-value `content` and the rest of the grammar: no confirmed trigger needs them; the existing "unsupported value drops the declaration" behaviour degrades safely (no pseudo-element, not garbage text).
- Whitespace inside a `content:"..."` string collapses the same way ordinary source HTML text does, because `appendWords` (shared with every other text node) does not distinguish the two — a real browser preserves a literal string's whitespace exactly. No confirmed trigger has leading/trailing whitespace in its separator (pkg.go.dev's own `content:">"` does not), so this is narrower-than-spec rather than wrong-for-any-known-page.
- **The reason MDN's own hamburger-icon and theme-toggle triggers (rounds 56/57) stay open even now**: a `background-image`/`mask-image` set on a `::before`/`::after` rule is not fetched. The engine's pre-layout image-loading walk (`images.go`) reads the real DOM's `n.Children` directly and runs BEFORE layout, so it never sees the synthetic node `renderedChildren` only builds once layout starts — the exact shape Shadow DOM's own background-image gap had (round 55), fixable the same way (teach that walk about `Style.Before`/`.After` too), not attempted this round. A form control's own `::before`/`::after` is separately still blocked by rounds 55-56's own disclosed "atomic box" limit regardless.

Coverage: `css` at 99.5% (the project floor, exactly — `computePseudoElement` and `MatchesPseudoElement` both individually at 100%, two new branch-only tests added directly against the uncovered lines a coverage diff named, not guessed at), `layout` at 100.0% (new `layout/content_test.go`, three tests: the confirmed trigger shape, the leading/trailing ordering, and a common-case guard that a page with no `content` rule anywhere gets the byte-identical inline-item stream it always did).

Also folded in, retroactively logged (shipped as v0.10.2 without a round-log entry at the time): **round 176 — `paint.Fonts.Measure` re-shaped text from scratch on every call, with nothing caching a result this engine's own layout algorithms (line-breaking, intrinsic-width estimation) call more than once BY DESIGN for the same run, and that a real page's own repeated strings (nav labels, button text) hit again independently.** Found profiling cia.gov (round 175's own subject, re-checked against current `main`): ~25% of total render CPU was in `Measure`, almost all in `Face.Shape`. A per-`(text, family, size, weight, italic)` cache (engine#301) dropped `Measure`/`Shape` out of the profile's top 25 entirely and cut total CPU for the same render ~34% (38s→25s); `BenchmarkMeasureRepeatedText` went from ~2–9µs/op to ~55–60ns/op (40–160×) for a repeated string. `Fonts` is already documented as "build one per render," so the only invalidation needed is on `RegisterFont` (a mid-render `@font-face` can change what a family/weight/italic resolves to) — it already clears the Face cache for the same reason and now clears this one too, caught by two pre-existing tests.

## 2026-10-19 — the "oksvg complex-path defect" of rounds 25, 64 and 79 was three separate defects in oksvg, none of them in this engine; all three are fixed in the fleet fork `go-gfx/oksvg` (v0.2.0), which the engine now uses (patch)

The octocat, the Wikipedia wordmark and tagline, and the MDN wordmark were filed as one third-party defect ("complex multi-subpath `<path>` mis-rendered", suspected to be the fill rule). Rebuilding each against Chrome headless (a standalone Go program, scratch only, pixels off by more than 40 in a channel) showed otherwise:

| | srwiley/oksvg | go-gfx/oksvg v0.2.0 | cause |
|---|---|---|---|
| GitHub octocat (20×20 mark, 600 px) | 24.0% | 0.3% | **arc flags written without separators**: `A9.564 9.564 0 0110 4.844` is flags 0 and 1 then x = 10 (SVG 1.1 §8.3.9); oksvg read `0110` as one number and lost the rest of the path |
| Wikipedia wordmark | 27.7% | 4.2% | **an arc command with several parameter sets** (`a10 10 0 0 1 5 5 8 8 0 0 1 3 3`): every arc was drawn with the first set's radii and flags (fixed in v0.1.0) |
| Wikipedia tagline | 18.9% | 6.8% | the same |

The 4–7% that remains on the two Wikipedia logos is edge anti-aliasing of dense text. The MDN wordmark of round 25 was not re-fetched, so it is not verified; it is a path of the same kind, so the arc fixes are the likely cause.

The fill rule was NOT the cause of any of them. It was a real defect too, found by the same sweep: oksvg ignored `fill-rule`, and rasterx's scanner has one rule (nonzero) and a no-op `SetWinding`. v0.2.0 implements `evenodd` (hand-written star and donuts, with a gradient, opacity and a clip, all under 0.4% off Chrome). None of the 4009 Twemoji graphics changes with it. Also fixed in the fork, with the earlier clip work: `clipPath`/`clip-path`/`clip-rule`.

Not a change to this engine's own code: `svg.go` imports `github.com/go-gfx/oksvg`; the known-gap entries below are annotated.

## 2026-10-18 (round 175) — an ordinary production page (not a crafted one) took tens of seconds to render because of a per-pixel allocation the interface boxing in `image.Image.At()` was hiding, in the `background-repeat` paint path (security audit, patch)

Rounds 166–173 were all about a page's content making the engine do unboundedly MUCH work (deeper recursion, more nodes, more memory). This one is different in kind: the amount of work was bounded and ordinary — a handful of `background-repeat` sections on one real page — but each unit of that work cost far more than it needed to, because of how the pixel data was being read.

- **Finding**: rendering `https://www.cia.gov/` (a real, non-malicious page) with JavaScript disabled still took over a minute, entirely in the paint phase, with the caller's `context.Context` deadline (45s in the bundled `bench/` comparison tool) never actually honoured — nothing in the hot loop ever checks it, the same "uninterruptible native call" shape round 172 found for `cloneNode`, but reached through plain CSS rather than a crafted script. CPU profiling (`runtime/pprof`, bounded windows, cross-checked against a live instrumented call counter) traced ~73% of total paint time into `paint.blitTileClipped`'s per-pixel loop, ~69% of THAT inside `image.(*RGBA).At()`/`image.Image.At()`. The tile painted by `background-repeat` was an `*image.NRGBA` (straight alpha); reading a pixel through the `image.Image`/`color.Color` interface (a) heap-allocates to box the returned `color.NRGBA` value (`runtime.convTnoptr`/`runtime.mallocgc` were 40%/30% of cumulative time) and (b) `color.NRGBA.RGBA()`'s per-channel `/0xff` to premultiply, paid on EVERY blitted destination pixel — and a repeating background stamps the same small tile across a potentially large box, so this cost scales with the painted AREA, not the tile's own (usually much smaller) pixel count. Isolated with a direct instrumented call counter (not just wall-clock, which this machine's concurrent load makes noisy): ~1,759 tile stamps averaging ~698,000 px each — ~1.2 BILLION pixel reads for one page, still climbing when stopped.
- **A second, independent bug shared the same line**: `blitTileClipped`'s `tile.At(x,y).RGBA()` call returns ALREADY alpha-premultiplied values (the `color.Color` interface's own contract), which the code then passed as `blendPixel`'s STRAIGHT-color argument alongside the same alpha as `cov` — double-applying alpha. Invisible for a fully opaque pixel (alpha=255, where premultiplied == straight), so no existing test caught it; a semi-transparent background-image pixel rendered too dark/washed-out, by roughly `(alpha/255)` too little.
- **Fixed**: `paintBgBitmap` and the object-fit image-tiling path now normalise `tile` to a concrete `*image.NRGBA` once (`toNRGBA`, mirroring the existing `toRGBA` helper used elsewhere in the same file), BEFORE the repeat loop rather than inside it. `blitTileClipped` now takes `*image.NRGBA` directly and reads `tile.NRGBAAt(x,y)` — a plain `Pix` slice read, no interface indirection, no allocation, no premultiply math — and passes the straight R/G/B/A through to `blendPixel` correctly, fixing the color bug as the same change.
- **Measured effect**: `BenchmarkPaintBgBitmapRepeat` (new; a 64×64 tile repeated across a 2000×2000 box, `-benchtime=10x -count=3` back-to-back on the same machine to control for its otherwise-noisy concurrent load) — before: ~300–900ms/op (also visibly higher-variance, consistent with GC pressure from the removed allocations); after: ~220–260ms/op.
- **Test**: `paint/background_paint_test.go`'s new `TestBlitTileClippedHalfTransparentPixel` pins the blend math for a half-transparent source pixel against a hand-computed straight-alpha-over-white result (semi-transparent pixels were exactly what no prior test exercised); `BenchmarkPaintBgBitmapRepeat` guards the performance going forward. All existing paint tests (opaque and fully-transparent source pixels) pass unchanged, confirming no visible regression for the common case.
- **Same honest caveat as every budget in this file**: this closes the specific cost multiplier `background-repeat` was triggering; it does not make paint context-aware. A sufficiently large single (non-repeated) image blit, or `blitImage`'s identical `src.At(...).RGBA()` pattern for a plain `<img>`/replaced element, still pays the interface-boxing cost once per element rather than once per repeat — unmeasured as a comparable real-world severity (no multiplier), left as a known, smaller instance of the same anti-pattern rather than folded into this round's fix.

## 2026-10-17 (round 173) — the SAME memory-exhaustion shape as round 172, but simpler to trigger: a single `innerHTML` assignment, no loop at all, found by generalizing the lesson rather than re-checking the same code path (security audit, patch)

Continuing round 172's own sweep (JS methods that can allocate unboundedly from a tree it already has) into the one other place a script routinely hands this engine a whole subtree at once: `innerHTML`/`outerHTML`/`insertAdjacentHTML`. All three ultimately call `dom.ParseFragment` (or, for the top-level page, `dom.Parse`), both of which share one core: `convertChildren`.

- **Finding**: `golang.org/x/net/html`'s own parser refuses markup nested past 512 levels, but that guard is about DEPTH — a WIDE document (millions of sibling elements, never nesting past a handful of levels) sails straight through it. `convertChildren` had no count of its own at all. Confirmed live, two ways: `root.innerHTML = "<div>x</div>".repeat(3000000)` — one statement, no loop, the repeated fragment a mere 13 bytes — allocated **~11.5GB of real memory in 15 seconds**; a 25-iteration `root.innerHTML = root.outerHTML + root.outerHTML` loop (the same doubling shape as round 172's `cloneNode` case, but through serialize-then-reparse instead of direct cloning) reached ~6GB.
- **Fixed**: `convertChildren` now carries a shared node-count `budget` (`maxParsedNodes = 200,000`, matching `js.maxCloneNodes`'s own value and reasoning — the same bug, one round earlier, for `cloneNode`'s bushy-growth case) threaded through both its own recursion and the `<template>` content-fragment branch. Past zero, remaining siblings/descendants are simply not converted — the real tree loses nodes near the bound rather than crashing or exhausting memory, the same degradation shape as every other budget cap in this codebase.
- **Measured effect**: the flat `repeat(3000000)` reproduction drops from ~11.5GB/15.4s to ~1.1GB/1.8s; the `outerHTML`-doubling loop drops from ~6GB/8.3s to ~1.4GB/3.5s.
- **Test**: `dom/mutate_test.go`'s `TestParseFragmentCapsTotalNodeCount` and `dom/dom_test.go`'s `TestParseCapsTotalNodeCount` (the shared `countTreeNodes` helper, new in `mutate_test.go`), each building a `maxParsedNodes+1000`-repeat string. `Parse`'s own count needed a `+1` margin over `ParseFragment`'s: `Parse` wraps `convertChildren`'s budgeted output in a synthetic `Document` node created BEFORE the budget starts counting, so the full tree is one node larger than the budget alone would suggest — confirmed precisely (it reported exactly `maxParsedNodes+1`, not a looser overshoot) before adjusting the assertion rather than second-guessing the fix.
- **Coverage**: `convertChildren` at 100%; `dom` package 98.4%→98.5% (confirmed via `git stash`, no regression — and this time stashing tracked and untracked paths SEPARATELY, per round 172's own named lesson, which held up cleanly).
- **Same honest caveat as round 172's own `cloneNode` fix**: this bounds one `Parse`/`ParseFragment` call, not a whole render — a script calling `innerHTML =` many times could still accumulate several×`maxParsedNodes` before the JS script timeout stops it. Bounded and linear now, not a hard per-render ceiling.

## 2026-10-17 (round 172) — closing round 171's own open lead: 25 lines of ordinary-looking script allocated ~21GB of REAL memory, and neither an existing heap watchdog nor this round's own depth cap could stop it (security audit, patch)

Round 171 flagged, but did not characterize, a 25-iteration `root.appendChild(root.cloneNode(true))` loop that rendered a 10.5-million-pixel-tall page without crashing — unclear at the time whether that was genuinely safe (sparse, unpainted memory) or just not yet pushed hard enough. Measured directly with `runtime.MemStats` before/after: **~19.75GB of real HeapAlloc, ~21GB of real Sys**, in under 9 seconds, from 25 ordinary DOM calls on a trivial page. Not sparse at all — `image.NewRGBA` eagerly allocates and zero-fills every byte, and `newCanvas`'s own fill pass then writes to all of it.

Two independent gaps, found while root-causing this:

- **The existing JS heap watchdog (`heapGuardedContext`, `MaxJSHeapBytes`, default 768MB) cannot stop this, regardless of how low its limit is set** — confirmed by setting it to 100MB and seeing IDENTICAL total memory usage to the 768MB default. Its `Interrupt()` only takes effect at the next bytecode instruction goja executes; a single `cloneNode(true)` call already in flight is one uninterruptible native Go call from goja's point of view, and that one call alone is what balloons memory once its input tree is already large. The watchdog is real and does fire (confirmed via `JSLog`), it is just structurally unable to abort a single native call once started.
- **Round 171's own `maxDOMWalkDepth` cap does not apply here either**: `root.appendChild(root.cloneNode(true))` builds a BUSHY tree (each clone nests a full copy of everything accumulated so far, one level deeper under root), so no single `cloneNode` call's own recursion ever exceeds the depth cap — the runaway dimension is TOTAL NODE COUNT, which doubles every iteration, not recursion depth, which stays modest.
- **Root cause of the actual memory number**: `newCanvas` (`engine.go`) sizes the output image from the laid-out content height alone, with no upper bound — a script can grow that height arbitrarily (here, to 13,288,016px), and `vpW × canvasH × 4 bytes` scales accordingly with nothing stopping it.

**Fixed two ways, addressing both the tree-size and the canvas-size dimensions**:
- `cloneNode` now carries a node-count `budget` (`maxCloneNodes = 200,000`) alongside its existing depth cap, shared across one call's whole recursion (the same shape as round 170's `expandFunctionalPseudosBudget`). Verified directly: node-count growth that was exactly doubling every iteration (4, 8, 16, … 262,144) becomes linear (+200,000/iteration) exactly once the budget is first exhausted, confirmed node-by-node with a throwaway counting test run against the real doubling loop.
- `newCanvas` gains `maxCanvasPixels = 200,000,000` (200 megapixels — 7× the tallest page in this engine's own 500-page bench corpus, Wikipedia at ~29M pixels, while bounding worst-case allocation to ~800MB instead of tens of GB), clamping `canvasH` rather than failing the render.
- **Together**: the original 21GB reproduction now uses ~5.6GB (a ~4× reduction) at 25 iterations and ~7.8GB at 40 iterations — confirmed LINEAR in iteration count now, not exponential, meaning an attacker needs proportionally more explicit script statements for more damage rather than getting free exponential growth, and the existing JS script timeout (5s default) bounds how many such statements can run at all.
- **Test**: `js/dom_test.go`'s `TestCloneNodeCapsTotalNodeCountPerCall` and `TestCloneNodeCapsTotalNodeCountInsideTemplateContent` (the `<template>` content-fragment clone is a separate loop sharing the same budget); new file `canvas_bomb_test.go` (matching the existing `images_bomb_test.go` naming), `TestNewCanvasCapsTotalPixelCount`, `TestNewCanvasOrdinaryHeightUnaffected`, `TestNewCanvasClampFloorHoldsWhenViewportAloneExceedsCap`.
- **Coverage**: both `newCanvas` and `cloneNodeDepth` land at 100% (confirmed clean baseline beforehand — the two added branches were the only gaps).
- **Not fully closed, documented honestly**: a sufficiently long-running script could still accumulate several×`maxCloneNodes` across many separate `cloneNode` calls before the 5-second script timeout stops it — bounded and linear now, not literally unbounded, but not a hard per-render ceiling either. A session-wide budget shared across every `cloneNode` call in one render would close that remaining gap; not attempted this round, since the per-call budget already converts the practically-unbounded exponential case into a bounded, attacker-costly linear one.
- Also hit, again, the same `git stash` pathspec trap from round 171's own write-up (mixing a tracked and an untracked path in one `git stash push --` call aborts the whole push silently, and a subsequent blind `pop` can then resurface an unrelated OLDER stash instead) — the canonical bench baseline reappeared modified a second time mid-round from exactly this, fixed the same way (a clean, single-type-of-path stash), but worth naming the trap explicitly this time rather than just the symptom.

## 2026-10-12 (round 171) — `node.cloneNode()`/`node.isEqualNode()` recursed on a script-built tree independently of round 166's cascade/layout caps — a script reaches them directly, bypassing that pipeline entirely (security audit, patch)

Continuing the same sweep as round 170, this time across JS-reachable DOM methods rather than CSS parsers: `js/dom.go`'s `isEqualNode` (structural comparison, `node.isEqualNode(other)`) and `cloneNode` (`node.cloneNode(deep)`, also `document.importNode`) each recurse over a node's own `Children` with no depth check — and critically, round 166's own fix (capping the CSS cascade and layout walks) does **not** cover this: those caps guard the pipeline a page's tree flows through automatically during rendering, while these two methods are called directly by a script on whatever tree it already has, independently of whether that tree is ever rendered at all.

- **Finding**: confirmed with a 10,000-deep synthetic chain (built directly, bypassing `dom.Parse`'s 512-level refusal, the same `createElement`+`appendChild`-loop shape round 166 already established has no depth check of its own) that both functions recurse unboundedly. Checked two JS-reachable ways an attacker might build a tree deep enough to matter without excessive `createElement`/`appendChild` FFI overhead (a direct loop, and a `cloneNode`-and-reinsert doubling trick) — neither reached a dangerous depth within a reasonable script budget in practice, consistent with round 166's own finding that JS-driven tree construction is inherently throttled by call overhead. That made this lower urgency than round 166's or round 170's own findings, but the underlying gap (recursion with literally no bound, reachable in principle regardless of how hard it is to construct the input today) is the same category this session has treated as worth closing on principle: `recover()` cannot catch a Go stack overflow regardless of how the input arrived.
- **Fixed**: both functions now take a `depth` parameter, capped at a new `maxDOMWalkDepth = 512` (matching the HTML parser's own bound, same reasoning as `css.maxTreeDepth`/`layout.maxLayoutDepth` from round 166). Past the cap: `isEqualNode` reports `false` (two trees too deep to safely compare are deemed unequal, never crashing); `cloneNode` stops descending and returns a real, correctly-typed node with an empty subtree rather than failing the whole call.
- **Test**: `js/dom_test.go` (new file) — `TestIsEqualNodeDoesNotCrashOnPathologicallyDeepTree`, `TestIsEqualNodeDeepMismatchPastCapIsNotEqual`, `TestCloneNodeDoesNotCrashOnPathologicallyDeepTree`, each using a 10,000-deep synthetic chain.
- **Coverage**: `js` package stays at its 95.9% baseline; the two uncovered statements in `isEqualNode` (an attribute-count mismatch, a children-count mismatch) are pre-existing gaps this round's depth-cap addition did not touch.
- Also discovered and fixed in passing: the canonical ten-page `bench/REPORT.md`/`bench/results.json`/two montage PNGs had been silently overwritten with 500-page-corpus data at some earlier point in this session (the exact round-166 mistake recurring, likely from before that round's own "always pass distinct -report/-json/-out" fix, sitting unstaged and unnoticed through several subsequent commits since `git commit` without `-a` never touched it). Restored via `git stash` (recoverable, not discarded) rather than `git checkout --`.

## 2026-10-11 (round 170) — three text-level CSS parsers (`calc()`, `:not()`, `:is()`) recursed on attacker-controlled nesting depth with no guard, the same class of bug round 166 fixed for DOM trees — one of them exponential, not merely deep recursion (security audit, patch)

Continuing the "audit de sécurité" pass after round 166's DOM-depth fix: checked whether any OTHER recursive-descent parser in this codebase has the same shape — unbounded recursion keyed to attacker-controlled input depth, with no equivalent to the HTML parser's own 512-node guard. `css/calc.go`'s `calcParser` does: its grammar is `expr := term (('+'|'-') term)*`, `term := factor (('*'|'/') factor)*`, `factor := number[unit] | '(' expr ')'` — a textbook mutually-recursive expression grammar, and `factor`'s own paren branch recurses straight back into `expr` with no depth check at all.

- **Finding**: unlike the DOM-depth case, nothing upstream bounds how many parens a single `calc(...)` value's SOURCE TEXT can carry — a `style="width:calc((((...1...))))"` attribute on the main document (itself capped only at 32MB — see `engine.go`'s own body-size cap) can carry millions of them without ever touching a separate parser-level guard the way deep HTML *element* nesting does (x/net/html's own 512-node limit has no equivalent for a single attribute VALUE's own text). Confirmed live: a synthetic 3,000,000-deep `calc((((...1...))))` crashes with `fatal error: stack overflow` (not a panic — `recover()` cannot catch it) where 1,000,000 does not yet.
- **Fixed**: `calcParser` now carries a `depth` counter, incremented/decremented around each paren group in `factor`, capped at `maxCalcDepth` (256 — enormously generous against the measured 1,000,000–3,000,000 crash threshold, and far past anything a real `calc()` expression ever nests in practice). Past the cap, `factor` returns its own existing "unparseable" failure (`0, false, false`) — the exact same degradation this parser already uses for any other malformed input — rather than recursing further.
- **Test**: `TestResolveCalcDoesNotCrashOnPathologicallyDeepParens` — a 10,000-deep synthetic value (comfortably past the 256 cap, cheap to build and run) asserts `resolveCalc` reports failure instead of crashing.
- **Coverage**: `css` package coverage unaffected (99.5% before and after, confirmed via `git stash`); `factor`'s own coverage actually rises slightly (92.0%→95.0%) since the new depth-cap branch is exercised by the new test — the two statements it remains short of (a `ParseFloat` failure path, a numeric-sign-prefix path) are pre-existing gaps this round did not touch.

Continuing the same check into `css/selector.go` found two MORE instances of the same general class, one of them more severe than `calc()`'s:

- **`:not(:not(:not(...)))` nesting**: `parseSimple` (compound parsing), `parseSimpleSelectorList` (`:not`/`:host` arguments) and `parseHasSelectorList` (`:has` arguments) form one mutually recursive cycle with no depth check, the exact same shape as `calc()`'s — and WORSE than a plain stack overflow at smaller scale: each level's own string-scanning cost compounds, so the cost is closer to quadratic in nesting depth than linear. Confirmed live: a 500,000-deep synthetic `:not(:not(...))` chain that used to time out past 60 seconds unbounded now completes in ~2.5s. Fixed the same way as `calc()`: a `depth` parameter threaded through all three functions, capped at `maxSelectorNestingDepth` (256, matching `maxCalcDepth` — both guard the identical shape of bug). Test: `TestParseSimpleDoesNotCrashOnPathologicallyDeepNotNesting`.
- **`:is(:is(...),:is(...))` nesting — a DIFFERENT, more severe failure mode: exponential output, not merely deep recursion.** `expandFunctionalPseudos` (the `:is()`/`:where()`/`:matches()` expander) doubles its OUTPUT COUNT at every level a comma-containing wrapper is nested inside another one of itself: `n` levels of self-nesting give `2^n` alternatives from a selector whose own TEXT nesting depth is just `n`, not `2^n` — so even a depth cap alone would not have been enough; a selector that LOOKS only moderately nested can already be computationally enormous. Confirmed live: 20 levels of this shape — elaborate-looking but not obviously malicious — already produces 1,048,576 expanded alternatives and takes several seconds; one size class further and this becomes a real denial of service from a selector well within this engine's existing page-size limits. Fixed with a shared output BUDGET (not a depth counter) threaded through the whole recursive expansion (`expandFunctionalPseudosBudget`, `maxSelectorExpansions = 4096`): once the total number of alternatives produced across the ENTIRE expansion hits the cap, expansion stops and the selector list is truncated — same "reduce, don't drop" degradation this package already uses elsewhere — rather than continuing to multiply. Test: `TestExpandFunctionalPseudosCapsCombinatorialBlowup` (plus a direct white-box test for the budget-already-exhausted entry guard, a real but awkward-to-drive-naturally path).
- Coverage: both new functions' depth/budget-cap branches land at 100%; `css` package stays at its 99.5% baseline.

## 2026-10-11 (round 169) — go.mod's own declared Go version had drifted behind CI's, making `govulncheck` report 8 already-patched stdlib vulnerabilities as live; `golang.org/x/net` bumped too (security audit, patch)

A concurrent session's PR #270 moved CI's `GO_VERSION` from 1.27.1 to 1.27.2 (a security release, 13 stdlib advisories) but — correctly, since a `go` directive is a floor, not a pin — left `go.mod`'s own `go 1.27.1` line untouched. Re-reading this session's own notes per the standing "relis tes notes" instruction, then re-running `govulncheck` to confirm the new CI pin actually closed something, surfaced a real gap: `govulncheck ./...` reported 8 reachable stdlib vulnerabilities (GO-2026-6605, -6603, -6607, -6608, and others) "Found in: net/http@go1.27.1, Fixed in: go1.27.2" — even though the installed toolchain is 1.27.2. `govulncheck -version` showed why: it had resolved its own subprocess `go` to literally **1.27.1**, not the 1.27.2 my interactive shell's `go version` reported — a live, concrete instance of the exact trap an earlier round's own memory note already names ("Go's `stable` tag can silently point at a different minor than you think — measure with the CI's own toolchain, not an assumption"), except here the drift was go.mod's own stale `go` directive feeding govulncheck's stdlib-vulnerability baseline, not a `stable`-tag alias.

- **Fixed**: `go.mod`'s `go 1.27.1` → `go 1.27.2`, matching CI's own pin — Go's toolchain management already auto-selects whatever satisfies the directive, so this changes nothing about what actually gets built; it only corrects what `govulncheck` (and any other tool reading the directive) believes the floor is. Re-ran `govulncheck ./...`: 0 reachable vulnerabilities, down from 8.
- **Also bumped while auditing**: `golang.org/x/net` v0.59.0 → v0.61.0 (`go get` + `go mod tidy`), closing 5 further advisories (GO-2026-6617/6612/6611/6610/6603, all HTTP/2 **server**-side — this engine is a client, so `govulncheck` already confirmed none were reachable either way; bumped anyway as cheap defense-in-depth rather than leaving a known-vulnerable version pinned for no reason). Pulled `golang.org/x/crypto` v0.57.0→v0.58.0, `x/sys` v0.48.0→v0.49.0, `x/text` v0.42.0→v0.43.0 along as transitive deps of the same `go mod tidy`.
- **Re-confirmed, not re-litigated**: `golang.org/x/crypto`'s `openpgp` package (GO-2026-5932, round 162's own finding) remains the one advisory `govulncheck` still lists as present-but-unreachable — the package is unmaintained by design with no fix version to bump to, not something this bump could close, and still isn't called by this engine's own code. Consistent with round 162; no new action.
- `go build ./...` and `go test ./...` both green on the bumped dependencies; no behaviour or API change, so this is a patch.

## 2026-10-10 (round 168) — the 500-page bench corpus completed a full run for the first time (both crash fixes held); one real performance bug found and fixed along the way: font-face fallback candidates in one slot were tried one at a time, not concurrently (engine#267, patch)

The 500-page run relaunched at the end of round 167 (both crash fixes in place) ran to completion: 500/500 pages, `wrote results-500.json and REPORT-500.md`, no crash — the first time this corpus has ever finished. Of the 96 pages marked "failed", 95 failed for BOTH webengine and Chrome (dead URLs, DNS, timeouts — confirmed via each entry's own `note` field) and exactly 1 was a transient DNS flake on this machine; zero are engine bugs. Ten-odd pages (`azure.microsoft.com`, `www.cs.cmu.edu`, `www.cia.gov`, `aws.amazon.com`, the `gitlab.com`/`about.gitlab.com` family, `foundation.zurb.com`, `www.lemonde.fr`) rendered 10-20× slower than Chrome (20-45s vs 1.5-5s), which is not a crash but is squarely what "mesure... améliore les performances" asks for.

- **Investigation**: profiled `www.cs.cmu.edu` (45s) first since its page is small and simple — the CPU profile showed almost no computation (220ms of samples over 45s wall time), ruling out a CPU-bound layout/paint cost. Raising `maxPerHostConcurrency` (transport.go) from 2 to 6 made ZERO measurable difference (45.09s both times, suspiciously identical) — a useful negative result, not a dead end: it meant the per-host semaphore wasn't the active bottleneck for whatever was actually slow, so the change was reverted rather than shipped on a hunch. Instrumented `e.Client.Transport` directly (a temporary wrapper logging every request's URL, status and duration, in a scratch harness, not committed) with `DisableJS` set to rule out JS first: the slow pattern persisted with JS off, isolating it to the fetch layer.
- **Root cause**: cs.cmu.edu's own Font Awesome v4-compatibility shim (`fa/css/all-*.css`) declares five separate `@font-face` rules under the legacy family `'FontAwesome'`, all `font-weight: 400` with no `font-style`, differentiated ONLY by `unicode-range` (one per icon subset: solid, brands, regular, and a `fa-v4compatibility` catch-all). This engine has no `unicode-range` model at all — `css.FontFace` carries no such field — so `LoadFontFaces`'s own slot key (`family + italic + weight`) treats all five as fallback alternatives of ONE logical face, exactly the shape round 151 already parallelizes ACROSS (each slot loads concurrently) but NOT within: `loadOneFontFace`'s per-slot candidate loop tries them one at a time, stopping only at a success. cs.cmu.edu's own `/webfonts/` path was returning HTTP 500 for every one of them, and the ORIGIN took the full ~6.4s doing it — so the render serially burned through up to 5 candidates × 2 format fallbacks (woff2, then ttf) ≈ 50+ seconds of pure sequential failure, for a resource the page's own markup never asked to be tried one-by-one; a real browser's own unicode-range-aware subsetting would have made this moot, but even without implementing that, nothing requires trying candidate rules in series once declaration order is preserved by sorting on completion rather than by waiting in turn.
- **Fixed**: `LoadFontFaces`'s per-slot loop now fires every DISTINCT candidate concurrently (deduped by a new `srcsKey` — the same src list parsed twice, e.g. the same stylesheet named twice, still fetches once) and picks the lowest-declaration-order success after they all finish, instead of walking the list waiting for each failure in turn. `e.Client`'s existing per-host semaphore (`maxPerHostConcurrency`, round 57/58, left at 2 — the round-152/153-measured value, re-confirmed still right for Wikimedia, not re-opened by this round) still bounds real network concurrency, so this is a bounded, not unlimited, speedup: candidates now batch through that cap rather than waiting out every predecessor's own fetch one at a time.
- **Test**: `TestLoadFontFacesTriesSlotCandidatesConcurrentlyNotSequentially` — a local server with 5 slow-then-500 candidates plus 1 fast-success candidate declared LAST in one slot, deliberately shaped like the live cs.cmu.edu case. Stash-verified: ~460ms with the fix (bounded by the per-host cap, confirmed deterministic across 3 runs) vs ~760ms on the unfixed code (sequential), a measured, reproducible, and reliably-separated difference, not assumed from the live site alone (which could not be cleanly re-measured after this round's own repeated testing had, by its own escalating response times, visibly triggered the origin's own rate-limiting/anti-scraping defenses — an honest limitation of live-site verification for this specific fix, mitigated by the deterministic local-server test instead).
- **Coverage**: `fontfaces.go`'s `LoadFontFaces` and the new `srcsKey` both land at 100%; the top-level `engine` package moves from a pre-existing 96.1% baseline (confirmed via `git stash`, unrelated gaps) to 96.2% — no regression.
- **Not fixed this round, flagged honestly**: `unicode-range` itself remains unmodelled (this fix removes the serialization cost of the collision, not the collision — all five rules still compete for one slot, and whichever succeeds first wins for the WHOLE family+weight regardless of which glyphs it actually covers; a page relying on Font Awesome's REAL icon-subset behavior could still render the wrong glyphs, just not slowly). `cs.cmu.edu` itself could not be cleanly re-measured after this fix landed — the live-site contamination described above persisted (still ~45s on a careful single re-check well after the fix merged), consistent with the origin's own throttling outlasting this session rather than the fix being ineffective (the deterministic local test is the real evidence it works). Checked ONE other slow page from the same bench run, `foundation.zurb.com` (24.8s in the run, not yet touched by any of this round's live testing, so uncontaminated): still ~40s after the fix, and — checked directly rather than assumed — `DisableJS=true` dropped it to ~6.2s on the SAME page. **Correcting the round's own earlier guess**: `cs.cmu.edu`'s shape is not a shared cause across the other ~9 slow pages. `foundation.zurb.com`'s own cause (round 169's follow-up CPU profiling pinned it precisely: SVG rasterisation of content the JS-settled DOM reveals that a no-JS render never has to pay for — see this file's "Known gaps" section) is distinct from both the font-face bug this round fixed AND from JS execution being slow in CPU terms.

## 2026-10-09 (round 167) — the interrupted 500-page bench run (round 166's own queued work) found a second real crash: a lone CSS Grid item using `grid-row:1 / -1` with no explicit row template panicked on an empty row-offset slice (engine#266, patch)

Round 166's fix shipped, then the still-queued 500-page bench run (launched before that fix, to measure the round-165 rotate fix) was resumed to see it through. It crashed again, on page 406: `https://www.lego.com/`, with `panic: runtime error: index out of range [0] with length 0` in `layout.grid` at `rowY[it.r0]`. Reproduced directly with `cmd/render` against the live URL, independent of the bench harness.

- **Root cause**: `resolveAxisPlacement` (grid item row/column placement) resolves a pair of explicit grid lines (e.g. `grid-row:1 / -1`, the common "span the whole axis" idiom — Tailwind's `row-span-full` compiles to exactly this) by normalising both lines, swapping if needed so `end >= start`, then clamping only the *start* to be non-negative (a track index can't be negative) — without re-checking that the span survived the clamp. With no `grid-template-rows` at all (a grid relying purely on implicit/auto rows, nTracks=-1 so the negative end line resolves against nothing), `1 / -1` normalises to start=0, end=-2, swaps to start=-2, end=0 (a valid, non-empty `[-2,0)` span) — then clamping start alone to 0 left end at 0 too: a degenerate **zero-track span**. CSS Grid's own spec (§8.3) guarantees an item always occupies at least one track per axis; this was a real divergence from it, with a concrete crash consequence: with this as a grid's *only* item (a second, plainly-auto-placed sibling would mask the bug — its own default 1-row span raises `nRows` regardless), `nRows` never rises above 0, so `sizeRows`/`trackOffsets` return empty slices, and indexing `rowY[it.r0]` panics.
- **Fixed**: `resolveAxisPlacement`'s explicit-start-and-end case now re-checks `end <= clampedStart` *after* the clamp and widens the span back to at least 1 rather than leaving it collapsed — the same "span must stay at least 1" invariant the function's other four cases already guarantee unconditionally.
- **Test**: `TestGridRowSpanFullWithNoExplicitRowsDoesNotCrash` (`layout/grid_test.go`) — a single-item grid with no `grid-template-rows`, `grid-row:1 / -1`. Stash-verified against the unfixed code (the exact same discipline round 166 and the round-165 rotate fix both used): it panics with the identical trace without the fix, passes with it.
- **Coverage**: `layout` stayed at 100% (the fixed branch is reached unconditionally whenever this case executes at all — no separate dead branch was introduced).

## 2026-10-07 (round 166) — a script-built DOM tree far deeper than any real page crashed every whole-document tree walk with an unrecoverable stack overflow, not a panic (security audit, patch)

Continuing this session's "audit de sécurité" pass after the rotate-crash fix below: checked for ReDoS and stack-overflow risk from pathological input. Go's `regexp` package is RE2-based (confirmed: every custom pattern in this repo — `svg.go`, `css/supports.go`, `css/selector.go`, `css/container.go`, `css/parse.go` — compiles under `regexp.MustCompile`, never a backtracking engine), so ReDoS is structurally impossible here, same category of finding as round 161's XXE check. Stack-overflow risk was real.

- **Finding**: `golang.org/x/net/html` already refuses to *parse* markup nested past 512 levels (`html: open stack of elements exceeds 512 nodes`), so a page's raw HTML can never build a pathologically deep tree. But that guard lives in the parser alone — `dom.AppendChild`/`InsertBefore` have no depth check of their own, by design (they are generic tree-mutation primitives, not a parser), so a page's own `<script>` can still build an arbitrarily deep tree at runtime (`document.createElement` + `appendChild` in a loop). Built a 500,000-deep tree directly in Go (bypassing both the HTML parser's cap and the 5-second JS script budget, to isolate the question precisely) and ran it through `css.Cascade` → `layout.LayoutDocumentViewport` → `paint.Paint`: it died with `fatal error: stack overflow` inside `css.CascadeMediaContainers`'s own recursive walk, somewhere between 150,000 and 200,000 levels. Unlike a panic, a Go stack overflow is **not recoverable** — `recover()` cannot catch it, so it kills the whole embedding process, not just the one render. Four separate unbounded recursive walks were found and fixed: `css.StylesheetLinks` (the very first DOM walk in the whole render pipeline), `collectAuthorRulesFrom`'s `styleElementText`, `css.Cascade`'s own element walk, and `layout`'s `place`/`contents` mutual recursion (paint's own recursion over the already-built `layout.Box` tree needs no separate cap: it is naturally bounded once layout's is in place, since paint only ever walks `Box.Children`, which layout's cap already keeps shallow).
- **Fixed**: each of the four walks now carries (or, for `layouter.place`, already carried as a natural per-call-site value) a depth counter, capped at 512 — reusing the HTML parser's own empirically-confirmed bound, which is both consistent with it and enormously generous relative to the measured 150,000–200,000 crash threshold. Past the cap, a walk simply stops descending (the subtree is left unstyled / un-laid-out, as if it were not there) rather than erroring — the same "cap and move on" shape this codebase already uses for `@import` cycles (`maxImportDepth`, `engine.go`) and `var()` cycles (`maxVarDepth`, `css/vars.go`). `layout.place` returns a minimal childless box past its own cap, using the same non-nil default style every `Box` already carries when passed a nil `Style`.
- **Test**: `TestCascadeDoesNotCrashOnAPathologicallyDeepTree` and `TestStylesheetLinksDoesNotCrashOnAPathologicallyDeepTree` (`css`), `TestLayoutDoesNotCrashOnAPathologicallyDeepTree` (`layout`) — each hand-builds a 10,000-level chain (bypassing `dom.Parse`, which would refuse it at 512 anyway) and confirms the walk returns instead of crashing. The layout test needed a hand-built `StyleMap` giving every node `display:block`, not `css.Cascade`'s own output: `Cascade` has the same cap, so its StyleMap would leave everything past ~512 unstyled, and `layout.hasBlockLevelChild` treats a nil style as "not block-level" — stopping the recursion there for a different reason, before layout's own cap ever needed to act. Building the map by hand isolates the two caps so each is exercises by its own test, not accidentally covered by the other one firing first.
- **Coverage**: `layout` initially dropped to 99.9% (the new cap branch in `place` unreached) then 100.0% once the `StyleMap` was fixed to actually drive recursion that deep (the first attempt, with an empty `StyleMap`, never recursed past the root at all — `hasBlockLevelChild` treated every unstyled child as inline). `css` is unaffected: its pre-existing 99.5% floor (several unrelated functions in `selector.go`, `background.go`, `calc.go`, `gradient_sample.go`, `parse.go`) predates this change, confirmed identical before and after via `git stash`.

## 2026-10-07 (round 165) — a 500-page bench sweep found a real renderer crash: `transform:rotate()` on a box with an overflowing descendant wrote past its own offscreen buffer (engine#263, patch)

Building a 500-page corpus (268 distinct sites, at most four pages each, round 164's own planned expansion) and running the bench against it crashed on page 21: `https://www.smashingmagazine.com/2018/10/mobile-app-retention-rate/`, with `panic: runtime error: index out of range [6339531] with length 6336512` inside `blendPixel`, reached via `blitImage` → `paintItem` → `paintRotated`. Reproduced directly with `cmd/render` against the live URL, no bench harness involved — a real, user-facing crash, not a test artefact.

- **Root cause**: `paintRotated` (round 145) sized its offscreen buffer (`tmp`) using only the rotated box's own border box (`rectOf(box)`), trusting it to bound everything `paintBoxContent`'s recursion could write into that buffer. But a descendant can paint outside its ancestor's own `Y+H` — a shrink-wrapped float, a negative margin, a marker, an outset box-shadow — exactly the case `subtreeExtent` (the filter/opacity/mask-image group path's own ink-bounds helper, already in this file) exists to catch. `paintRotated` never used it. `blitImage`'s own per-pixel write (`blendPixel` → `dst.PixOffset` → `dst.Pix[i]`) has no bounds check beyond the ancestor clip rectangle, which is NOT scoped down to `tmp`'s own smaller height — so a descendant placed between the box's own bottom and the real canvas's bottom silently wrote past `tmp`'s allocation and crashed the whole render.
- **Fixed**: `tmp`'s height now comes from `subtreeExtent(box).Intersect(dst.Rect).Max.Y` instead of `bx.Max.Y` alone. `subtreeExtent`'s own base rectangle already covers the box's full border box unconditionally, so the result is provably never shorter than before (verified by reasoning, not just testing: `bx ⊆ dst.Rect` and `bx ⊆ subtreeExtent(box)`, so their intersection already contains `bx`) — no separate floor/clamp needed, and the coverage gate confirmed that by flagging the clamp branch I initially wrote as dead code, which was deleted rather than tested around (round 143/144's own established practice). The final rotated image's own disclosed scope (content outside the box's own border box is not captured into what gets rotated) is unchanged — only the OFFSCREEN BUFFER'S allocation grows to stop the crash; what ends up in the visible output does not.
