- **Test**: `TestRotateDoesNotCrashWhenAChildOverflowsTheBoxBottom`, a hand-built box tree with a rotated box whose child's own border box extends below the rotated box's own bottom, painting an opaque inline image (routed through the exact `paintItem → blitImage → blendPixel` path the real crash trace showed — a transparent test image would have silently skipped the vulnerable write via `blendPixel`'s own zero-coverage short-circuit, which is what made my first two attempts at this test pass even against the unfixed code; caught by deliberately stash-verifying against the OLD code and seeing it ALSO pass, the same "a passing test proves nothing until it fails the right way" discipline this session has used before). Stash-verified: reverting `paint.go` alone reproduces the real panic, with the identical `paintBox → paintBoxContent → blendPixel` stack.
- **Coverage**: `paint` initially dropped to 99.9% on the clamp branch described above; fixed by deleting the unreachable code, restoring the 100% floor on the first attempt after that correction — not by adding a test for a branch that cannot execute.
- **Corpus**: `bench/urls-100.txt` (100 pages) and `bench/urls-500.txt` (500 pages, 268 sites, ≤4/site) are new, built from each site's own sitemap and checked for a live HTTP 200 before inclusion, extending the original ten-page `urls.txt`. This entry is the crash fix the first 500-page run surfaced on page 21, before a single full run completed; the corpora's own measured numbers (once a full run completes without crashing) are a separate, later entry.

## 2026-10-04 (round 155) — each Wikimedia 429 costs about 2s of wall time per article render (its Retry-After is 1s, plus queueing); pacing requests removes the 429s but makes renders slower (docs only, no behaviour change)

Round 154 showed the 429s cost no fidelity. This round measures what they cost in time. Ten renders of the same saved en.wikipedia.org article: runs with no 429s take about 0.85s; runs with three take 2.9–3.1s; a run with five takes 4.0s. So each rate-limited response adds about 2s to the page, far more than the font-loading gain in round 151. Every 429 carried `Retry-After: 1`, which `images.go`'s retry honours, and the rest is queueing behind the other in-flight requests.

Pacing was tried to avoid the 429s in the first place. A minimum gap between request starts per host removes them at 150ms and 300ms, but mean render time rises to 4.0s and 7.2s, because about 15 requests queue behind each other. At 40ms and 80ms the 429s persist (22 and 14 over eight runs) and the time does not improve. No pacing value beat the retry, and the source is unchanged.

## 2026-10-04 (round 154) — Wikimedia's 429s are recovered by the existing retry path: renders with 1–3 rate-limited thumbnails are pixel-identical to a clean run (docs only, no behaviour change)

Round 153 left open whether the 429s that Wikimedia returns on en.wikipedia.org (about 3 per article load, in most runs) cost any image. Checked directly: six renders of the same saved article, each counted for 429 responses on the wikimedia host. Runs with 0, 1 and 3 such responses all produced PNGs byte-identical to the main-branch reference. `images.go`'s rate-limit retry (`maxRateLimitRetries`, honouring `Retry-After`, with a default backoff when the header is absent) recovers them within the same render. So the 429s cost latency, not fidelity, and the per-host cap question is a latency and politeness question only.

## 2026-10-04 (round 152, corrected round 153) — the remaining wall-clock cost on tailwindcss.com is the per-host concurrency cap of 2; raising it to 4 saves ~30%, but the Wikimedia 429 claim below was WRONG (docs only, no behaviour change)

With the font change in (round 151), the request log shows the last network response at ~3.2s of a 3.7s render, so the critical path is now network, not CPU. Image decode/resample and the settle-time JS are small by comparison. The JS modules were checked too: their requests run effectively one after another (~20ms apart, ~0.4s in total for tailwindcss.com). That is small next to the fonts, but it is serial, and the cause was not isolated (corrected in round 156).

What limits the network is `maxPerHostConcurrency` (`transport.go`): every Tailwind font and image is on one host, and the cap of 2 in-flight requests serialises them into pairs.

- **Measured, live tailwindcss.com, three interleaved pairs**: cap 2 at 4.3 / 3.5 / 3.9s; cap 4 at 2.8 / 2.6 / 2.7s, roughly 30% less wall time.
- **Correction (round 153)**: the 429 comparison above used two runs at cap 2 (0 429s) and two at cap 4 (3 each). Eight runs at the baseline cap of 2 produced 429s in six of them (3–5 each), so Wikimedia's 429s are not a function of this cap, and the cap-4 "regression" was a sampling artifact. The cap is not the lever for Wikimedia's limit. Its current baseline rate needs a separate investigation (retry/backoff behaviour, Retry-After handling, and whether the retried images still render).
- **Adaptive limiter tried in round 153, not shipped**: start at 2, grow to 4 after a run of clean responses, drop back on a 429. Growing after 10 clean responses still produced 429s (5 in one of three runs); growing after 40 never grows on a single Wikipedia page, so it cannot help there, and its Tailwind gain was within noise. Parked, not merged.

## 2026-10-04 (round 160) — module fetches ignored a rate-limited origin's Retry-After and retried inside its window, which could drop a module and break the page (engine#260, patch)

A survey of how other renderers accelerate loading recommended honouring Retry-After in module fetches. Checked against the code before acting, because the survey also claimed that no Retry-After parsing existed and that image fetches had no 429 retry. Both claims were wrong: `doWithRateLimitRetry` and `retryAfterDelay` in `images.go` already honour Retry-After for images and fonts (three retries). The real gap was narrower: `fetchModuleSource` in `modules.go` retried after a fixed 150ms then 300ms, ignoring the header.

- **Why it matters beyond latency**: a module refused with `Retry-After: 1` was retried 150ms later, drew another 429, and was dropped after the attempts ran out. A dropped module is a broken page, not a slow one.
- **Fixed**: `fetchModuleOnce` returns the Retry-After delay for a 429, and `fetchModuleSource` waits that long when present, keeping the fixed backoff otherwise. The same helper as the image path is reused.
- **Test**: `TestFetchModuleSourceHonoursRetryAfter` serves 429 with `Retry-After: 1` for the first second. With the fix it succeeds after about 1.0s. Stash-verified: the old code drops the module at 0.46s.
- **Corpus effect**: none measured. The saved Tailwind, Wikipedia and Hacker News renders are pixel-identical before and after, because no module on those pages was rate-limited. The change corrects a case the corpus does not exercise.

## 2026-10-04 (round 162) — Go 1.27.1 becomes the floor; the upgrade surfaced one real test-harness assumption (golden PNGs compared by encoded bytes), now corrected by pixels; govulncheck clean for the code (engine#262, v0.5.0)

Upgrade: `go 1.27.1` in `go.mod`, the CI `GO_VERSION`, the README badge and floor, and the bench module. Go 1.27.1 is the final release (1.27.0 and 1.27.1 are published; 1.27 release candidates are not used).

**Breakages found and what they were**
- **Golden PNGs failed (`TestSVGGolden`, `TestRenderDocumentGolden`)**. The goldens compared the encoded PNG bytes. Go 1.27's DEFLATE chooses different block types, so the same pixels encode to different bytes (2503 vs 2394 bytes). Measured: the SVG golden has 0 differing pixels out of 28000. The golden comparison now accepts the same pixels, decoded; regeneration with `UPDATE_GOLDEN=1` is unchanged. This is a test-harness correction, not a rendering change.
- **gofmt reindents** (the fleet note's first mechanism): a formatting-only change across 23 files, one of which removes a trailing blank line.
- **Coverage**: unchanged floors met (css 99.5, layout 100, paint 100, dom 98.5, paginate 100).

**Security (govulncheck, Go 1.27.1)**
- Code-affected vulnerabilities: 0. The six standard-library findings that the go1.26.4 pin carried are fixed in the new toolchain.
- Module dependency: `github.com/klauspost/compress` v1.17.4 → v1.18.7 (fix for GO-2026-5841). It is an indirect dependency through the browser HTTP transport.
- `golang.org/x/crypto` v0.57.0 has GO-2026-5932, with no fixed release; the code does not call the affected path. It is recorded here, not worked around.
- The SSRF finding from round 161 stands: the library does not filter destinations, and the README and docs say so.

**Release**: minor under 0.x, because the Go floor rises to 1.27.1 for every consumer.

## 2026-10-04 (round 161) — security audit: a 62KB image file could allocate 128MB; raster decode now refuses declared canvases above 25 megapixels; SSRF and toolchain findings recorded (engine#261, patch)

Scope: outbound fetches the engine makes for a page (images, sheets, fonts, modules, fetch/XHR, form posts), the decode path for untrusted bytes, the link hit-map, and the Go toolchain (govulncheck).

**Found and fixed**
- **Decompression bomb (image decode)**. `codec.Decode` has no size guard, and the PNG decoder allocates the canvas from a valid compressed stream. Measured: a 62KB PNG of zeros declaring 4000×4000 allocates 128MB in 40ms. Fixed: `decodeRaster` reads the declared dimensions with `image.DecodeConfig` and refuses anything above 25 megapixels (about 100MB of RGBA, still admitting a 6000×4000 photograph). Both raster decode sites use it. Test: `TestDecodeRasterRefusesOversizedDeclaredImage`, which fails with the guard disabled.
- **A correction to my own first measurement**: a header alone (IHDR with no pixel data) allocates nothing on the old path. It fails at the missing data. The allocation risk needs a valid compressed stream, as above. The test is named for what it proves (the declared size is refused), not for an allocation it does not reproduce.

**Checked and found adequate**
- Response bodies are capped on every fetch path (`LimitReader`: 32MB pages, 4MB sheets, 8MB fonts, 16MB images, 48MB module source, 32MB JS responses).
- Scheme restrictions: images, fonts and sheets accept only http(s) and data:. Modules require an http prefix. The link hit-map rejects `javascript:`, `mailto:`, `tel:` and `data:` hrefs.
- JS `fetch`/XHR go through the same client as the engine's own fetches.

**Recorded, not changed (decisions for the owner)**
- **SSRF is not guarded by the library.** A page can name any URL in an image, sheet, font, module, or script `fetch`, and the engine will fetch it, including private and link-local addresses. `browserproxy` guards this at dial time (`guard.go`, a `net.Dialer.Control` hook). A program that embeds the engine directly must add an equivalent dial guard through `Engine.Client`. The README now says so.
- **Go toolchain**: govulncheck reports six standard-library findings fixed in go1.26.5 and go1.26.6 (`net/http`, `crypto/tls`, `net/url`, `encoding/xml`, `encoding/asn1`). The module pins go1.26.4 in `go.mod` and in CI. Moving the pin is a CI and supply-chain change, so it is left to the owner.

## 2026-10-04 (round 158) — the settle loop is a small lever: about 0.26s of CPU on tailwindcss.com, all of it full re-cascades, bounded by the network wait (docs only, no behaviour change)

Third candidate after the cascade and the image path: the settle-then-render loop. Profiling one Tailwind render shows `settle` at about 12% of CPU samples, and of that the cascade (`CascadeMediaContainers`) is almost all of it, about 0.26s. JavaScript execution is about 0.07s. A render performs five full cascades. Each is a whole-document pass.

- **Ceiling**: removing every redundant cascade would save at most about 0.26s of CPU per page. The render is about 3.7s of wall time and is dominated by the network, so the realistic wall-clock gain is a few percent.
- **What it would take**: knowing whether a settle pass changed anything the cascade reads (DOM structure, attributes, stylesheets). That needs a mutation or dirty flag through the DOM and the JS binding. A missed dirty path would silently keep stale styles, which is a fidelity risk that outweighs the gain.

Not implemented. The lever is real but small, and the correctness risk is not justified by it.

## 2026-10-04 (round 157) — image decode and resampling are not the next lever: one redundant resample in 46 on tailwindcss.com, and decode is already parallel (docs only, no behaviour change)

With the cascade and font loading done, the image path was the remaining CPU candidate. A CPU profile of one Tailwind render shows `loadOneImage` at about 12% of samples, decode about 10%, and resampling about 11% (mostly `resample.resampleH`/`resampleV`). Two hypotheses to check: that the same background bitmap is resampled again for every element that paints it, and that decoding is serial.

- **Resample calls**: 46 `scaleTile` calls per render, 2.38 Mpx of output. Keyed by source, size and mode, 45 are distinct and one is a repeat. A repeat cache would save about 2% of the resampling work, not worth the complexity.
- **Decode**: already fan-out through `parallelDo`, bounded by `imgWorkers` (up to 8). There is no serial decode to remove.

So the image path is close to minimal for this page: the remaining work follows from the number of distinct images at their display sizes. Nothing changed. Further CPU gains would need a faster codec, or skipping decode of images a page never shows, and neither is measured yet.

## 2026-10-04 (round 151) — web fonts downloaded one after another, ~4.4s of serial waiting on tailwindcss.com; now fetched concurrently with the same fetches and identical pixels (engine#252, performance only)

Logging every request through the engine's own client during one tailwindcss.com render showed the real wall-clock cost: not the CPU, but ten `@font-face` files fetched in a plain loop, each starting only when the previous finished (~430ms each, ~4.4s in total). Images and stylesheets were already concurrent; JS modules were effectively serial (corrected in round 156). CPU-side, after the round-150 rule index, the remaining costs are image decode/resample and PNG encode, which are smaller.

- **Change**: `LoadFontFaces` (`fontfaces.go`) groups candidate faces by slot in declaration order and loads the slots concurrently. Within a slot, candidates still run in order and stop at the first that loads, so the same fetches happen; results are returned in declaration order. Font fetching writes no shared engine state, so concurrency is safe.
- **Test fixture fix**: the font test server's hit counter was unsynchronised. It is now guarded, since concurrent fetches would race under `-race`.
- **Tests**: `TestLoadFontFacesLoadsSlotsConcurrentlyInDeclarationOrder` serves four 300ms fonts and asserts both declaration order and that the total stays under 900ms. Stash-verified: against the serial loader it takes 1.21s and fails. The existing font tests pass under `-race`.
- **Measured**: live tailwindcss.com, three interleaved pairs, HEAD 6.0 / 7.7 / 7.0s against new 4.5 / 6.3 / 5.3s, roughly 25% less wall time. The saved pages (Tailwind, react.dev, Wikipedia, pkg.go.dev, HN) render pixel-identically.
- **Next**: image decode/resample and the settle-loop JS sequencing, both measured by the same request log.

## 2026-10-04 (round 150) — the cascade tested every author rule against every element; a rule index keyed on each selector's subject compound makes the same cascade ~10× cheaper, with byte-identical output (engine#251, performance only)

Profiling tailwindcss.com showed the cascade (`css.computeElement` → `Selector.MatchesHost`) at 46% of CPU, with allocation and GC behind much of the rest. SIMD and assembly were not the bottleneck: image resampling and PNG together were 5–8%.

- **Change**: `css/cascade.go` builds a `ruleIndex` once per document stylesheet, bucketing each selector by the id, class or tag its subject (rightmost) compound requires. Rules with no such key (`*`, `:root`, `::part`, `:host` subjects, empty selectors) go in an always-tested bucket. Candidates are returned sorted and de-duplicated, so declarations reach the cascade in exactly the source order a linear scan produced. Shadow-DOM scopes keep the linear scan, so their behaviour is untouched.
- **Output identical**: nine saved pages (HN, Wikipedia, pkg.go.dev, caniuse, GitHub, react.dev, go.dev/blog, example.com, tailwindcss.com) render pixel-for-pixel the same before and after.
- **Measured** (single runs, `-file` on saved snapshots): tailwindcss.com 7.8s→5.4s, GitHub 2.5s→0.7s, Wikipedia 2.3s→1.3s. Profiled tailwindcss.com: cascade 1.86s of 4.08s CPU → 0.19s of 2.02s. Pages whose cost lies outside the cascade (pkg.go.dev, go.dev/blog, caniuse) are unchanged.
- **Tests**: `css/ruleindex_test.go` covers the bucket keys (id, class, tag, descendant subjects keyed on their subject, universal and `:root` always), sorted and de-duplicated candidates, and the `:host`/`::part`/empty subjects.
- **Scope not done**: the rest of the time is still in style cloning (`cloneProps`), allocation churn and image/offscreen buffers. Those are the next round's targets.
- **Bench**: no fidelity change is expected or claimed; the pixel-identity check above is the stronger guarantee.

## 2026-10-04 (round 149) — a `<tr>`'s own CSS height was ignored, so HN's `height:5px` spacer rows laid out at 0px; fixing it moves the front page from 140px short to 30px tall against Chrome (engine#250)

Picked up round 148's parked finding. Round 148 blamed a Verdana font-metrics gap; that was WRONG, and it is corrected below. Measuring it properly ruled the font out: Chrome's own computed geometry for the same HN snapshot (rendered with its stylesheet inlined, so the cascade is real) gives a 13.3px title at a 16px line box and a 9.33px subtext at an 11px line box, and ours matches the 11px subtext line exactly.

- **The real defect**: `layout/table.go` sized a row from its cells alone. `<tr class="spacer" style="height:5px">` (HN puts one between every story, and one under its nav bar at `height:10px`) therefore laid out at 0px. Chrome honours a row's CSS height as a minimum (CSS 2.1 §17.5.3). Fixed: the row's own resolved height is now a floor under the cell-derived height, and a percentage or auto height is left alone.
- **Measured on one fixed HN snapshot, rendered from the same saved HTML**: before, 1077px; after, 1247px; Chrome on that snapshot, 1217px. The error went from −140px to +30px. The story pitch went from 31px to 36px against Chrome's 35px.
- **Bench, live pages**: HN SSIM 0.604 (round 146) → 0.609. Attribution by diffing each page's own webengine panel against the last commit: only HN changed as intended. GitHub's panel changed only in its live star/fork/commit counters (its markup has no `<tr>` with a height). tailwindcss.com's changed region is the known sponsor-logo carousel, same bounding box as every prior round. The other eight pages are byte-identical in their webengine panels.
- **Tests**: `layout/table_rowheight_test.go` pins a row's height at 30px from `tr style="height:30px"`, and that a row's CSS height never shrinks a 50px cell. Git-stash-confirmed: the minimum-height test fails without the fix.
- **What round 148's cellpadding experiment actually showed**: with this fix in place, adding cellpadding=0 takes HN to 1117px (−100px against Chrome). So cellpadding is still parked, for a different reason than round 148 gave. Chrome's title row is 19px with zero padding, and ours is 16px of content: a 3px deficit that the 1px UA padding had been hiding. Land cellpadding only after that title-row content height is fixed.

**Correction to round 148**: its "Verdana fallback line-height" diagnosis and the font-metrics Known-gaps bullet were wrong. The font metrics are close to Chrome's; the errors were the ignored row height and the title row's content height.

## 2026-10-04 (round 148) — table `cellpadding` is unmapped, and fixing it on its own makes news.ycombinator.com WORSE — measured, not shipped (engine#249, docs only)

Re-checked round 146's stale-premise method on the inline-flex/inline-grid/inline-table Known-gaps bullet first: `inline-flex` was already fixed (`DisplayInlineFlex` + the `NestedBox` atomic inline path), so that bullet's lead is corrected; `inline-grid`/`inline-table` remain lossy, but the only corpus use of `inline-grid` (tailwindcss.com's header button and theme switch) sits inside `display:flex` containers, where a flex item blockifies regardless — no visible effect.

A fresh audit of HTML presentational attributes surfaced `cellpadding`, verified independently: zero production matches in the repo, and news.ycombinator.com's live HTML has `cellpadding="0"` on all four of its tables, covering all 159 of its `<td>`s. A real browser takes that as a presentation hint on every cell (padding 0); this engine kept `ua.go`'s `padding:1px`.

- **The fix was written, tested and worked as specified**: a `td`/`th` took its nearest table ancestor's integer `cellpadding` as `padding`, at author-origin zero specificity so an author `padding` rule still wins; tests covered the table itself staying unpadded, nested tables, unmodelled values (percentage/non-numeric/negative keep 1px), and a parentless cell. Git-stash-confirmed. Coverage held (css exactly at its 99.5% floor after covering the no-table-ancestor branch with a real test).
- **Measured on the real page, same saved HN snapshot rendered before and after**: page height 1077→947px; story-row pitch 31→27px. But Chrome's pitch on the same page is ~34–35px, so the fix moved the rows AWAY from the reference. Bench confirmed it on the live page: HN SSIM 0.604→0.585 (Chrome 1249px tall vs ours 979px).
- **Why**: HN's `.title` is `font-family:Verdana,...; font-size:10pt`, and Verdana is not bundled, so the engine falls back to a font with different line-height metrics, making each title row shorter than Chrome's. The 2px-per-row phantom padding from the 1px `td` default was partially cancelling that deficit. Two errors offsetting each other — correcting one alone exposes the other.
- **Decision: NOT merged.** A change whose only measured effect on its own target page is a regression is not a fix, even when it is spec-correct. The validated patch is preserved (in the session scratchpad, and in git stash `r148 cellpadding`) to land together with a line-height/fallback-metrics correction. Landing order matters: cellpadding first would make HN measurably worse.
- **Also tidied**: gofmt on `css/ua.go`, the round-146 comment indentation inside the concatenated stylesheet string. That is formatting only, from my own earlier hunk.
- **Known gaps (new, disclosed)**:
  - Font fallback metrics: when a page names a font this engine does not bundle (Verdana here), the substitute's line-height differs from the real face's, so row heights diverge from Chrome. This is now the gate for landing `cellpadding` (above), and it probably affects every page using an unbundled system font.
  - The engine's `border-spacing` initial value is `0` where a real browser's UA default for `table` is `2px`, and `cellspacing` as an HTML attribute is unmapped. The corpus only uses `cellspacing="0"`, which matches the accidental default, so no corpus page proves a visible effect yet.
  - `input size="N"` is unmapped (one search box on HN; effect unmeasured).

## 2026-09-30 (round 147) — table cells never honoured `vertical-align` at all; a real browser's own UA default (`middle`, not `top`) was also simply missing, so EVERY unstyled table cell row-pair of differing heights was wrong, not just an explicit author declaration (engine#248)

Ruled out `transform:scale()` again this round (already-documented dead end, round 146) and re-checked goja's ES2018 `for await...of` gap (dop251/goja#498) after Renovate bumped the pinned commit mid-week — still open, nothing relevant landed upstream, confirmed dead end. Delegated a fresh systematic CSS-property audit, which this time surfaced something closer to a correctness bug than a missing feature: `css.Style.VerticalAlign` and all seven of its keyword constants were already fully PARSED (`css/parse.go`'s `vertical-align` case) and documented as "recognised but not yet given positional effect" — but `layout/table.go`'s own per-cell positioning loop never read the field at all, and `css/presentational.go` had no `valign` attribute mapping (`align`'s own direct HTML-attribute counterpart) despite already mapping `align` for the exact same tags.

- **Independently re-verified every claim before writing any code**: read `layout/table.go`'s own per-cell loop directly (confirmed its comment, "Stretch the cell to the row height (content stays top-aligned)," and that `cs.VerticalAlign` is read nowhere in the file), grepped `css/presentational.go`/`css/ua.go` for `valign`/`VerticalAlign` (zero hits, confirming both the missing attribute mapping and the missing UA default), and independently fetched news.ycombinator.com's own LIVE HTML (not trusted from the audit report) to confirm its exact top-nav markup: three `<td>`s (an 18px logo, a line of nav text, a "login" link) with no `valign` anywhere, which a real browser centres against each other by its own UA default — and separately confirmed its story rows explicitly override with `valign="top"` on the rank-number/vote-arrow cells (60 occurrences), proving the SAME page relies on both the missing default AND the missing attribute mapping at once.
- **Fixed in three small, independently-reused pieces, no new machinery**: (1) `css/ua.go`'s `td`/`th` UA declarations gained `vertical-align:middle` — a real browser's own table-cell-specific default (every OTHER box's default is the property's initial value, `baseline`). (2) `css/presentational.go` gained a `valign` branch mirroring the existing `align` branch exactly, scoped to `td`/`th` (the only tags real usage confirmed a need for; the spec also allows it on `tr`/`thead`/`tbody`/`tfoot`/`col`/`colgroup`, not modelled). (3) `layout/table.go`'s per-cell loop now computes each cell's slack (row height minus its own natural height) and, for `middle`/`bottom` only, shifts the cell's CONTENT down by `translateBox` then pulls the cell's own outer `Y`/`ContentY` back up by the same amount — so the box's own background/border still spans the FULL row height (a real browser's table box model: vertical-align never affects a cell's own background fill, only where its content sits inside it) while only the content moves. `top`/`baseline`/every other keyword (`text-top`, `text-bottom`, `sub`, `super` — none have a confirmed real trigger ON A TABLE CELL specifically, where the spec's own real-baseline-row-alignment algorithm for the latter group is a materially larger feature this round doesn't attempt) keep the pre-existing top-aligned behaviour unchanged.
- **A pre-existing test-coverage gap matched the pre-existing code gap exactly, and both are now fixed together**: `TestPresentationalColorAndAlign` already included `valign="top"` in its own test markup but never once asserted on `VerticalAlign` — the real bug and the untested assertion sat side by side the whole time. Added the missing assertion to that test and wrote two new ones (`TestTdThDefaultToVerticalAlignMiddle`, `TestValignAttribute`) covering the UA default, every modelled keyword, an unrecognised value being ignored, and the attribute correctly NOT applying to an unscoped tag like `tr`.
- **8 new/updated tests total**: the three `css/presentational_test.go` tests above, plus four new `layout/table_valign_test.go` tests exercising the actual positional math (middle centres content in the row's slack, bottom flushes it to the row's own bottom, top is unaffected, and — the slack-free common case — two naturally-equal-height cells produce no shift at all for `vertical-align:bottom`, proving the offset logic is correctly gated on `slack > 0` rather than always firing). Each positional assertion is exact, not a tolerance band — an early draft used a ±2px tolerance that masked a 2px miscalculation in the test's own expected value (forgetting the UA-default 1px top+bottom cell padding folds into each cell's own "natural height," not just its final stretched height); caught by deliberately tightening the tolerance to exact equality and finding the real arithmetic, not by assuming the loose version was already correct. Git-stash-confirmed: reverting the three production files alone (keeping the new/updated tests) fails every one of the new/updated assertions, not just a compile error this time, since no production file's own exported surface changed shape — the closest thing to a compile-time guarantee this particular fix can offer, and still a stronger signal than any test passing on its own.
- **Verified directly on the real live page, not just unit tests**: re-rendered news.ycombinator.com before and after the fix and pixel-diffed the two PNGs directly. The diff's bounding box lands EXACTLY on the top-nav row (x:111-935, y:10-30) and nowhere else on the entire 1024×1109 page — confirming the fix changes precisely its own confirmed target and nothing else. Separately confirmed the story rows' own `valign="top"`-marked rank-number/vote-arrow cells are BYTE-IDENTICAL before and after (a second pixel-diff over that whole region returned no bounding box at all), proving the explicit-attribute override path works correctly without disturbing already-correct content.
- **New Known-gaps entry**: table-cell `vertical-align` now has real positional effect for `top`/`middle`/`bottom` (the three keywords with a confirmed trigger); `baseline`'s own real-browser behaviour — aligning cells' first-line baselines across a row, not simply top-aligning them — is NOT modelled (this engine's current `baseline`/default behaviour is really "top," a pre-existing approximation this round didn't touch), and `text-top`/`text-bottom`/`sub`/`super` have no modelled effect on a table cell specifically (unlike `VerticalAlign`'s own general, non-table doc comment, which already discloses `text-bottom`'s narrow inline-replaced-element scope elsewhere). Also corrected the stale, "not re-verified, treat as unconfirmed" `colspan`/`rowspan`/`border-collapse` bullet into a precise, independently-checked statement (see below).
- **Bench: 2 of 10 pages (`developer.mozilla.org`, `react.dev`) are fully byte-identical to round 146's committed baseline; `pkg.go.dev/net/http`'s own webengine-side panel is ALSO byte-identical (diff bbox literally `None`) — its tiny SSIM tick is 100% the live Chrome reference image changing, entirely on the other side of the montage. The other 7 moved, and every one is individually attributed, none to this round's own fix except the one it targets.** `news.ycombinator.com` shows BOTH effects at once, visually confirmed side by side: the top-nav row now centres its logo against the nav text (this round's own fix, matching the isolated before/after pixel-diff already done above) AND an entirely different 30-story front page (the same already-documented rotation every prior round has seen). `github.com/golang/go`'s large diff is its own live repo stats still ticking (commits 67,766→67,870, PRs 573→589, forks 20.8k→21.1k, stars 139k→139.2k) — the same skeleton-loading file-list placeholder rows as every prior round, not real cell content a `vertical-align` change could touch. `go.dev/blog`'s diff is a genuinely NEW post ("Arch-specific SIMD in Go", 2 October 2026) published since the last fetch, pushing every older entry down one slot — an ordered list, not a table. `tailwindcss.com`'s diff is its own changing, 768→787) is the already-documented, still-unfixed animation-timeline gap (round 145's Known-gaps entry) — a different snapshot of the same non-deterministic multi-language crossfade, no ## 2026-09-28 (round 146) — a checkbox/radio's own cascaded style was structurally unreachable from its paint function at all, so `appearance:none` custom-toggle patterns always rendered as this engine's generic square regardless of the author's own CSS (engine#246)

Ruled out `transform: scale()`/the standalone `scale` property first (this round's own initial candidate, given `github.com/go-images/images`' own `resample` package is already a dependency): no reachable real-world trigger anywhere in the ten-page bench corpus — tailwindcss.com declares `scale-*` utilities in its compiled CSS but never applies any `scale-*` class in its actual HTML, and react.dev's only usage (`active:scale-95`) is gated behind the `:active` pseudo-class, invisible in this engine's static, non-interactive renders. Delegated a fresh systematic CSS-property audit in its place, which surfaced a structural gap rather than a missing property: `paint/paint.go`'s `paintFormControl` dispatches checkbox/radio controls to a SEPARATE function, `paintCheckboxLike`, whose signature had no `*css.Style` parameter at all — unlike the non-checkbox path two lines below it in the same function, which already reads `it.Style.Background`/`it.Style.Border` (round 65's own fix, engine#154). Independently verified before writing any code: read `paintCheckboxLike`'s own body directly (confirmed it draws a hardcoded `formFieldBg`/`formAccent`/`formBorder` square, branching only on the `checked` attribute) and fetched developer.mozilla.org's own live HTML directly (not trusted from the audit report) to confirm its `<mdn-switch>` custom element's declarative-shadow-DOM CSS sets `appearance:none;background-color:...;background-image:radial-gradient(...);border-radius:9999px` on a real `<input type=checkbox class="switch__input">` UNCONDITIONALLY — the base pill shape is not gated behind `:checked` at all, only the knob's position/colour REFINE on `:checked` via an ordinary rule this engine's cascade already re-evaluates automatically.

- **Fixed**: `css/value.go`/`css/parse.go` — new non-inherited `AppearanceNone bool` field, backing `appearance`/`-webkit-appearance: none` (the only value modelled; `auto`/`initial`/`unset` reset it, any other CSS Basic UI keyword like `textfield`/`menulist-button` is syntactically valid but has no confirmed trigger and is left untouched, matching every other unsupported-value convention in this codebase). `paint/paint.go` — `paintCheckboxLike` now takes `dst`, the element's `*css.Style` and `bgImgs`; when `AppearanceNone` is set it paints like any other styled box (solid background, `paintBackgroundLayers` for gradient/bitmap layers — reused verbatim from a real `layout.Box`'s own background path via a small signature generalisation from `*layout.Box` to `(st *css.Style, bx image.Rectangle)`, its only two actual uses — `styleRadius` for `border-radius`, and `paintEdges` for the per-side border) instead of the hardcoded square; without it, nothing changed at all.
- **A pre-existing UA-stylesheet comment predicted this exact fix and needed correcting, not just the code**: `css/ua.go`'s `input[type=checkbox], input[type=radio] { background-color: transparent; border: 0 }` rule already carried a comment stating "paint's own checkbox-square renderer never consults Style.Background/Border for them" — true before this round, false now for the `appearance:none` branch. Updated the comment rather than leaving a confidently-wrong claim next to the very code it was describing; the transparent/0 default itself needed no change; it is exactly a real browser's own reset for a native-chrome-suppressed checkbox with no author styling (invisible until the author's own rule paints something), not a leftover value that happened to become newly load-bearing.
- **8 new tests**: `css/props_test.go`'s `TestApplyAppearanceProperty` (none, auto/initial/unset reset, the `-webkit-` alias, an unsupported keyword left untouched, explicit `inherit`) and six new cases in `paint/formcontrol_test.go` — a regression guard proving a checkbox WITHOUT `appearance:none` still ignores author style entirely (the dispatch condition itself, not just the generic look), then `appearance:none` honouring background colour, a linear-gradient background-image (reusing the existing `linearGradStyle` test helper), a custom border (plus the bare/no-author-style case painting literally nothing at the edge), and `border-radius:50%` producing an actual rounded/pill shape (corner pixel stays unpainted, centre fills). Git-stash-confirmed: reverting the four production files alone fails to even compile in both `css` and `paint` (`unknown field AppearanceNone`). Coverage floors held on the first attempt: `css` 99.5%, `paint` 100.0% (unchanged from before this round).
- **Verified directly on the real live page's own exact markup, not just unit tests**: developer.mozilla.org's `<mdn-switch>` dropdown is behind a closed, JS-driven language-switcher menu invisible in this engine's static render (the same "real cascade trigger, interaction-gated visibility" situation as round 142's `caniuse.com` `<details>` case) — so built an isolated repro reproducing its EXACT real shadow-DOM markup/CSS byte-for-byte (declarative shadow DOM, already supported) instead of trusting the unit tests alone. Rendered it standalone: an unchecked switch now shows a grey pill with a white knob on the left, a checked one a blue pill with the knob slid right — both the background/background-image/border-radius fix AND the `:checked` colour/position swap (no special-casing needed; it flows through the ordinary cascade) confirmed on the real component's own markup. A plain, unstyled `<input type=checkbox>` (the common case) re-rendered identically to before this round — the generic square, unaffected.
- **Bench: six of the ten pages' own webengine-side renders are byte-identical to round 145's committed ones; the other four moved, and each is individually attributed to already-documented, unrelated live-content churn — none reach a checkbox at all.** `github.com/golang/go`'s tiny diff is its own commit counter ticking 572→573 (the same live-repository-activity noise documented every round this page has shown any movement). `news.ycombinator.com`'s large diff is its own already-extensively-documented rotating front page — screenshotted both panels and confirmed an entirely different thirty-story list, points, and comment counts. `tailwindcss.com`'s diff is its own already-documented JS-driven sponsor-logo carousel (base ui/KIRO/namespace/... → Resend/greptile/momentic/...), not the (still off-screen, behind a closed dropdown) `<mdn-switch>`-style trigger this round targets. `caniuse.com`'s own webengine panel is BYTE-IDENTICAL (`diff.getbbox()` returns `None`) — its 0.001 SSIM tick is 100% the live Chrome reference image changing between fetches, nothing on this engine's own side. None of the ten corpus pages' static renders contain an `appearance:none` checkbox reachable outside a closed interactive menu, so this fix is real and demonstrated (the isolated `<mdn-switch>` repro above) but bench-invisible, the same "confirmed, narrowly-scoped, bench-invisible" pattern as pkg.go.dev's multicol fix (round 144) and tailwindcss.com's own P3-colours rotation (round 145).

## 2026-09-28 (round 145) — `transform:rotate()` AND the newer standalone `rotate` property were both entirely unimplemented; the real confirmed trigger turned out to use the property this session's own existing doc comment never even considered (engine#245)

Continuing the bibliography-first, re-scope-a-deferred-gap method rounds 141/144 both used successfully: FIDELITY.md's own Known gaps already flagged `rotate`/`scale`/`skew`/`matrix` as unsupported `transform` functions, alongside the ALREADY-implemented `translate` (round 87). Read the actual CSS Transforms mechanics (via MDN) before assuming scope, and found `rotate` alone (no scale/skew/matrix mixed in) plausibly tractable: unlike a full affine transform, this engine's existing `paint.go` group-buffer machinery (render a box's subtree to an offscreen buffer, transform it, composite back — already used for `filter`/opacity/`mask-image`) is architecturally the exact shape a "render, rotate the pixels, composite" implementation needs, and the pinned `github.com/go-images/images` dependency already ships a ready-made, arbitrary-angle, bilinear, resize-to-fit `Rotate` function (reproducing scikit-image's own `rotate`) — no rotation math needed from scratch.

- **Verified the sign convention BEFORE writing any paint code, not after**: go-images' own doc comment states its `Rotate` is counter-clockwise-positive (matching scikit-image); CSS's `rotate()`/`rotate` is clockwise-positive. Negated the angle at the one call site and PROVED it with an isolated repro — a box with four differently-coloured borders (top/right/bottom/left) rotated 90° — checking the exact predicted edge mapping (left→top, top→right, right→bottom, bottom→left for a correct clockwise rotation) against the actual rendered pixels before trusting the implementation, the same discipline round 141 used for the ligature glyph.
- **The real confirmed trigger was NOT what it first looked like, caught by checking the compiled CSS directly rather than assuming from a class name**: tailwindcss.com's `rotate-(--angle)`/`.rotate-90` utility classes looked, by name, like the classic `transform: rotate()` function this session's own pre-existing doc comment already anticipated — but modern Tailwind (v4) actually compiles them to CSS Transforms Level 2's newer, INDEPENDENT `rotate` property (`rotate:var(--angle)`, `rotate:90deg`), confirmed directly from the site's own compiled CSS bundle. Implementing only the function-syntax parser (already fully built and tested at that point) would have shipped a real, verified rotation capability that silently did NOTHING on the one page that motivated it — caught by re-rendering the actual real page and seeing the diagonal "P3 colors" swatch-name labels (`red`, `orange`, `amber`, …) still perfectly horizontal, not by assuming success from passing unit tests alone. Added the standalone `rotate` property (a bare angle, no function-call parsing needed at all) as its own `case`, reusing the identical `RotateDeg` field and paint path; kept the `transform: rotate()` parser too, since it is the same now-evidenced visual capability under CSS's older syntax, not a separate speculative addition.
- **Scoped independently of the existing filter/opacity/mask-image group pass, not unified with it**: rotating pixels needs the box's own exact border-box rectangle and a buffer that GROWS to fit the rotated corners; the existing group pass keeps the full canvas width and only crops vertically, sized for blur spread rather than rotation. No confirmed trigger combines rotate with filter/opacity/mask-image on the same element, so `paintBox` only takes the new `paintRotated` path when none of those apply — disclosed, not engineered around, in `RotateDeg`'s own doc comment.
- **A legitimate test-expectation update, not a regression**: the pre-existing `TestApplyTransformTranslate` asserted `transform:rotate(10deg)` was a complete no-op, written back when rotate was genuinely unrecognised. Moved it out of that no-op list into its own new `TestApplyTransformRotate`, and confirmed the OTHER no-op cases in that list (`scale(2)`, `translateX(5px) rotate(10deg)` mixed, `matrix(...)`, malformed values) are still correctly no-ops — same "legitimate test-expectation update" class as round 78's box-shadow test and round 70's `TestDocumentMisc`.
- **A real design correctness check caught before shipping, not after**: an early version of the `transform` cascade logic unconditionally reset every transform field before checking which function parsed — which would have let an INVALID later `transform` declaration silently erase an EARLIER, valid one's effect, violating CSS's own "an invalid declaration is dropped, the cascade keeps the last VALID one" rule. Reasoned through this before running any test, not caught by one: both branches now only reset the OTHER function's fields on their OWN successful parse; an unrecognised/malformed value leaves everything untouched.
- **26 new tests**: `css/props_test.go` (`TestApplyTransformRotate` — every value/unit, `rotateZ`, cascade replacement in both directions, `none`, and no-op-on-invalid; `TestApplyStandaloneRotateProperty` — the same matrix for the real property) and the new `paint/rotate_test.go` (a hand-built box tree, mirroring the existing `backdrop_filter_test.go`/`mask_paint_test.go` convention: 90° clockwise verified by exact edge-colour mapping, a negative-angle mirror check, zero-rotation is a no-op, outside-clip is a no-op mirroring `TestBackdropFilterOutsideClipIsANoOp`, and the disclosed non-combination with opacity). Git-stash-confirmed: reverting the production files alone fails to even compile in both `css` and `paint`. Coverage floors held — `paint` stayed at its 100.0% ratchet floor on the first attempt this time (round 144's own "coverage floor caught unreachable defensive code" lesson was applied while writing `paintRotated`, not discovered again after the fact).
- **Verified directly on the real live page**: re-rendering tailwindcss.com now shows the "P3 colors" section's diagonal swatch-name labels tilted -45° and reading correctly uphill left-to-right (confirming BOTH the standalone-property fix and the negative-angle sign convention together, on real content, not just the isolated repro).
- **Bench: every page's own webengine-side render is byte-identical to round 144's committed one, except three — each individually confirmed NOT this round's own effect before moving on, the same pixel-diff discipline rounds 143/144 both used.** `github.com/golang/go`'s tiny diff is a real commit count ticking 67,762→67,763 between fetches (already-documented live-repository-activity noise). `news.ycombinator.com`'s large diff is its own already-extensively-documented live, rotating front page — screenshotted both and confirmed an entirely different eight-story list, not a rendering change. `tailwindcss.com`'s diff sits in its own already-documented "supported by the best" sponsor-logo carousel (Resend/Lovable/Postmark/… → base-ui/KIRO/namespace/…, a JS-driven rotation, confirmed by cropping and comparing both panels directly) — NOT the P3-colours section this round's own fix targets, which sits well below this benchmark's own capped comparison region, the same "real, confirmed, bench-invisible" pattern as pkg.go.dev's multicol fix in round 144.
- **A genuinely new, real, and unrelated finding surfaced while checking `example.com`'s own alarming-looking SSIM drop (0.954→0.936) — investigated fully before ruling it out, not dismissed as noise on a guess.** `example.com` gained real client-side JavaScript for the first time in this session's whole history: a newly-added `s.js` cross-fades through six `<p lang="…">` translations (Arabic/Chinese/French/Russian/Spanish/English) stacked in the SAME CSS Grid cell, toggling each character's own `<span>` opacity via a `setInterval` every 5 seconds with per-span `transitionDelay`. This engine's settle-then-render model has no real animation-timeline simulation, so it renders whatever opacity state the JS/CSS transition machinery happens to be in when script execution settles — here, several languages' spans simultaneously above zero opacity, overlapping into illegible layered text. Confirmed via git-stash that this reproduces IDENTICALLY on round 144's own pre-rotate code — entirely unrelated to this round, a pre-existing (if newly-exposed) animation-timing gap on a page that had never needed one before. Not fixed here (a real `setInterval`-driven, CSS-`transition`-based crossfade needs actual animation-timeline modelling, well outside a `rotate` round's own scope) — flagged as a concrete lead for a future round instead of silently absorbed into "the reference changed" noise bucket it superficially resembled.

## 2026-09-28 (round 144) — multi-column layout (`columns`/`column-count`/`column-width`) was entirely unimplemented — the round 143 gap re-examined bibliography-first rather than left deferred, and found narrower than it first looked (engine#244)

Round 143's own audit found `columns` real (pkg.go.dev's own `.UnitFiles-fileList{columns:12.5rem 5;...}`, its package-documentation "Source Files" list) but judged a full implementation "comparable in scope to Shadow DOM" and deferred it. This round re-opened that judgment the way the user's own instruction asked — bibliography first — by actually reading the CSS Multi-column Layout spec's real mechanics (via MDN) before trusting the earlier scope estimate.

- **The bibliography changed the assessment**: the spec's own true balancing algorithm (iteratively finding the column height that minimises the tallest column while respecting break-inside/break-before avoidance) IS genuinely large — that part of the earlier estimate holds. But the ONE confirmed trigger is a `<ul>` of ~29 short, uniform-height `<li>` filenames — not arbitrary flowing prose needing mid-paragraph fragmentation. Scoped down to exactly that shape: distribute the container's own in-flow ELEMENT children (each already laid out once, at the real column width, as a fully atomic box — never split across columns, matching this engine's own "atomic child" convention for a replaced element or a table row elsewhere) into N columns by a single greedy cumulative-height pass (target = total÷count), then reposition each already-correct box subtree with the existing `translateBox` helper — no second layout pass, no new fragmentation machinery.
- **Fixed** (`css/value.go`/`css/parse.go`: new non-inherited `ColumnCount`/`ColumnWidth` fields, `column-count`/`column-width`/`columns` shorthand parsing — the shorthand's `||` combinator accepts either sub-value in either order, matching real CSS; `layout/multicol.go`'s new `layoutMultiCol`/`resolveColumns`, dispatched in `contents()` for a plain `display:block` container with either property set).
- **Reused the existing `column-gap` property rather than inventing a new field**: real CSS defines `column-gap` once, in the Box Alignment module, shared by flex/grid/multicol — this engine already had the field (`Style.ColumnGap`) for flex/grid. Its shared "unset" default was changed from the Go zero value to `Length{Auto:true}` so multicol can tell "never set" apart from "explicitly 0" — confirmed safe for flex/grid's own `gapLen`/`gapPxCSS`, which already treat Auto identically to an explicit 0, so this is a genuinely free win, not a compromise between the two contexts. Multicol alone resolves that Auto to 1em (the spec's own "normal" default, distinct from flex/grid's own 0).
- **A real, caught-before-shipping regression from an existing footgun class**: the first working version broke FIVE pre-existing Shadow DOM slot-distribution tests. Root cause: two hand-built fallback `css.Style{}` literals elsewhere in this engine (`layout.go`'s `place()`'s `st == nil` fallback, `table.go`'s `cellStyle`'s default) never set `ColumnWidth`, so its Go zero value (`Auto:false, Px:0`) silently read as "column-width:0px set" to any new code checking `!st.ColumnWidth.Auto` — exactly what this round's own dispatch check does. Both fallbacks now explicitly set `ColumnWidth: Length{Auto:true}`, the same discipline every OTHER Auto-defaulted field in those two literals already followed; confirmed via `grep` that no other hand-built `css.Style{}` literal exists anywhere else in the repo.
- **Deliberately NOT modelled, disclosed in `ColumnCount`'s own doc comment**: `column-span:all` (no confirmed trigger), `column-rule` (pure decoration painting, not attempted since the column gap alone is already the confirmed visual difference), true iterative balancing (this engine's single greedy pass approximates it closely for a roughly-uniform list but can leave a visibly taller last column for adversarial, very unevenly-sized content), and fragmenting a single child's own internal content across two columns (a real browser can split one long paragraph's own lines between columns; this engine cannot).
- **Verified directly against the real live page, not just the isolated repro**: re-rendering pkg.go.dev/net/http live now lays its "Source Files" list out in 4 columns (the container is narrower than 5×12.5rem+gaps, so `resolveColumns`'s own width-fit constraint correctly requests fewer than the CSS's own literal `5` — exactly the spec's documented "fewer columns when the container is narrower" behaviour, not a bug), matching real Chrome's own behaviour far more closely than the previous single tall column; total page height dropped by 520px.
- **20 new tests** across `css/parse_test.go` (`column-count`/`column-width`/`columns` — every value shape, the `||` order-independence, invalid shapes left unchanged, `unset`, explicit `inherit`, `inheritFrom`) and the new `layout/multicol_test.go` (`resolveColumns` tested directly for every resolution case — width alone, count alone, both constraining, both fitting, explicit vs default gap, negative-width clamping — plus full-pipeline tests: balanced columns, column-width-alone, the real trigger's own narrower-than-requested shape, `display:none`/out-of-flow/bare-text children correctly excluded, an overlong child staying whole, and `column-count:1` falling back to ordinary block stacking). Git-stash-confirmed: reverting `css/parse.go`/`css/value.go`/`layout/layout.go`/`layout/table.go`/`layout/multicol.go` alone (keeping the new tests) fails to even compile in both packages.
- **Bench: pkg.go.dev exactly flat (0.71557), and confirmed WHY rather than assumed real fixes never move it — the fix's own effect sits entirely outside this benchmark's own capped comparison region.** A direct pixel-diff of this round's own webengine-side montage panel against round 143's committed one is byte-identical for EVERY page except three, and none of those three is caused by this round's own code: `developer.mozilla.org`'s SSIM movement (0.6369→0.6057, back toward its pre-round-143 value) is entirely the CHROME reference image changing between fetches — its own webengine panel is byte-identical, confirmed by diffing it directly against round 143's committed PNG (a real, if worrying-looking, instance of round 74's own already-documented "SSIM move is entirely the Chrome reference image changing" pattern, checked rather than assumed given how the number looked at a glance). `news.ycombinator.com`'s small movement is its own already-extensively-documented live, rotating front page — confirmed directly this round too: the exact same top eight stories carry different point counts, different ages, and one story swapped out for a new one between fetches. `tailwindcss.com`'s webengine-panel diff is confirmed NOT a `columns` reach: grepped its real homepage HTML directly for `columns-` (the Tailwind utility class this round's fix could plausibly touch) and found zero uses anywhere on the page, despite the utility being defined in its shipped CSS bundle — the already-documented "declared, unreachable" pattern, not this round's own effect. `github.com/golang/go` and `caniuse.com` moved by less than 0.001 either way, ordinary noise. `en.wikipedia.org`'s own FULL page grew by ~3,900px between fetches (live article editing) but its own compared region (capped well short of that growth) is byte-identical, so its SSIM stayed exactly unchanged too. The real fix is confirmed instead by the direct, targeted re-render in the log above: pkg.go.dev's own "Source Files" list, around y≈75,000px on a ~77,000px page, sits nowhere near any bench comparison region ever capped to a page's own top ~2,500px — the same "real, confirmed, bench-invisible" pattern already established for many earlier rounds' below-the-fold fixes.

## 2026-09-28 (round 143) — `word-break`/`overflow-wrap` were entirely unimplemented, so an unbreakable long token always overflowed its container instead of wrapping — CONFIRMED real, independent usage on THREE corpus pages, one of them (developer.mozilla.org) setting it on `html` itself (engine#243)

The user chose a new method for this round: rather than sweeping a corpus for JS API usage or auditing this project's own "Known gaps" for staleness (round 142's method, now exhausted), a systematic CSS-property audit — checking this engine's actual property support against a structured reference. Delegated the bulk of the cross-referencing to a background agent, which reported two candidates surviving verification against real corpus CSS: `columns`/`column-count` (a genuinely new multi-column layout mode, only one confirmed trigger — pkg.go.dev's own file list — and comparable in scope to this session's other deliberately-deferred larger features, so left as a documented future gap rather than attempted this round) and `word-break`/`overflow-wrap` (three independent triggers, no new layout algorithm needed — implemented this round).

- **Independently re-verified every claim before acting**: confirmed via `grep` that neither property nor any backing `Style` field existed anywhere in `css`/`layout`/`paint`; re-fetched all three cited pages' live CSS directly (not trusting the agent's quotes) — pkg.go.dev/net/http's `.UnitFiles-fileList{columns:12.5rem 5;...word-break:break-all}` and `.UnitDirectories-pathCell{...word-break:break-all}`/`.UnitDirectories td{...word-break:break-word}`, news.ycombinator.com's `.title a{word-break:break-word}` (every story headline link), and developer.mozilla.org's own `html{...overflow-wrap:break-word;...}` (inherited page-wide, confirmed via its actual served `styles-global.css`).
- **Scoped to exactly the one evidenced effect, not the full spec surface**: both properties, and `word-break:break-word`'s deprecated alias, now OR into one `css.Style.BreaksOverlongWords()` check (`WordBreakAll`/`OverflowWrapAnywhere` are separate fields, not collapsed into one at parse time, so an unrelated `word-break:normal` on one declaration can never silently cancel an independent `overflow-wrap:break-word` from another — a real cascade-correctness bug the obvious single-field design would have had). Only the one visible effect every confirmed trigger actually needs is modelled: a single unbreakable "word" wider than its own line splits at a rune boundary instead of overflowing. NOT modelled, disclosed: `break-all`'s stronger spec behaviour of breaking eagerly even when a word already fits elsewhere (no evidenced trigger, and a much larger blast radius on ordinary greedy-wrap line counts); `overflow-wrap:anywhere`'s distinct intrinsic-sizing effect; `word-break:keep-all` (this engine has no CJK line-breaking to suppress in the first place); and splitting WITHIN a glued run (`152,3<sup>†</sup>`-shaped, engine#149) rather than a single lone word — no confirmed trigger for that combination either.
- **Fixed** (`css/value.go`/`css/parse.go`: new inherited `WordBreakAll`/`OverflowWrapAnywhere` fields, wired through `inheritFrom` and the explicit-`inherit` handler like every other inherited property; `layout/layout.go`'s new `splitOverlongWord`, tried in `layoutInline`'s main loop right before `forceOne`'s own unconditional overflow, on exactly the same leading item `forceOne` would otherwise place whole).
- **A test bug caught along the way, not shipped**: an initial `TestWordBreakAllDoesNotSplitAGluedRun` wrongly assumed a glued run (`152,3<sup>†</sup>`-shaped) that must overflow stays together on one line — it does not: `forceOne` places only the run's FIRST item (it has never called `glueRun` at all), splitting the run at its own glue seam, a genuine PRE-EXISTING inconsistency with `glueRun`'s own documented invariant. Confirmed unrelated to this round (the identical split reproduces with no word-break/overflow-wrap property set anywhere) and NOT fixed here — no confirmed real-world page trigger for an unbreakable glued run wider than its own container was found; the test now pins the actual current behaviour instead of asserting something false.
- **Two new test files' worth of coverage**: `css/parse_test.go` (`TestApplyWordBreak`/`TestApplyOverflowWrap`/`TestBreaksOverlongWords` — every value, the deprecated alias, `unset`, the explicit `inherit` keyword, and `inheritFrom`) and `layout/layout_test.go` (six tests using the existing `fakeMeasurer`'s exact 10px-per-rune convention: the unaffected default, both properties independently reaching the same split, no effect on already-fitting text, inheritance to a descendant, the single-rune-cannot-split edge, and the glued-run non-split above). Git-stash-confirmed: reverting `css/parse.go`/`css/value.go`/`layout/layout.go` alone (keeping the new tests) fails to even COMPILE (`s.WordBreakAll undefined`) — the strongest class of regression signal.
- **Simplified `splitOverlongWord` after the coverage floor caught unreachable defensive code**: an initial version defensively skipped a leading `LineBreak` item and guarded an empty `items` slice, mirroring `forceOne`'s own symmetric checks — but `layoutInline` only ever calls it once `wrapOneLine` has already confirmed `items[0]` itself is a non-break item that does not fit, making both checks provably dead code. `layout`'s 100% coverage floor caught this immediately (87% on the new function); removed rather than tested around, per this session's own standing rule against validating conditions that cannot occur.
- **Bench confirmed a real, directly-attributable win on the ONE page whose own trigger applies site-wide**: developer.mozilla.org's own SSIM rose 0.6028→0.6369 (pixdiff 18.10%→13.60%) — this round's single largest movement, and exactly the page whose `overflow-wrap:break-word` sits on `html` itself, reaching every long unbreakable token anywhere in its prose and code samples, not just one narrow widget. pkg.go.dev stayed exactly flat (0.71557, unchanged) — checked directly rather than assumed: a live re-render confirmed its own Source Files list has no filename long enough to need splitting on this specific page, and its `.UnitFiles-fileList`'s OTHER property, `columns:12.5rem 5`, is the still-unimplemented, separate gap actually keeping that list single-column (documented above, not this round's fix). news.ycombinator.com's small -0.0023 and react.dev's -0.0019 are both within the same live-content noise band already established across many rounds for these two frequently-changing pages (neither's own current front-page/homepage content combines a long-enough bare token with a narrow-enough container to exercise this fix either way). github.com/golang/go (-0.00012), tailwindcss.com (+0.0008) and caniuse.com (+0.0030) are ordinary rounding/live-fetch noise; example.com, en.wikipedia.org and go.dev/blog byte-identical.

## 2026-09-28 (round 141) — go.dev/blog's own nav dropdown carets rendered nothing at all, not the literal text this engine's own "Known gaps" entry said they would — a stale gap description, checked against the current code rather than trusted (engine#241)

Following the user's own "switch approach entirely" instruction after round 140, moved off corpus-sweeping and checked this engine's own FIDELITY.md "Known gaps" section against the CURRENT code instead — the method rounds 71 and 82 already used successfully for stale-premise gaps. The `@font-face`/Material Icons entry (round 20, last touched 2026-09-03) claimed a fix would be "genuinely comparable in scope to Shadow DOM," needing both `@font-face` fetching (which did not exist yet when written) and a whole new OpenType-shaping package. Both premises turned out to be stale: `@font-face` fetching/WOFF2 decoding shipped separately three rounds ago (engine#233/#234/#236), and re-reading `go-opentype/opentype`'s current source (rather than trusting the gap entry's own description of it) found `Face` had gained `Shape`/`ShapePositioned`/`GlyphMaskIndex`/`AdvanceIndex` methods directly — no separate `go-opentype/shape` package needed at all.

- **Reproduced the ACTUAL current behaviour before trusting the old description**: rendering an isolated `<span class="material-icons">arrow_drop_down</span>` repro against the real Google Fonts Material Icons WOFF2 showed the icon area as completely BLANK — not the literal readable text the 2026-09-03 entry described. Root cause: `@font-face` now loads the real icon font (shipped separately), and its cmap maps every ASCII letter of "arrow_drop_down" to its own real, non-`.notdef` (so not skipped) but visually blank glyph — a deliberate authoring convention so a renderer that skips the required substitution shows nothing rather than mangled letter shapes, worse than the old (pre-`@font-face`) fallback-font behaviour the gap entry described.
- **Found the actual OpenType feature empirically rather than assuming "liga"**: `Face.Shape("arrow_drop_down", "liga")` — the obvious first guess, and what a real browser applies by default for cosmetic ligatures — left all 15 glyphs unmerged. Tried the font's declared GSUB features one at a time (`dlig`, `hlig`, `calt`, `rlig`, `ccmp`, `aalt`, `rclt`); only `"rlig"` (Required Ligatures) collapsed the word to its one pictogram glyph. Confirmed this is the OpenType-spec-intended feature, not a special case: the registry's own text for `rlig` says "Control of this feature should not generally be exposed to the user" — it is meant to always apply, unlike `liga`, which a real browser turns off under non-zero `letter-spacing` (confirmed via MDN's own `letter-spacing` page). Verified the four bundled prose fonts (Inter, Lora, Go Mono, DejaVu Sans) declare no `rlig` lookups at all (a 46-character mixed sample shaped to the same 46 glyphs under `rlig` for all four), so applying it unconditionally is isolated to fonts that actually declare required ligatures — zero risk to this engine's existing bundled-font text.
- **Fixed** (`paint/fonts.go`'s `Measure`, `paint/paint.go`'s `drawText`): both now call `Face.Shape(run.Text, "rlig")` per run (after `Runs` has already split text by cmap coverage, so a ligature never spans a fallback-font boundary) and iterate the shaped glyph indices — `AdvanceIndexUnits` for measurement, `GlyphMaskIndex` for painting — instead of the old per-rune `font.GlyphIndex`/`GlyphMask` loop, which could never produce a merged glyph at all. A `gid == 0` guard skips glyphs `Shape` maps an uncovered rune to (`.notdef`), preserving the existing "a character neither face covers measures and draws nothing" contract `paint`'s own `TestMeasureIsExactAndLinear` already asserted — caught by that pre-existing test failing on the first version of this change, not written in anticipation of it.
- **Deliberately NOT implemented this round** (see the corrected "Known gaps" entry): `liga` (optional/cosmetic ligatures) and its `letter-spacing` interaction — `Measure`'s signature has no letter-spacing parameter, and threading one through its thirteen call sites for a feature this engine does not otherwise model was judged out of scope for this round; and GPOS-based positioning (pair kerning, mark-to-base attachment) — `ShapePositioned` already exists in the dependency but nothing calls it, with no confirmed real-world trigger found yet.
- **Three new tests** in `paint/ligature_test.go`, using the real Material Icons WOFF2 (Google, Apache License 2.0 — `paint/testdata/materialicons-LICENSE.txt`) as a fixture, matching the existing `testdata/IBMPlexSans-Regular-subset.woff2` precedent for real-WOFF2 tests: `Measure("arrow_drop_down")` now equals exactly one glyph's advance instead of fifteen; `drawText` now paints visible ink for it (git-stash-confirmed against the pre-fix code: both failed as expected — `Measure` returned the unshaped 720, `drawText` painted nothing); and the `gid == 0` skip is covered directly by drawing an uncovered CJK rune and confirming both the pen advance and the painted ink stay at zero.
- **Bench**: all ten pages within the same small run-to-run noise band already established for this live, network-fetched comparison across many prior rounds (largest move tailwindcss.com's own +0.013 SSIM; go.dev/blog itself barely moved, 0.6902→0.6889 SSIM, 16.73%→16.78% pixdiff) — expected, since the fix's real effect (a few dozen pixels of one icon glyph) is far too small a share of a ~1.5M-pixel page to register above this benchmark's already-documented noise floor. The fix is confirmed instead by direct visual inspection: both the isolated repro and a live re-render of go.dev/blog itself now show a real triangle glyph where the nav previously rendered nothing. All coverage floors held (`paint` back to its 100.0% floor, requiring the `gid == 0` test above).

## 2026-09-28 (round 140) — `HTMLMediaElement.currentTime`/`.play()`/`.pause()`/`.paused` were entirely missing — CONFIRMED real usage on a THIRD, freshly-fetched corpus page (archive.org's own media-player component) after both prior corpora were exhausted (engine#240)

Following the same "sweep a fresh real bundle" method rounds 138/139 used on github.com, fetched a genuinely different KIND of page (archive.org's item-detail viewer, a real `<video>`/`<audio>` player) after the github.com corpus itself stopped turning up anything new. Its `details-av.js` bundle (1MB, real production code) is saturated with media-API-shaped identifiers — `.currentTime`, `.duration`, `.volume`, `.muted`, `.paused`, `.play(`, `.pause(` — and this engine had NOTHING for `<video>`/`<audio>` at all: no entry anywhere in `isReplacedTag` or any binder file.

- **Read the grep hits skeptically before trusting them — most were noise, not evidence**: `.duration=` turned out to be almost entirely jQuery's own `$.fx.speeds` animation-duration internals; `.paused`/`.pause()` collided heavily with Bootstrap's carousel widget (`this.paused`, `t.prototype.cycle`), which has nothing to do with media at all; `.volume`/`.muted` hits were an ambiguous `syncMute` helper with unclear operands. Tracing each hit's actual surrounding code (not just the bare grep match) found exactly ONE unambiguous real trigger: `transcriptEntrySelected(e)` looks up the real element via `shadowRoot.querySelector("video")` and does `videoEl.currentTime = this.currentTime; videoEl.play()` — clicking a transcript entry seeks the video and resumes playback.
- **Scoped to exactly that confirmed trigger, nothing more**: implemented `currentTime` (read/write), `play()`/`pause()` (methods), and `paused` (the natural, unavoidable companion state `play()`/`pause()` need to be internally consistent — checking it after calling either would otherwise read `undefined`). Deliberately did NOT implement `duration`/`volume`/`muted`/`readyState`/`playbackRate` — no confirmed real trigger for any of them survived closer inspection, per this session's own anti-speculative-API discipline.
- **Fixed** (`js/js.go`/`session.go`: new `binder.mediaTime`/`mediaPaused` per-node maps, the same "storage without real playback/rendering effect" shape as round 137's `scrollPos`; `js/dom.go`): `play()` returns a real (immediately-resolved) `Promise`, matching the spec's own return type; a media element starts `paused` per spec default.
- **One new test**, git-stash-confirmed: reverting turns `currentTime`/`paused` into `undefined` and makes `.play()` throw `TypeError: Object has no member 'play'`.
- **Bench**: exactly flat on pkg.go.dev (0.716, unchanged) as expected, and within already-documented noise elsewhere — no visual effect by design. caniuse.com showed unusually slow fetch times across three separate runs (webengine 8-22s vs. its own typical ~4-5s) — investigated rather than assumed: an isolated single-page re-run showed real headless CHROME taking even longer (20.6s) on the same fetch, confirming this is a real but purely environmental/network condition (not a code regression); SSIM stayed within the same established 0.657-0.667 band throughout regardless of fetch time. All coverage floors held.

## 2026-09-28 (round 139) — the Constraint Validation API (`setCustomValidity`/`.validity`/`.validationMessage`/`checkValidity`) was entirely missing — CONFIRMED extensive real usage on github.com's own client-side form-validation UI (username/label/2FA fields) (engine#239)

Continued sweeping the same fresh github.com bundles round 138 found `.indeterminate` in, this time for method calls rather than property assignments. `.checkValidity(` (7 hits) and `.setCustomValidity(` (4 hits) stood out immediately, alongside `.validity.customError`/`.validationMessage` reads — GitHub's own form-validation UI relies on this API extensively for username/label/2FA-code field checks.

- **Scoped precisely to what's evidenced, not the full spec surface**: this engine models NO native constraints at all — no `required`/`pattern`/`min`/`max`/`step` checking against a value. The corpus usage is exclusively the CUSTOM-validity pattern (`setCustomValidity(msg)` / `setCustomValidity("")` / reading `.validity.customError`/`.validationMessage` / gating on `.checkValidity()`), so that's what's implemented — but the `ValidityState` object's SHAPE is spec-complete (all nine other flags — `valueMissing`, `typeMismatch`, `patternMismatch`, `tooLong`, `tooShort`, `rangeUnderflow`, `rangeOverflow`, `stepMismatch`, `badInput` — are present and always `false`, not silently `undefined`), so a script that checks any of them gets a real, defined answer rather than a crash.
- **Fixed** (`dom.Node.CustomValidity string`, the same non-attribute-runtime-field shape as round 138's `Indeterminate`; `js/dom.go`): `setCustomValidity(msg)` sets it; `.validationMessage` and `.validity.customError`/`.valid` read it; `checkValidity()` returns `validity.valid` and, per spec, fires a cancelable `"invalid"` event at the element when invalid.
- **`.willValidate` deliberately left out**: zero corpus usage found for it anywhere in this session's fetched scripts, unlike the four members above.
- **One new test**, git-stash-confirmed: reverting makes the test's own script throw `TypeError: Cannot read property 'valid' of undefined` — the same class of failure rounds 124-126 documented for a missing method.
- **Bench**: flat/within already-documented noise across all ten pages, including github.com/golang/go itself (0.638, unchanged) — expected, pure JS-correctness fix with no paint-visible effect by design. All coverage floors held.

## 2026-09-28 (round 138) — `HTMLInputElement.indeterminate` and the `:indeterminate` CSS pseudo-class were entirely missing — CONFIRMED real corpus usage on github.com's own "select all" bulk-action checkboxes (engine#238)

Broadened the corpus sweep to a genuinely fresh page (github.com's own homepage and its `behaviors.js`/`element-registry.js`/`environment.js` bundles, not previously in this session's ten-page set), after the existing corpus stopped turning up new evidenced gaps. `.indeterminate=` was the one new real hit: `(0,v.l)("[data-indeterminate]",{constructor:HTMLInputElement,initialize(e){e.indeterminate=!0}})` — GitHub's own tri-state "select all" checkbox pattern.

- **Read the bibliography first, not assumed symmetric with `checked`**: HTML Standard §4.10.5.1.19 — `indeterminate` has NO backing content attribute at all, unlike `checked`. It is pure script-set runtime state with no HTML markup equivalent.
- **Fixed across three layers, matching that architectural difference**: a new `dom.Node.Indeterminate bool` field (the same shape as the existing `Quirks`/`Shadow` runtime-only fields, not `Attr`) backs both a new `js/dom.go` `.indeterminate` accessor (reads/writes the field directly, NOT via `setAttr`/`removeAttr`) and a new `:indeterminate` CSS pseudo-class in `css/selector.go` (`compound.Indeterminate`, wired into `Matches`/the parser switch/the specificity count/the bare-compound-is-non-empty check — the same four sites `:disabled` already uses).
- **Deliberately left out of scope, disclosed honestly**: this engine's own checkbox painter (`paint.paintCheckboxLike`) already approximates `checked` as a plain accent-coloured fill with no real checkmark glyph — adding a visually distinct indeterminate mark (a real browser's usual dash) would mean inventing new iconography beyond what `checked` itself has, not extending an existing one. This round only makes `.indeterminate`/`:indeterminate` correct for script logic and AUTHOR CSS (`input:indeterminate { ... }` rules), not for this engine's own default checkbox appearance.
- **Two new tests**, git-stash-confirmed (a `go vet` compile failure, the strongest class): `css/selector_test.go`'s `TestIndeterminatePseudo` (mirroring `TestCheckedPseudo`/`TestDisabledEnabledPseudo`'s exact structure) and `js/js_test.go`'s `TestIndeterminateAccessor` (confirms setting it does NOT touch `getAttribute('indeterminate')`, unlike every other boolean accessor on this element).
- **Bench**: exactly flat/within already-documented noise across all ten pages, including github.com/golang/go itself (0.638, unchanged) — expected, since this round's fix is a pure JS/CSS-correctness change with no paint-visible effect by design (see the scoping note above). All coverage floors held.

## 2026-09-27 (round 137) — `scrollTop`/`scrollLeft` were hardcoded to always read 0 with no setter at all, so a script-set value silently did nothing — CONFIRMED real corpus usage on pkg.go.dev's own scroll-into-view logic, which both sets AND reads the value back to decide what to do next (engine#237)

Continued sweeping this session's own two hottest bundles for `.propertyName =` assignments across the FULL corpus this time (not just the two hottest files), broadened past boolean properties to numeric/string ones too. `.scrollTop=` stood out on pkg.go.dev's own `frontend.js`, in three separate call sites of a "keep the active item visible in a scrollable list" routine: `f<e.scrollTop?e.scrollTop=f:p>e.scrollTop+e.clientHeight&&(e.scrollTop=p-e.c…)`.

- **Scoped honestly, not oversold**: this engine has no real scroll/clip model at all — a single static layout pass, no overflow viewport that could actually be scrolled within — so this fix does NOT make pkg.go.dev's own scroll-into-view widget visually functional; disclosed plainly in the code comment and here. What it fixes is narrower but still real: a script that SETS `scrollTop` then immediately (or later) READS it back to decide what to do next was comparing against a value hardcoded to a permanent, unchangeable 0 — the exact shape of bug this session has repeatedly found elsewhere this round-130+ series (a property that looks wired but silently never holds what was written to it), here risking a stuck branch or wrong decision in real widget code rather than a rendering difference.
- **Fixed** (`js/dom.go`, `js/js.go`, `js/session.go`): a new `binder.scrollPos map[*dom.Node][2]float64` stores each element's own last-set scrollTop/scrollLeft; the two accessors now read/write it instead of a hardcoded `return 0`/`nil` setter pair.
- **One new test**, git-stash-confirmed: reverting leaves a script-set `scrollTop`/`scrollLeft` reading back `0` regardless of what was assigned.
- **Bench**: exactly flat on pkg.go.dev (0.716, unchanged) as expected, and within already-documented noise bands elsewhere — no visual effect is possible or claimed, by design (see the scoping note above). caniuse.com hit a repeated `dial tcp: i/o timeout` to its own resolved IP across two runs (confirmed live, direct `curl`/`dig` to the same host and IP succeeded instantly in between — an infra-level flake unrelated to this JS-only change, not a regression); a third run came back clean. All coverage floors held.
